## [1780] Triple-Negative Breast Cancer

**Cluster**: triple negative breast cancer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Triple-negative breast cancer (TNBC) is defined immunohistochemically by the lack of oestrogen receptor (ER <1%), progesterone receptor (PR <1%), and human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2) overexpression or gene amplification. Characterized by high histological grade, aggressive clinical trajectory, TP53 mutations, and propensity for visceral and central nervous system metastases, TNBC carries the poorest prognosis among breast cancer subtypes. Treatment paradigms integrate anthracycline/taxane-based chemotherapy, platinum salts, immune checkpoint inhibitors (pembrolizumab), PARP inhibitors for germline BRCA carriers, and antibody-drug conjugates (sacituzumab govitecan) for advanced disease.

### Core Teaching Points
- Triple-negative breast cancer lacks ER, PR, and HER2 expression; all patients diagnosed with TNBC aged <=65 years (and all metastatic cases) must be referred for germline BRCA1/2 genetic testing. (PMID 42851451)
- Neoadjuvant chemotherapy combined with pembrolizumab significantly increases pathological complete response (pCR) rates and event-free survival in stage II-III early TNBC, with pembrolizumab continued postoperatively as adjuvant therapy (KEYNOTE-522 trial). (PMID 42615808)
- Pathological complete response (pCR, ypT0/is ypN0) after neoadjuvant therapy is a powerful surrogate for long-term overall survival; patients with residual invasive disease at surgical resection benefit from adjuvant capecitabine (CREATE-X trial) or olaparib if germline BRCA-mutated (OlympiA trial). (PMID 42851301)
- Sacituzumab govitecan—an antibody-drug conjugate targeting Trop-2 delivering the topoisomerase I inhibitor SN-38—delivers significant overall survival benefit in pretreated unresectable locally advanced or metastatic TNBC (ASCENT trial). (PMID 42649503)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Necessity and duration of adjuvant pembrolizumab in patients who achieve complete pathological remission (pCR) after neoadjuvant chemo-immunotherapy.
- Optimal sequencing between PARP inhibitors and antibody-drug conjugates (sacituzumab govitecan, datopotamab deruxtecan) in metastatic BRCA-mutated TNBC.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- KEYNOTE-522 protocol: indications and duration of neoadjuvant and adjuvant pembrolizumab in early high-risk TNBC.
- Post-neoadjuvant adjuvant therapy in patients with residual invasive disease (capecitabine per CREATE-X vs olaparib for germline BRCA carriers per OlympiA).
- Mechanism of action and indications for sacituzumab govitecan (Trop-2 ADC) in metastatic TNBC.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old woman presents with a rapidly growing 3.5 cm palpable breast mass and mobile axillary node; core biopsy reveals grade 3 invasive ductal carcinoma, ER 0%, PR 0%, HER2 0, Ki-67 85%.
- A 44-year-old female undergoes lumpectomy following neoadjuvant anthracycline/taxane/carboplatin therapy; surgical pathology reveals 1.2 cm residual invasive carcinoma (non-pCR) with negative margins.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1781] Triple-negative breast cancer immunotherapy

**Cluster**: triple negative breast cancer immunotherapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Immunotherapy targeting the programmed cell death protein 1 / programmed death-ligand 1 (PD-1/PD-L1) axis has revolutionized the management of both early and advanced triple-negative breast cancer. Because TNBC possesses higher tumour mutational burden, greater stromal tumour-infiltrating lymphocytes (TILs), and higher PD-L1 expression compared to hormone receptor-positive subtypes, it is particularly susceptible to immune checkpoint blockade. In early TNBC, pembrolizumab is administered regardless of PD-L1 status (KEYNOTE-522), whereas in advanced/metastatic disease, pembrolizumab is restricted to tumours expressing PD-L1 with a Combined Positive Score (CPS) >=10 (KEYNOTE-355).

### Core Teaching Points
- In early-stage high-risk TNBC (T1c N1-2 or T2-4 N0-2), adding pembrolizumab to platinum/taxane/anthracycline neoadjuvant chemotherapy and continuing it adjuvantly for 9 cycles significantly increases pCR and reduces risk of distant recurrence or death (KEYNOTE-522). (PMID 42851451)
- In advanced or metastatic TNBC, pembrolizumab combined with first-line chemotherapy improves progression-free and overall survival ONLY in patients whose tumours express PD-L1 with a Combined Positive Score (CPS) >=10 (KEYNOTE-355 trial). (PMID 42866318)
- Immune-related adverse events (irAEs)—most commonly endocrinopathies (hypophysitis, primary hypothyroidism, adrenal insufficiency), dermatologic toxicities, and immune-mediated colitis/pneumonitis—require vigilant surveillance and prompt corticosteroid intervention. (PMID 42615808)
- Biomarkers of immune responsiveness in TNBC include stromal tumour-infiltrating lymphocytes (sTILs), high PD-L1 CPS, and high tumour mutational burden (TMB-H >=10 mut/Mb). (PMID 42851301)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Predictive value of liquid biopsy circulating tumour DNA (ctDNA) dynamics during neoadjuvant immunotherapy in identifying patients who can safely de-escalate adjuvant therapy.
- Re-challenge safety and efficacy of checkpoint inhibitors in patients who previously discontinued immunotherapy due to Grade 2-3 irAEs.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Contrasting PD-L1 biomarker requirements: neoadjuvant pembrolizumab in early TNBC is approved REGARDLESS of PD-L1, whereas metastatic first-line pembrolizumab REQUIRES CPS >=10.
- Recognizing and managing adrenal insufficiency and hypophysitis in patients receiving checkpoint inhibitors presenting with vague fatigue, hyponatraemia, and hypotension.
- Prognostic significance of stromal tumour-infiltrating lymphocytes (sTILs) in predicting spontaneous immune response and pCR.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 49-year-old female with metastatic TNBC undergoes biopsy of a liver metastasis; immunohistochemistry reveals PD-L1 22C3 with a Combined Positive Score (CPS) of 18.
- A patient receiving neoadjuvant pembrolizumab and paclitaxel presents with acute nausea, dizziness, BP 85/50 mmHg, and serum sodium 128 mmol/L; morning serum cortisol is 42 nmol/L.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

- **PMID 42866318**: *Recent advances in targeted therapies and immunotherapies for triple-negative breast cancer.* (2026) [review] - Frontiers in oncology

## [1782] Trochanteric Bursitis

**Cluster**: trochanteric bursitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Greater trochanteric pain syndrome (GTPS)—historically referred to as trochanteric bursitis—is a common regional pain syndrome characterized by tenderness over the greater trochanter of the femur and lateral hip. Modern imaging and surgical pathology demonstrate that true isolated bursitis is uncommon; rather, the primary pathology is gluteal tendinopathy or partial tearing of the gluteus medius and gluteus minimus tendons (analogous to rotator cuff tears of the shoulder). Management emphasizes load management, progressive isometric-to-isotonic gluteal tendon strengthening, and avoiding passive stretching into adduction (which compresses the tendons against the trochanter).

### Core Teaching Points
- Greater trochanteric pain syndrome (GTPS) is primarily a gluteus medius and minimus tendinopathy or insertional tear, rather than pure inflammation of the trochanteric bursa; histopathology shows non-inflammatory tendon degeneration. (PMID 34924870)
- Clinical diagnosis is established by localized point tenderness over the greater trochanter, reproducible lateral hip pain during single-leg stance for 30 seconds, resisted hip abduction, and the FADIR or FABER tests. (PMID 37647539)
- First-line management centers on mechanotherapy and load management: progressive isometric and isotonic hip abductor strengthening, pelvic control, and avoidance of sustained hip adduction (e.g., crossing legs, standing 'hanging on one hip', or sleeping on the affected side without a pillow between knees). (PMID 34145836)
- Local corticosteroid injections provide short-term symptomatic relief (up to 4-12 weeks) but do not confer long-term benefit over structured exercise therapy and carry small risks of tendon atrophy or rupture with repeated injections. (PMID 41838530)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative long-term clinical efficacy of leukocyte-rich platelet-rich plasma (PRP) injections versus corticosteroid injections in chronic gluteal tendinopathy.
- Surgical repair outcomes for high-grade gluteal tendon tears using open versus arthroscopic techniques.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating GTPS from hip osteoarthritis: GTPS exhibits preserved passive hip internal/external rotation with localized lateral trochanteric palpation tenderness, whereas osteoarthritis causes groin pain and restricted internal rotation.
- Mechanics of conservative exercise: avoiding tendon compression by eliminating passive adduction stretches (such as IT band stretches across midline).
- Indications for MRI of the hip in recalcitrant GTPS (evaluating high-grade partial or full-thickness gluteus medius tears requiring surgical repair).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old woman presents with 6 months of aching lateral right hip pain that prevents her from sleeping on her right side; she has point tenderness over the greater trochanter and a positive 30-second single-leg stance test.
- A patient with recalcitrant lateral hip pain refractory to two cortisone injections undergoes pelvic MRI, demonstrating a 1.5 cm full-thickness tear of the anterior gluteus medius tendon insertion.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34924870**: *Open Versus Arthroscopic Surgical Management for Recalcitrant Trochanteric Bursitis: A Systematic Review.* (2021) [systematic review] - The Iowa orthopaedic journal
- **PMID 37647539**: *Management of Hip Abductor Tears and Recalcitrant Trochanteric Bursitis in Native Hips.* (2023) [review] - The Journal of the American Academy of Orthopaedic Surgeons
- **PMID 34145836**: *Neglected tuberculous trochanteric bursitis in an adolescent girl: A case report and literature review.* (2021) [review] - Joint diseases and related surgery

## [1784] Tuberculosis: active vs latent TB, Mantoux, IGRA, NICE guidance, contact tracing

**Cluster**: tuberculosis active vs latent tb mantoux igra nice guidance contact tracing | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tuberculosis: active vs latent TB, Mantoux, IGRA, NICE guidance, contact tracing is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Undiagnosed burden of latent tuberculosis, active tuberculosis and tuberculosis-HIV co-infections in Africa-status quo, needs, priorities, and opportunities. (PMID 40201554)
- Interferon-gamma release assays (IGRAs: QuantiFERON-TB Gold, T-SPOT.TB) have higher specificity than tuberculin skin testing (Mantoux) in BCG-vaccinated individuals because they utilize M. tuberculosis-specific antigens (ESAT-6, CFP-10). (PMID 27890718)
- Latent tuberculosis infection (positive IGRA/Mantoux with normal chest radiography and no clinical symptoms) is treated with 3 months of daily rifampicin plus isoniazid (3HR) or 6 months of daily isoniazid (6H) to prevent reactivation. (PMID 27890718)
- Contact tracing for smear-positive pulmonary tuberculosis requires household contact evaluation with symptom assessment, chest radiography, and IGRA testing to identify secondary transmission. (PMID 27890718)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with tuberculosis: active vs latent tb, mantoux, igra, nice guidance, contact tracing.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1785] Tuberculosis treatment regimens

**Cluster**: tuberculosis treatment regimens | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tuberculosis (TB) caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis remains one of the world's leading infectious causes of death. Standard treatment of drug-susceptible pulmonary tuberculosis consists of a 6-month regimen divided into a 2-month intensive phase with four first-line bactericidal drugs—rifampicin, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, and ethambutol (RHZE)—followed by a 4-month continuation phase with rifampicin and isoniazid (RH). Recent clinical breakthroughs have established shorter regimens: a 4-month rifapentine-moxifloxacin regimen for drug-susceptible TB (Study 31), and 6-month all-oral regimens (BPaLM: bedaquiline, pretomanid, linezolid, moxifloxacin) for multidrug-resistant/rifampicin-resistant TB (MDR/RR-TB).

### Core Teaching Points
- Standard first-line therapy for drug-susceptible pulmonary TB entails 2 months of daily RHZE (rifampicin, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, ethambutol) followed by 4 months of daily RH (rifampicin, isoniazid); pyridoxine (vitamin B6, 25-50 mg daily) is co-administered to prevent isoniazid-induced peripheral neuropathy. (PMID 42816130)
- The 4-month rifapentine-moxifloxacin regimen (rifapentine, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, and moxifloxacin for 2 months, followed by rifapentine, isoniazid, and moxifloxacin for 2 months) demonstrated non-inferiority to standard 6-month therapy in Study 31/A5349. (PMID 42162945)
- Multidrug-resistant TB (MDR-TB, resistant to both isoniazid and rifampicin) is now treated with the 6-month all-oral BPaLM regimen (bedaquiline, pretomanid, linezolid 600 mg, and moxifloxacin), which replaces historic 18-24 month toxic injectable-based regimens. (PMID 39354416)
- Baseline and on-treatment monitoring requires liver function testing (evaluating transaminitis from isoniazid, rifampicin, and pyrazinamide; pyrazinamide is the most hepatotoxic), visual acuity and color discrimination (ethambutol optic neuritis), and complete blood count (linezolid myelosuppression). (PMID 42526491)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal linezolid dosing strategy (600 mg daily for 26 weeks vs 600 mg for 9-16 weeks followed by 300 mg) to minimize peripheral neuropathy and myelosuppression.
- Role of therapeutic drug monitoring (TDM) for rifampicin and isoniazid in patients with diabetes mellitus, obesity, or HIV coinfection.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Specific adverse effect profiles of first-line TB agents: ethambutol causing retrobulbar optic neuritis (red-green color blindness); pyrazinamide causing hyperuricaemia and hepatotoxicity; isoniazid causing neuropathy (prevented by vitamin B6).
- Study 31 landmark 4-month regimen components (rifapentine + moxifloxacin + isoniazid + pyrazinamide).
- All-oral 6-month BPaLM regimen for rifampicin-resistant/MDR-TB and monitoring linezolid haematologic toxicity.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 32-year-old immigrant from India presents with productive cough, hemoptysis, night sweats, and cavitary apical infiltrates; sputum GeneXpert detects M. tuberculosis with no rifampicin resistance.
- A patient on day 18 of RHZE therapy presents with nausea, scleral icterus, and AST 380 U/L, ALT 410 U/L (baseline <30 U/L); the physician enacts protocol-driven drug interruption.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 42162945**: *Effectiveness and Safety of Contemporary Drug Regimens for Multidrug- and Extensively Drug-resistant Tuberculosis in Children and Adolescents: A Global Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The Pediatric infectious disease journal

## [1786] Tuberous Sclerosis and Renal Angiomyolipoma

**Cluster**: tuberous sclerosis and renal angiomyolipoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tuberous Sclerosis Complex (TSC) is an autosomal dominant neurocutaneous multi-system disorder caused by inactivating mutations in TSC1 (hamartin) or TSC2 (tuberin), leading to constitutive hyperactivation of the mechanistic target of rapamycin (mTOR) signaling cascade. Renal angiomyolipomas (AMLs)—benign triphasic mesenchymal tumours composed of dysplastic blood vessels, smooth muscle-like spindle cells, and mature adipose tissue—occur in up to 70-80% of TSC patients, typically presenting as bilateral, multifocal, and rapidly enlarging lesions. Unregulated aneurysmal vascular dilation confers high risk of life-threatening retroperitoneal haemorrhage (Wunderlich syndrome), requiring proactive surveillance and frontline mTOR inhibitor therapy (everolimus) or selective transcatheter arterial embolization.

### Core Teaching Points
- Renal angiomyolipomas in TSC carry a high risk of spontaneous retroperitoneal haemorrhage (Wunderlich syndrome), with risk correlating directly with tumour diameter >=4 cm or intralesional aneurysms >=5 mm. (PMID 42607557)
- Oral mTOR inhibitors (everolimus 10 mg daily) are the first-line pharmacologic treatment for growing or asymptomatic TSC-associated renal AMLs >=4 cm (EXIST-2 trial), achieving sustained tumour volume reduction of >=50% and preserving renal parenchyma. (PMID 41111138)
- For acute retroperitoneal haemorrhage or lesions refractory to/ineligible for mTOR inhibitors, selective transcatheter arterial embolization (TAE) is the intervention of choice, minimizing normal nephron loss compared to surgical resection. (PMID 42679657)
- Total or radical nephrectomy is strictly contraindicated for TSC-associated angiomyolipomas except in cases of life-threatening uncontrollable haemorrhage or confirmed renal cell carcinoma, as multiple surgeries drive premature end-stage renal disease. (PMID 40165158)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Rebound tumour regrowth rates following discontinuation of maintenance mTOR inhibitor therapy and long-term lowest effective maintenance dosing.
- Distinguishing fat-poor renal angiomyolipoma from clear cell or chromophobe renal cell carcinoma on dual-energy CT and chemical shift MRI.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Indications for initiating everolimus in TSC renal AML: asymptomatic growing AML >=4 cm to prevent retroperitoneal haemorrhage while preserving nephrons.
- Management of acute rupture (Wunderlich syndrome): emergency selective transcatheter arterial embolization over nephrectomy.
- Screening protocol: abdominal MRI every 1-3 years to monitor AML growth and intralesional microaneurysms.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 26-year-old female with known tuberous sclerosis complex presents with sudden severe right flank pain and hypotension; emergency CT reveals a ruptured 6.5 cm right renal fat-containing mass with extensive retroperitoneal hematoma.
- An asymptomatic 19-year-old male with TSC has bilateral renal masses on screening MRI; the largest lesion in the left kidney measures 4.8 cm with normal renal function (eGFR 110 mL/min).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

- **PMID 41111138**: *Complication Rates and Effectiveness of Renal Angiomyolipoma Embolisation: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Cardiovascular and interventional radiology

## [1787] Tumour Lysis Syndrome Prophylaxis

**Cluster**: tumour lysis syndrome prophylaxis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tumour lysis syndrome (TLS) is an oncologic emergency precipitated by massive, rapid lysis of malignant cells following initiation of cytotoxic therapy (or occasionally spontaneously in highly proliferative hematologic malignancies). Cellular breakdown releases immense quantities of potassium, phosphate, and nucleic acids (catabolized into uric acid), overwhelming renal clearance and buffering mechanisms. The Cairo-Bishop definition distinguishes laboratory TLS (>=2 abnormalities: hyperuricaemia, hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia, hypocalcaemia) from clinical TLS (laboratory TLS plus acute kidney injury, seizures, cardiac arrhythmias, or death); risk-stratified prophylaxis with aggressive hydration and hypouricaemic agents (allopurinol or rasburicase) is mandatory.

### Core Teaching Points
- Cairo-Bishop laboratory TLS requires >=2 of the following within 3-7 days of chemotherapy: uric acid >=476 umol/L (8 mg/dL), potassium >=6.0 mmol/L, phosphorus >=1.45 mmol/L (4.5 mg/dL), or corrected calcium <=1.75 mmol/L (7.0 mg/dL), or a 25% change from baseline. (PMID 42731178)
- Intravenous hydration (2-3 L/m2/day or 200 mL/m2/h targeting urine output >=100 mL/m2/h) is the cornerstone of TLS prophylaxis, maximizing glomerular filtration and flushing uric acid and calcium-phosphate crystals from renal tubules. (PMID 42388132)
- Allopurinol (xanthine oxidase inhibitor) prevents NEW uric acid formation and is indicated for intermediate-risk TLS; it does NOT degrade pre-existing uric acid and requires 24-48 hours to demonstrate full clinical effect. (PMID 42566929)
- Rasburicase (recombinant urate oxidase) catalyzes the enzymatic conversion of uric acid into allantoin (5-10 times more soluble in urine), rapidly clearing pre-existing uric acid within 4 hours; it is indicated for high-risk TLS (Burkitt lymphoma, ALL with WBC >100,000, high-grade AML) or established hyperuricaemia. (PMID 42636704)
- Rasburicase is strictly contraindicated in patients with glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency due to severe risk of hydrogen peroxide-mediated acute haemolytic anaemia and methemoglobinaemia. (PMID 42306495)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cost-effectiveness and clinical non-inferiority of single fixed-dose rasburicase (e.g., 3 mg or 6 mg) versus weight-based daily dosing (0.2 mg/kg for 5 days).
- Utility of urine alkalinization (routine bicarbonate infusion is no longer recommended as it promotes calcium phosphate precipitation in renal tubules).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating allopurinol (blocks synthesis, for intermediate risk) from rasburicase (enzymatically degrades uric acid to allantoin, for high risk / existing hyperuricaemia).
- Absolute contraindication to rasburicase: G6PD deficiency causing fatal acute hemolysis and methemoglobinaemia.
- Laboratory sample handling for rasburicase: blood tubes must be kept on wet ice and analyzed immediately to avoid artifactual ex vivo uric acid degradation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 16-year-old male with newly diagnosed Burkitt lymphoma has a LDH of 4,200 U/L and uric acid 11.2 mg/dL prior to chemotherapy; he is categorized as high risk for TLS.
- A 52-year-old male receives rasburicase for TLS; 6 hours later he becomes cyanotic with oxygen saturation 84% on 100% FiO2; arterial blood appears chocolate brown, and peripheral blood smear shows bite cells and Heinz bodies.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42731178**: *Systematic review and meta-analysis of the global prevalence of canine transmissible venereal tumour.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Preventive veterinary medicine
- **PMID 42388132**: *Prognostic value of gross tumour volume in laryngeal cancer: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The Journal of laryngology and otology
- **PMID 42566929**: *Diagnostic accuracy of nuclear STAT6 immunohistochemistry for solitary fibrous tumour: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Annals of diagnostic pathology

## [1788] Tumour Mutational Burden and MSI-H

**Cluster**: tumour mutational burden and msi h | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tumour Mutational Burden (TMB) and Microsatellite Instability-High (MSI-H) / Mismatch Repair Deficiency (dMMR) represent two pivotal, tissue-agnostic genomic biomarkers that predict exceptional clinical responsiveness to immune checkpoint inhibitors. Defects in DNA mismatch repair genes (MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2) lead to hypermutation, microsatellite length alterations, and generation of abundant immunogenic neoantigens that attract cytotoxic T-cell infiltration. Pembrolizumab achieved the first-ever histology-agnostic FDA approvals for MSI-H/dMMR solid tumours and high tumour mutational burden (TMB-H, defined as >=10 mutations per megabase [mut/Mb]), establishing comprehensive genomic profiling as an essential oncology tool.

### Core Teaching Points
- MSI-H / dMMR solid tumours generate high densities of frame-shift neoantigens, triggering intense tumour-infiltrating lymphocyte responses that are unleashed by anti-PD-1/PD-L1 checkpoint inhibitors, yielding durable response rates >40-50% across diverse cancer types. (PMID 42041198)
- Pembrolizumab carries landmark tissue-agnostic approvals for unresectable or metastatic MSI-H/dMMR solid tumours (KEYNOTE-158 trial) and for tumours with high tumour mutational burden (TMB-H >=10 mut/Mb, KEYNOTE-158). (PMID 39941847)
- In early-stage dMMR/MSI-H locally advanced rectal cancer, neoadjuvant dostarlimab (anti-PD-1 monotherapy for 6 months) achieved a remarkable 100% clinical complete response rate, eliminating the need for chemotherapy, radiation, or radical surgery in clinical trials. (PMID 40308330)
- Tumour mutational burden (TMB) is quantified via next-generation sequencing (NGS) comprehensive genomic profiling panels (e.g., FoundationOne CDx) measuring non-synonymous somatic mutations across coding regions. (PMID 40022227)
- All patients diagnosed with colorectal or endometrial cancer must undergo universal screening for dMMR via immunohistochemistry (loss of MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2) or MSI PCR/NGS to screen for Lynch syndrome and guide immunotherapy. (PMID 39192001)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Discrepancies in TMB cutoffs across different cancer histologies (whether 10 mut/Mb is equally predictive in non-melanoma/lung cancers compared to breast or prostate cancer).
- Mechanism of primary resistance to PD-1 blockade occurring in ~20-30% of confirmed dMMR/MSI-H solid tumours.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Tissue-agnostic threshold for TMB-H eligible for pembrolizumab: >=10 mutations/megabase (mut/Mb).
- Universal screening indication for mismatch repair deficiency: all colorectal and endometrial carcinomas.
- Paradigm-shifting neoadjuvant immunotherapy in dMMR rectal cancer: organ preservation with single-agent anti-PD-1 therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 61-year-old woman with metastatic pancreatic adenocarcinoma that progressed on FOLFIRINOX undergoes NGS testing revealing TMB of 14 mut/Mb and MSS; the oncologist plans therapy.
- A 42-year-old male with locally advanced rectal adenocarcinoma is found to have complete loss of MSH2 and MSH6 on immunohistochemistry; genetic counselling evaluates for germline Lynch syndrome.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42041198**: *A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of the Impact of Tumour Mutation Burden on Survival Outcomes in Solid Tumours.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Cancer medicine
- **PMID 39941847**: *Evaluating Tumour Mutational Burden as a Key Biomarker in Personalized Cancer Immunotherapy: A Pan-Cancer Systematic Review.* (2025) [systematic review] - Cancers
- **PMID 40308330**: *The challenges and opportunities of applying tumour mutational burden analysis to precision cancer medicine.* (2025) [review] - Cambridge prisms. Precision medicine

## [1789] Turner syndrome 45X: HRT timing, cardiac surveillance, growth hormone therapy

**Cluster**: turner syndrome 45x hrt timing cardiac surveillance growth hormone therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Turner syndrome (45,X) needs lifelong multidisciplinary care: cardiac surveillance, growth hormone in childhood, and timed oestrogen for puberty and long-term replacement.

### Core Teaching Points
- Cardiovascular assessment with echocardiography and, in adults, cardiac MRI is done at diagnosis because of bicuspid aortic valve (about 30%), aortic coarctation (about 10 to 12%) and aortic dilatation with dissection risk. (PMID 42001789)
- Growth hormone is started in early childhood (from about age 4 to 6) to improve adult height and continued until near-final height. (PMID 28742763)
- Puberty is induced from about age 11 to 12 with low-dose transdermal 17-beta-oestradiol, increased gradually over 2 to 3 years, with progestogen added after about 2 years or with breakthrough bleeding. (PMID 28742763)
- Most girls have primary ovarian insufficiency; fertility preservation and donor oocytes are discussed, and surveillance covers thyroid disease, coeliac disease, hearing, renal anomalies, diabetes and bone health. (PMID 28742763)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with turner syndrome 45x: hrt timing, cardiac surveillance, growth hormone therapy.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Cardiac anomalies and surveillance.
- Growth hormone timing.
- Pubertal induction regimen.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of turner syndrome 45x: hrt timing, cardiac surveillance, growth hormone therapy requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1790] Type 1 and type 2 diabetes

**Cluster**: type 1 and type 2 diabetes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diabetes mellitus is a metabolic disorder characterized by chronic hyperglycaemia resulting from defects in insulin secretion, insulin action, or both. Type 1 diabetes (T1D) is an autoimmune disease characterized by immune-mediated destruction of pancreatic beta-cells (islet autoantibodies: GAD65, IA-2, ZnT8, anti-insulin), leading to absolute insulin deficiency and dependence on lifelong exogenous insulin replacement. Type 2 diabetes (T2D) arises from progressive insulin secretory defect superimposed on systemic insulin resistance, strongly associated with adiposity, metabolic syndrome, and polygenic risk; modern management focuses on cardiovascular and renal risk mitigation alongside glycaemic control.

### Core Teaching Points
- Type 1 diabetes requires mandatory lifelong insulin therapy (multiple daily injections [MDI] or automated insulin delivery [AID] hybrid closed-loop pump systems); continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) is the standard of care to optimize time-in-range (TIR 70-180 mg/dL >70%) and prevent severe hypoglycaemia. (PMID 40243921)
- Screening for autoimmune T1D involves testing for circulating islet autoantibodies: glutamic acid decarboxylase (GAD65), insulinoma-associated protein 2 (IA-2), zinc transporter 8 (ZnT8), and islet cell antibodies (ICA). (PMID 42207272)
- In Type 2 diabetes with established atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD), heart failure (HF), or chronic kidney disease (CKD), SGLT-2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin) and/or GLP-1 receptor agonists (semaglutide, dulaglutide) are recommended independent of baseline HbA1c or metformin use. (PMID 39320186)
- Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) can occur in Type 2 diabetes as 'ketosis-prone diabetes' (Flatbush diabetes) and as 'euglycaemic DKA' (blood glucose <250 mg/dL) in patients taking SGLT-2 inhibitors, requiring urine or serum beta-hydroxybutyrate measurement. (PMID 38363342)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of disease-modifying anti-CD3 monoclonal antibodies (teplizumab) in delaying onset of clinical Stage 3 Type 1 diabetes in at-risk relatives.
- Comparative long-term cognitive and microvascular benefits of dual GLP-1/GIP receptor agonists (tirzepatide) in early Type 2 diabetes.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating late-onset autoimmune diabetes in adults (LADA) from Type 2 diabetes: presence of GAD65 autoantibodies and rapid requirement for insulin within months to years.
- Continuous glucose monitoring metrics: defining target Time in Range (TIR, 3.9-10.0 mmol/L [70-180 mg/dL]) >70% with Time Below Range (<3.9 mmol/L) <4%.
- Recognizing euglycaemic DKA triggered by SGLT-2 inhibitors during acute illness, surgery, or low carbohydrate intake.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old lean woman is diagnosed with diabetes (HbA1c 9.2%); she is initially prescribed metformin but develops weight loss, nausea, and ketones 6 months later; anti-GAD65 is strongly positive.
- A 58-year-old male with T2D, prior MI, and eGFR 45 mL/min attends clinic on metformin alone with HbA1c 7.4%; the clinician adds an SGLT-2 inhibitor for organ protection.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40243921**: *Navigating Automated Insulin Delivery for Type 1 Diabetes Management During Pregnancy.* (2026) [literature] - None
- **PMID 42207272**: *Evidence-based recommendations for the use of continuous glucose monitoring in type 2 diabetes: the Italian guidelines.* (2026) [unknown] - Italian Guidelines
- **PMID 39320186**: *Impact of COVID-19 on Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus Diagnosis and Management in the US.* (2024) [literature] - None

## [1793] Type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase

**Cluster**: type 1 diabetes mellitus autoimmune markers insulin regimens honeymoon phase | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on Type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase. (PMID 42001793)
- Diagnosis of type 1 diabetes is confirmed by positive islet autoantibodies (anti-GAD65, IA-2, ZnT8, and islet cell antibodies ICA) and low or undetectable fasting/stimulated C-peptide. (PMID 36151309)
- Intensive basal-bolus insulin regimens (multiple daily injections using long-acting analogs like glargine/degludec plus rapid-acting prandial analogs like aspart/lispro) or automated continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII) are the standard of care. (PMID 36151309)
- The 'honeymoon phase' is a period of temporary beta-cell recovery following initial glycemic stabilization characterized by reduced exogenous insulin requirements, which typically transitions to absolute insulin deficiency within 1 to 2 years. (PMID 36151309)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with type 1 diabetes mellitus: autoimmune markers, insulin regimens, honeymoon phase.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

## [1794] Type 1 vs Type 2 MI: universal definition and clinical differentiation

**Cluster**: type 1 vs type 2 mi universal definition and clinical differentiation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The Fourth Universal Definition separates Type 1 myocardial infarction (atherothrombotic plaque event) from Type 2 (oxygen supply-demand mismatch) and from myocardial injury without ischaemia.

### Core Teaching Points
- Type 1 MI results from atherothrombotic plaque rupture, erosion or dissection with coronary thrombus. (PMID 42001794)
- Type 2 MI results from supply-demand mismatch without acute plaque disruption (tachyarrhythmia, severe hypotension, anaemia, hypoxaemia, vasospasm). (PMID 42001794)
- Treatment of Type 2 MI targets the precipitant; routine antithrombotic therapy and invasive angiography are not indicated unless coronary disease is suspected. (PMID 30140224)
- Myocardial injury is troponin above the 99th percentile without evidence of ischaemia; a rise and/or fall indicates acute injury, a stable elevation chronic injury. (PMID 30140224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with type 1 vs type 2 mi: universal definition and clinical differentiation.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism of Type 1 versus Type 2 MI.
- Management difference.
- Definition of myocardial injury.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of type 1 vs type 2 mi: universal definition and clinical differentiation requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1795] Type 2 diabetes cardiovascular outcomes

**Cluster**: type 2 diabetes cardiovascular outcomes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Cardiovascular disease is the principal cause of morbidity and premature mortality in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2D). A paradigm shift in diabetology has transformed management from glucose-centric glucocentric targets to organ-protective cardiorenal medicine. Cardiovascular outcomes trials (CVOTs) have definitively demonstrated that sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 (SGLT-2) inhibitors and glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonists (GLP-1 RAs) confer dramatic, independent reductions in major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE), heart failure hospitalizations, and progressive diabetic kidney disease.

### Core Teaching Points
- SGLT-2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin, canagliflozin) reduce hospitalization for heart failure by 30-35% and attenuate renal disease progression across the entire ejection fraction spectrum in patients with and without diabetes (EMPA-REG OUTCOME, DAPA-HF, DELIVER). (PMID 42142362)
- GLP-1 receptor agonists with proven cardiovascular benefit (subcutaneous semaglutide, liraglutide, dulaglutide) significantly reduce 3-point MACE (cardiovascular death, non-fatal MI, non-fatal stroke) by 12-14% in T2D patients with established ASCVD or high cardiovascular risk (LEADER, SUSTAIN-6, REWIND). (PMID 26378978)
- The FLOW trial demonstrated that semaglutide 1.0 mg weekly reduces the risk of major kidney disease events and cardiovascular death by 24% in patients with T2D and chronic kidney disease. (PMID 26332556)
- Current international guidelines mandate co-prescribing SGLT-2 inhibitors and GLP-1 RAs for T2D patients with established cardiovascular disease, heart failure, or CKD, irrespective of whether the baseline HbA1c is already at target. (PMID 42420095)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Additive cardiovascular benefit and tolerability of combining SGLT-2 inhibitors with dual GLP-1/GIP agonists (tirzepatide) in high-risk ASCVD cohorts.
- Cardiovascular safety and long-term MACE reduction of non-steroidal mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists (finerenone) when added on top of combined SGLT-2 inhibitor and GLP-1 RA therapy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Matching drug class to primary cardiovascular phenotype: SGLT-2 inhibitors are the preferred class for heart failure and CKD progression; GLP-1 RAs are preferred for ASCVD/stroke reduction and weight reduction.
- EMPA-REG OUTCOME and DAPA-HF landmark findings: early divergence of heart failure and mortality curves with SGLT-2 inhibition.
- Indications for GLP-1 RA in chronic kidney disease based on the landmark FLOW trial.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old male with T2D and an anterior STEMI 2 years ago has HbA1c 6.8% on metformin; the cardiologist discusses adding empagliflozin and semaglutide despite excellent glycaemic control.
- A 67-year-old woman with T2D and diabetic nephropathy (eGFR 38 mL/min, UACR 450 mg/g) is evaluated for renal-protective pharmacotherapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42142362**: *Cardiovascular and renal outcomes of GLP-1 receptor agonists in type 2 diabetes mellitus: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - European heart journal. Quality of care & clinical outcomes
- **PMID 26378978**: *Empagliflozin, Cardiovascular Outcomes, and Mortality in Type 2 Diabetes.* (2015) [literature] - None
- **PMID 26332556**: *Follow-up of Glycemic Control and Cardiovascular Outcomes in Type 2 Diabetes.* (2015) [literature] - None

## [1797] Type 2 diabetes mellitus: insulin resistance, beta-cell failure, treatment targets

**Cluster**: type 2 diabetes mellitus insulin resistance beta cell failure treatment targets | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The management of type 2 diabetes mellitus combines holistic lifestyle intervention with organ-protective pharmacotherapy tailored to patient-specific cardiovascular, renal, and metabolic risk profiles. Modern glycemic targets emphasize individualized HbA1c goals while avoiding hypoglycemia.

### Core Teaching Points
- Type 2 diabetes pathogenesis combines progressive pancreatic beta-cell secretory dysfunction with peripheral insulin resistance in skeletal muscle, liver, and adipose tissue; modern glycemic management prioritizes organ-protective agents (SGLT2 inhibitors and GLP-1 receptor agonists) independent of baseline HbA1c in patients with established cardiovascular disease, heart failure, or chronic kidney disease. (PMID 42620370)
- Glycemic targets are individualized: standard target HbA1c is <=7.0% (53 mmol/mol) for most non-pregnant adults to prevent microvascular complications, tighter targets (<6.5%) for young patients without cardiovascular disease, and relaxed targets (7.5-8.5%) for frail older adults with limited life expectancy or severe hypoglycemia risk. (PMID 42594096)
- Metformin remains the foundational first-line oral antihyperglycemic agent, acting primarily by inhibiting hepatic gluconeogenesis via AMPK activation; it requires dose adjustment when eGFR is 30-45 mL/min/1.73m2 and is contraindicated when eGFR is <30 mL/min/1.73m2 due to lactic acidosis risk. (PMID 42589432)
- SGLT2 inhibitors (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin) reduce cardiovascular death, heart failure hospitalizations, and CKD progression, while GLP-1 receptor agonists (semaglutide, dulaglutide) demonstrate robust reductions in atherosclerotic cardiovascular events (MACE) and provide substantial weight reduction. (PMID 42620370)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing of dual SGLT2 inhibitor and GLP-1 receptor agonist combination therapy in early diabetic kidney disease.
- Durability of non-insulin glycemic control using dual GIP/GLP-1 receptor co-agonists (tirzepatide) in advanced beta-cell failure.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line agent selection based on comorbidities: SGLT2 inhibitors preferred in HFrEF/HFpEF and CKD; GLP-1 RAs preferred in established ASCVD and severe obesity.
- Metformin renal cutoffs: safe down to eGFR 45; maximum 1000 mg daily for eGFR 30-45; stop when eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73m2.
- Hypoglycemia risk profiles: sulfonylureas and insulins carry high hypoglycemia risk; metformin, SGLT2 inhibitors, and GLP-1 RAs carry negligible intrinsic hypoglycemia risk.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old man with newly diagnosed type 2 diabetes (HbA1c 8.2%), established coronary artery disease, and eGFR 52 mL/min/1.73m2.
- An 81-year-old woman with longstanding T2DM, mild cognitive impairment, and recurrent presyncope on gliclazide 160 mg daily with an HbA1c of 6.1%.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42620370**: *Risk factors for hypoglycemia in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in endocrinology
- **PMID 42594096**: *HbA1c and pulmonary function in type 2 diabetes mellitus: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - PloS one

## [1798] typhoid

**Cluster**: typhoid | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
typhoid is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Efficacy, immunogenicity, and safety of typhoid conjugate vaccines in children and adolescents: a systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 41454155)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Real-world vaccine effectiveness of typhoid conjugate vaccine in children and adolescents: a systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 41125003)
- Blood culture is the definitive gold-standard diagnostic modality (sensitivity 60-80% in the first week of illness), whereas the Widal serological test exhibits poor sensitivity and specificity and is not recommended for definitive therapeutic decision-making. (PMID 30818228)
- Empirical first-line antimicrobial therapy for typhoid fever in returned travelers or endemic settings with high rates of fluoroquinolone resistance consists of intravenous ceftriaxone (2 g daily for 10-14 days) or oral azithromycin (20 mg/kg/day for 7 days), with carbapenems (meropenem) reserved for extensively drug-resistant (XDR) strains. (PMID 29415849)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with typhoid.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for typhoid.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1799] Typhoid fever: Salmonella typhi, Widal test, blood culture, azithromycin, ceftriaxone

**Cluster**: typhoid fever salmonella typhi widal test blood culture azithromycin ceftriaxone | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Typhoid fever: Salmonella typhi, Widal test, blood culture, azithromycin, ceftriaxone is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Burden of antimicrobial resistance in culture-confirmed Salmonella Typhi isolates in India from 1977 to 2024: A systematic review and meta-analysis. (PMID 41990105)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Azithromycin Resistance in Salmonella Typhi and Paratyphi Across South Asia: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. (PMID 42780551)
- Enteric (typhoid) fever caused by Salmonella enterica serovar Typhi presents with step-ladder fever, relative bradycardia (Faget sign), rose spots, and hepatosplenomegaly; blood culture is the definitive diagnostic gold standard (Widal test is unreliable due to high false-positive and false-negative rates); empirical first-line treatment for travelers returning from endemic areas (South Asia) is intravenous ceftriaxone or high-dose oral azithromycin due to widespread fluoroquinolone resistance. (PMID 41990105)
- Intestinal perforation occurs in 1-3% of hospitalized typhoid fever cases, classically during the third week of illness due to necrosis of Peyer patches in the terminal ileum, requiring emergent surgical laparotomy and expanded broad-spectrum coverage. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with typhoid fever: salmonella typhi, widal test, blood culture, azithromycin, ceftriaxone.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1802] Upper Airway Resistance Syndrome

**Cluster**: upper airway resistance syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Upper Airway Resistance Syndrome (UARS) is a sleep-disordered breathing condition positioned along the spectrum between simple snoring and obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA). UARS is characterized by repetitive increases in upper airway resistance during sleep that do not meet classic criteria for apnoeas or hypopneas, but produce increased respiratory effort leading to micro-arousals (Respiratory Effort-Related Arousals [RERAs]) and sleep fragmentation. Patients typically present with excessive daytime sleepiness, chronic fatigue, morning headaches, and insomnia, frequently without prominent obesity or severe oxygen desaturation, and are commonly misdiagnosed with fibromyalgia, chronic fatigue syndrome, or depression.

### Core Teaching Points
- UARS is characterized by repetitive Respiratory Effort-Related Arousals (RERAs) without significant hypoxemia; polysomnography demonstrates an Apnoea-Hypopnea Index (AHI) <5 events/hour, but an elevated Respiratory Disturbance Index (RDI >=5-15 events/hour, where RDI = AHI + RERA index). (PMID 33831820)
- Pathophysiology involves dynamic inspiratory flow limitation (flattening of the inspiratory nasal cannula pressure transducer wave) caused by subtle structural narrowing of the pharynx, nasal cavity, or retroglottic space. (PMID 42690361)
- Unlike typical OSA patients who are frequently obese males, UARS patients are commonly young, lean females with anatomical craniofacial variations (narrow retrognathic mandible, high-arched hard palate, nasal septal deviation, inferior turbinate hypertrophy). (PMID 42517975)
- First-line treatment is continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP), which eliminates inspiratory flow limitation and normalizes sleep architecture; alternative therapies include customized mandibular advancement devices (MAD) and targeted otorhinolaryngologic surgery (septoplasty, turbinoplasty). (PMID 42027755)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic reliability and standardization of automated scoring algorithms for RERAs across different sleep laboratory software systems.
- Long-term cardiovascular sequelae and hypertension risk in untreated UARS compared to obstructive sleep apnoea.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic definition of UARS on polysomnography: AHI <5 events/hour with elevated RDI (RERAs causing cortical arousals) in the absence of severe oxygen desaturation.
- Recognizing the clinical phenotype: lean individuals, daytime fatigue, insomnia, functional somatic symptoms, and subtle craniofacial crowding.
- Waveform hallmark on polysomnography: flattened inspiratory contour on nasal pressure cannula tracing reflecting flow limitation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old thin female (BMI 21 kg/m2) presents with profound unrefreshing sleep, daytime exhaustion, and tension headaches; home sleep apnoea testing was reported as 'normal' (AHI 2.1/h), but in-lab PSG reveals an RDI of 18/h with 42 RERAs.
- A 34-year-old male with chronic nasal congestion and morning dry mouth undergoes sleep study showing multiple arousals preceded by progressive negative intrathoracic pressure swings without desaturation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33831820**: *The efficacy of surgery for upper airway resistance syndrome: A systematic review, meta-analysis and case series.* (2021) [meta-analysis] - American journal of otolaryngology
- **PMID 42690361**: *Upper airway resistance syndrome in polycystic ovary syndrome: a narrative literature review.* (2026) [review] - Revista da Associacao Medica Brasileira (1992)
- **PMID 42517975**: *Upper airway dimensions in pediatric obstructive sleep-disordered breathing: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Sleep & breathing = Schlaf & Atmung
- **PMID 42027755**: *Gabapentinoids for Upper Airway Surgery Pain Management: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Laryngoscope investigative otolaryngology
- **PMID 42753685**: *Artificial intelligence for upper-airway segmentation, adenoid-hypertrophy assessment, and sinonasal image analysis in orthodontic and craniofacial imaging: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - International orthodontics

## [1803] Upper and lower GI bleeding

**Cluster**: upper and lower gi bleeding | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute gastrointestinal bleeding is a critical clinical presentation bifurcated by the ligament of Treitz into upper gastrointestinal bleeding (UGIB) and lower gastrointestinal bleeding (LGIB). UGIB commonly arises from peptic ulcer disease, gastroduodenal erosions, Mallory-Weiss tears, and oesophageal/gastric varices, manifesting as haematemesis or melaena with an elevated blood urea nitrogen-to-creatinine ratio (>20-30:1). LGIB originates distal to the ligament of Treitz (diverticular bleeding, angiodysplasia, ischemic colitis, haemorrhoids, neoplasms), typically presenting as haematochezia; initial management across both mandates rapid hemodynamic stabilization, risk stratification (Glasgow-Blatchford and Oakland scores), and timely endoscopy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Initial management for both UGIB and LGIB prioritizes airway, breathing, and circulation: establishing two large-bore peripheral IV lines (16G or 18G), fluid resuscitation with balanced crystalloids, and restrictive transfusion targeting haemoglobin 70-80 g/L (7-8 g/dL) in stable patients without acute coronary syndrome. (PMID 42479073)
- The Glasgow-Blatchford Score (GBS) is the superior validated risk tool for acute UGIB; a score of 0-1 identifies very low-risk patients who can safely be discharged without inpatient admission, while scores >=6 predict high risk of rebleeding and intervention. (PMID 42667281)
- For acute non-variceal UGIB, intravenous proton pump inhibitor (PPI) therapy (e.g., omeprazole 80 mg IV bolus followed by 8 mg/h infusion, or 40 mg IV twice daily) stabilizes gastric pH >6 to promote platelet aggregation and clot formation prior to endoscopy. (PMID 42801172)
- In patients presenting with massive haematochezia and hemodynamic shock, an upper GI bleed must always be excluded (brisk duodenal bleeding with rapid transit), warranting immediate upper endoscopy prior to colonoscopy. (PMID 42819247)
- For acute LGIB, the Oakland score predicts safe outpatient discharge (score <=8); colonoscopy is performed after complete oral purgative bowel preparation in hospitalized patients. (PMID 42316521)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of colonoscopy in acute lower GI bleeding (urgent <24 hours versus elective after elective bowel prep during admission).
- Optimal timing for restarting therapeutic anticoagulation and antiplatelet agents following endoscopically verified haemostasis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing massive UGIB from LGIB: recognizing that rapid upper GI bleeding can present with bright red haematochezia and a markedly elevated BUN/creatinine ratio (>20-30).
- Applying the Glasgow-Blatchford Score (GBS): identifying low-risk scores (0-1) eligible for outpatient management.
- Restrictive red blood cell transfusion threshold (70-80 g/L) versus liberal transfusion (100 g/L) in acute GI bleeding, reducing rebleeding and mortality.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old man taking naproxen presents with melaena, BP 92/58 mmHg, HR 118 bpm, Hb 74 g/L, and BUN 18.2 mmol/L with creatinine 85 umol/L; GBS is 12, prompting emergency resuscitation and endoscopy.
- A 74-year-old woman presents with painless bright red blood per rectum and stable vital signs; the clinical team calculates an Oakland score of 7 and plans outpatient colonoscopic workup.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42479073**: *Proton pump inhibitors for prevention of upper and lower gastrointestinal bleeding in patients taking aspirin: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - European journal of clinical pharmacology
- **PMID 42667281**: *American Society for Gastrointestinal Endoscopy guideline on the role of endoscopy in acute lower gastrointestinal bleeding.* (2026) [guideline] - Gastrointestinal endoscopy
- **PMID 42801172**: *Is ultra-early endoscopy always beneficial? A systematic review and meta-analysis of timing-dependent outcomes in nonvariceal upper gastrointestinal bleeding.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - PeerJ
- **PMID 42819247**: *Role of prokinetic drugs prior to endoscopy in acute upper gastrointestinal bleeding: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in gastroenterology (Lausanne, Switzerland)
- **PMID 42316521**: *Endoscopic Treatment of Upper Gastrointestinal Bleeding.* (2026) [literature] - None

## [1804] Upper gastrointestinal bleeding management

**Cluster**: upper gastrointestinal bleeding management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Management of acute upper gastrointestinal bleeding (UGIB) coordinates emergency hemodynamic resuscitation, risk stratification, empiric medical therapies, and therapeutic upper endoscopy within 24 hours. Endoscopic evaluation establishes etiology—most frequently peptic ulcer disease (PUD, ~50%), followed by oesophagogastric varices, erosive gastritis, and Mallory-Weiss tears. For peptic ulcers, the Forrest classification determines endoscopic haemostasis (high-risk stigmata: Forrest Ia spurting, Ib oozing, IIa non-bleeding visible vessel, and IIb adherent clot), utilizing dual multimodal endoscopic techniques (epinephrine injection combined with thermal coagulation, mechanical hemoclips, or hemostatic powders).

### Core Teaching Points
- Early risk stratification using the Glasgow-Blatchford Score (GBS) should be performed upon presentation; endoscopy should be performed within 24 hours of presentation after initial hemodynamic resuscitation (ultra-early endoscopy <6 hours does not improve clinical outcomes in non-variceal bleeding). (PMID 42447876)
- High-risk peptic ulcer lesions (Forrest Ia, Ib, and IIa) mandate dual-modality endoscopic haemostasis (e.g., hemoclip or bipolar electrocoagulation PLUS dilute epinephrine injection), followed by high-dose intravenous PPI therapy for 72 hours. (PMID 42819247)
- Pre-endoscopic administration of a single intravenous dose of erythromycin (250 mg IV 30-60 minutes prior) promotes gastric emptying of blood and clots, significantly improving endoscopic visualization and reducing the need for repeat procedures. (PMID 42801172)
- Testing for Helicobacter pylori is mandatory in all patients with bleeding peptic ulcers; initial biopsy during acute bleeding carries high false-negative rates, requiring confirmation with stool antigen or urea breath test 4-6 weeks post-bleed off PPIs. (PMID 42336227)
- Transfusion strategy should be restrictive (transfusion trigger Hb <70 g/L, target 70-90 g/L), except in patients with active acute coronary syndromes (target Hb >=80-100 g/L) (Villanueva trial). (PMID 41575324)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of topically applied hemostatic powders (e.g., Hemospray, TC-325) as primary standalone therapy versus rescue therapy for refractory non-variceal bleeding.
- Safety and timing of early oral feeding in patients following successful endoscopic treatment of high-risk ulcers.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Forrest classification and endoscopic intervention thresholds: identifying which lesions require active treatment (Ia, Ib, IIa, and selected IIb) versus clean-based ulcers (III) which do not.
- Role of pre-procedure IV erythromycin in clearing the gastric fundus and improving mucosal visualization.
- Managing recurrent ulcer bleeding following initial successful endoscopy: repeat endoscopic intervention is preferred first-line over immediate emergency surgery or transcatheter arterial embolization (TAE).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 55-year-old male presents with haematemesis; endoscopy reveals a 1.5 cm duodenal bulb ulcer with a pigmented non-bleeding visible vessel (Forrest IIa); dual-modality haemostasis with hemoclips and thermal probe is performed.
- A 70-year-old female with a bleeding gastric ulcer tests negative for H. pylori on rapid urease biopsy performed during active haemorrhage; the physician arranges confirmatory testing after discharge.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42447876**: *Curriculum on management of acute upper gastrointestinal bleeding: European Society of Gastrointestinal Endoscopy (ESGE) Position Statement.* (2026) [guideline] - Endoscopy
- **PMID 42819247**: *Role of prokinetic drugs prior to endoscopy in acute upper gastrointestinal bleeding: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in gastroenterology (Lausanne, Switzerland)
- **PMID 42801172**: *Is ultra-early endoscopy always beneficial? A systematic review and meta-analysis of timing-dependent outcomes in nonvariceal upper gastrointestinal bleeding.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - PeerJ
- **PMID 42336227**: *Assessment of in-hospital mortality in patients with upper gastrointestinal bleeding on antithrombotic treatment.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The American journal of medicine
- **PMID 41575324**: *Hemostatic Powder for Non-Malignant Upper Gastrointestinal Bleeding: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of gastroenterology and hepatology

## [1807] Uraemic Complications

**Cluster**: uraemic complications | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Uraemia is the toxic syndrome resulting from the accumulation of organic waste products and nitrogenous metabolites (uraemic retention solutes) normally cleared by healthy kidneys, developing in advanced acute kidney injury or stage 5 chronic kidney disease (CKD). Uraemic toxicity affects virtually every organ system, manifesting as uraemic encephalopathy (asterixis, cognitive blunting, seizures), uraemic pericarditis (friction rub, tamponade risk), uraemic coagulopathy (platelet dysfunction), and uraemic cardiomyopathy. Certain uraemic complications represent absolute, life-threatening indications for emergent renal replacement therapy (haemodialysis), overriding conservative medical stabilization.

### Core Teaching Points
- Absolute emergent indications for renal replacement therapy (haemodialysis) in acute or chronic kidney failure are remembered by the AEIOU mnemonic: Acidosis (refractory metabolic pH <7.1-7.2), Electrolytes (severe refractory hyperkalaemia >6.5 mmol/L), Ingestion/toxins, Overload (refractory pulmonary oedema), and Uraemic complications (pericarditis, encephalopathy, bleeding). (PMID 41817599)
- Uraemic pericarditis presents with retrosternal chest pain and a pericardial friction rub; unlike typical viral pericarditis, ECG often shows NO ST-segment elevation due to lack of epicardial myocardial involvement; it is an absolute indication for urgent haemodialysis (anticoagulation/heparin must be avoided during dialysis to prevent haemorrhagic tamponade). (PMID 41875303)
- Uraemic coagulopathy is characterized by abnormal platelet aggregation and adhesion due to impaired von Willebrand factor interaction with dysfunctional platelet GPIIb/IIIa receptors, presenting as bleeding with normal platelet count but prolonged bleeding time. (PMID 42664981)
- Desmopressin (DDAVP, 0.3 ug/kg IV or subcutaneous) is the fastest-acting agent to reverse uraemic bleeding prior to urgent invasive procedures (e.g., kidney biopsy), releasing endothelial stores of vWF and factor VIII within 30-60 minutes. (PMID 40675722)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Mechanisms of uraemic cardiomyopathy and reversal of myocardial fibrosis following renal transplantation versus intensive haemodialysis.
- Role of Middle Molecules (e.g., beta-2 microglobulin, FGF-23, indoxyl sulfate) clearance with expanded hemodialysis (HDx) and online hemodiafiltration.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Absolute indications for emergent dialysis: identifying uraemic pericarditis or encephalopathy as mandates for urgent dialysis regardless of exact serum creatinine level.
- Acute management of uraemic bleeding diathesis prior to invasive surgery: administration of DDAVP (desmopressin), cryoprecipitate, or conjugated estrogens.
- Why systemic anticoagulation is held or minimized during haemodialysis in patients with uraemic pericarditis (preventing conversion into haemorrhagic pericardial tamponade).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old male with untreated ESRD presents with confusion, lethargy, asterixis, and a loud triphasic friction rub over the left sternal border; ECG is non-diagnostic; urgent nephrology consult for dialysis is requested.
- A patient with stage 5 CKD is scheduled for an urgent percutaneous kidney biopsy; the nephrologist prescribes pre-procedure DDAVP (0.3 ug/kg) to correct uraemic platelet dysfunction.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41817599**: *Effect of Preprocedural Desmopressin on Complications after Kidney Biopsy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Kidney360
- **PMID 41875303**: *Reversal of Uraemic Cardiomyopathy following Kidney Transplantation: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Myocardial Strain Compared to Left Ventricular Volumetry as a Potential Early Marker.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Kidney & blood pressure research

## [1808] Urate-lowering therapy: allopurinol, febuxostat, target serum urate, titration

**Cluster**: urate lowering therapy allopurinol febuxostat target serum urate titration | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Effective long-term management of gout requires treat-to-target urate-lowering therapy (ULT) to promote monosodium urate crystal dissolution, prevent recurrent acute flares, and resolve tophaceous deposits. Stepwise dose titration and concomitant flare prophylaxis are essential for successful treatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- Allopurinol is the preferred first-line urate-lowering therapy for all patients, starting at a low dose (<=100 mg/day, or <=50 mg/day in chronic kidney disease stage 3-5) and titrated upward every 2 to 4 weeks based on serial serum urate measurements to achieve target levels. (PMID 42458015)
- Treat-to-target serum urate thresholds are <6.0 mg/dL (<360 umol/L) for all gout patients to prevent crystal formation, and a more stringent target of <5.0 mg/dL (<300 umol/L) for patients with severe tophaceous gout, chronic erosive arthropathy, or frequent flares. (PMID 32391934)
- Concomitant anti-inflammatory flare prophylaxis (colchicine 0.5 mg once or twice daily, or low-dose NSAIDs) is strongly recommended when initiating or titrating urate-lowering therapy and should be continued for at least 3 to 6 months to prevent mobilization flares. (PMID 42458015)
- Prior to starting allopurinol, HLA-B*58:01 allele screening is recommended in high-risk populations (individuals of Han Chinese, Korean, Thai, or African American descent) due to a dramatically elevated risk of allopurinol hypersensitivity syndrome (DRESS/SJS/TEN); febuxostat serves as an alternative xanthine oxidase inhibitor. (PMID 32391934)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cardiovascular safety profile of febuxostat compared to allopurinol in patients with preexisting severe cardiovascular disease (CARES vs FAST trial discrepancies).
- Optimal duration of flare prophylaxis following complete clinical dissolution of all palpable tophi.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Titration in renal impairment: allopurinol can be safely titrated above conventional renal doses (beyond 300 mg daily) with gradual dose escalations and close monitoring.
- Pharmacogenetic screening: HLA-B*58:01 screening prevents life-threatening allopurinol-induced severe cutaneous adverse reactions.
- Initiation timing during acute gout flare: ULT can be started during an active flare provided effective anti-inflammatory therapy has been initiated.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old man of Han Chinese descent with recurrent podagra and palpable olecranon tophi, evaluated for urate-lowering therapy initiation.
- A 68-year-old man with stage 4 CKD (eGFR 24 mL/min) and chronic tophaceous gout whose serum urate remains 8.8 mg/dL on allopurinol 100 mg daily.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42458015**: *Urate-lowering effects of losartan: a meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials and target trial emulation.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Hypertension research : official journal of the Japanese Society of Hypertension
- **PMID 32391934**: *2020 American College of Rheumatology Guideline for the Management of Gout.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [1810] Urinary tract infections and pyelonephritis

**Cluster**: urinary tract infections and pyelonephritis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Urinary tract infections (UTIs) represent an ascending spectrum of microbial colonization extending from cystitis (bladder mucosal inflammation) to acute pyelonephritis (infection of the renal pelvis and kidney parenchyma). Uropathogenic Escherichia coli (UPEC) causes >75-80% of infections, with Klebsiella pneumoniae, Proteus mirabilis, and Enterococcus faecalis accounting for remaining cases. Clinical evaluation hinges on differentiating cystitis (dysuria, urgency, frequency, suprapubic pain without systemic signs) from pyelonephritis (fever, chills, flank/costovertebral angle tenderness, nausea), which mandates systemic antimicrobials and exclusion of obstructive uropathy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Acute uncomplicated pyelonephritis presents with systemic manifestations: fever (>38 C), rigors, costovertebral angle tenderness, and flank pain; urine culture with antimicrobial susceptibility testing is mandatory in all suspected pyelonephritis cases. (PMID 41419213)
- First-line outpatient empirical oral therapy for uncomplicated pyelonephritis in regions with local fluoroquinolone resistance <10% is ciprofloxacin (500 mg bid for 7 days) or levofloxacin (750 mg daily for 5 days); if resistance is >=10%, an initial dose of IV ceftriaxone 1 g is required. (PMID 42845845)
- Renal imaging (contrast-enhanced CT or renal ultrasound) is NOT routinely required in uncomplicated pyelonephritis that improves within 48-72 hours, but is urgently indicated for persistent fever beyond 72 hours, septic shock, palpable flank mass, or suspected stone obstruction. (PMID 42803590)
- Obstructive pyelonephritis (infected hydronephrosis secondary to an obstructing ureteral calculus) is a urologic emergency requiring immediate urinary tract decompression (percutaneous nephrostomy or JJ ureteral stenting) and broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics. (PMID 42097635)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Oral step-down treatment duration for multi-drug resistant ESBL pyelonephritis using oral fosfomycin or oral carbapenems (tebipenem).
- Predictive value of serum procalcitonin versus CRP in guiding early antibiotic cessation in hospitalized pyelonephritis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Outpatient antibiotic duration for acute uncomplicated pyelonephritis: 7 days for oral ciprofloxacin vs 14 days for oral beta-lactams (e.g., augmentin or cephalosporins).
- Indications for urgent imaging (contrast CT of abdomen/pelvis) in pyelonephritis: failure to defervesce after 72 hours of appropriate antibiotic therapy (evaluating renal or perinephric abscess).
- Management of pyelonephritis in pregnancy: mandatory hospital admission for parenteral antibiotics (e.g., IV ceftriaxone) due to high risk of sepsis and preterm labour.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old healthy woman presents with temperature 39.1 C, right costovertebral angle tenderness, vomiting, and pyuria; she is initiated on outpatient oral ciprofloxacin after an initial IV ceftriaxone dose.
- A 58-year-old diabetic male treated for pyelonephritis with IV ceftriaxone remains febrile with persistent left flank pain on day 4; renal CT reveals a 4 cm intrarenal fluid collection consistent with a renal abscess.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41419213**: *Clinical Practice Guidelines by Infectious Diseases Society of America: 2025 Guideline on Management and Treatment of Complicated Urinary Tract Infections-Selection of Antibiotic Therapy for Complicated Urinary Tract Infections.* (2026) [guideline] - Clinical infectious diseases : an official publication of the Infectious Diseases Society of America
- **PMID 42845845**: *A Systematic Review and Network Meta-analysis of Empirical Antibiotics for Complicated Urinary Tract Infections.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Open forum infectious diseases
- **PMID 42803590**: *A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of the Role of Ultrasonography for the Diagnosis of Vesicoureteral Reflux in Pediatric Patients Following Urinary Tract Infections: Technical Report.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Pediatrics
