## [1811] Urinary tract infections: uncomplicated vs complicated, catheter-associated, treatment duration

**Cluster**: urinary tract infections uncomplicated vs complicated catheter associated treatment duration | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Accurate classification of urinary tract infections dictates diagnostic workup, choice of empirical antibiotics, and treatment duration. Uncomplicated cystitis occurs in non-pregnant, immunocompetent premenopausal adult women without anatomical or functional urinary abnormalities. Complicated UTIs (cUTIs) occur in the presence of factors that compromise host defenses or increase infection severity: male sex, pregnancy, catheterization, urologic instrumentation, neurogenic bladder, urinary tract stones, or immunosuppression, carrying higher rates of multidrug-resistant pathogens.

### Core Teaching Points
- Uncomplicated cystitis first-line therapies include: nitrofurantoin monohydrate/macrocrystals (100 mg bid for 5 days), trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (160/800 mg bid for 3 days, if local resistance <20%), or fosfomycin trometamol (3 g single oral dose); fluoroquinolones are explicitly not recommended for uncomplicated cystitis. (PMID 41419213)
- Catheter-associated urinary tract infection (CAUTI) is defined by presence of symptoms referable to the urinary tract (fever, suprapubic pain, altered mental status) with urine culture >=10^3 CFU/mL in a patient with a current or recently removed (<48 h) indwelling catheter; asymptomatic bacteriuria (ASB) in catheterized patients must NOT be treated. (PMID 42803592)
- When treating CAUTI, the indwelling urinary catheter must be removed or replaced prior to initiating antibiotic therapy if it has been in place for >7-14 days, as intraluminal biofilms harbor persistent bacterial colonies that prevent cure. (PMID 42803578)
- Treatment duration guidelines recommend short-course therapy: 3 to 5 days for uncomplicated cystitis, 7 days for uncomplicated pyelonephritis (fluoroquinolones), 7 days for prompt-responding cUTI/CAUTI, and 14 days for men with suspected acute bacterial prostatitis. (PMID 42803594)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of short 7-day courses versus traditional 14-day courses for complicated UTI in male patients with controlled lower urinary tract symptoms.
- Role of novel siderophore cephalosporins (cefiderocol) and beta-lactamase inhibitor combinations (ceftazidime-avibactam, meropenem-vaborbactam) in complicated UTIs due to carbapenem-resistant Enterobacteriaceae.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Appropriate antibiotic treatment durations: 5 days for nitrofurantoin, 3 days for TMP-SMX, 1 single dose for fosfomycin in uncomplicated cystitis.
- Management of asymptomatic bacteriuria in catheterized patients: withholding antibiotics except in pregnancy or prior to urologic instrumentation with anticipated mucosal bleeding.
- Management of CAUTI: mandatory replacement or removal of the catheter before starting targeted antimicrobial therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 29-year-old non-pregnant woman presents with 2 days of burning dysuria and frequency; urine dipstick is leukocyte esterase and nitrite positive; the clinician selects nitrofurantoin 100 mg bid for 5 days.
- A 72-year-old nursing home resident with a long-term Foley catheter has cloudy, foul-smelling urine and a urine culture growing 10^5 CFU/mL Klebsiella; he has no fever, flank pain, or systemic symptoms.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41419213**: *Clinical Practice Guidelines by Infectious Diseases Society of America: 2025 Guideline on Management and Treatment of Complicated Urinary Tract Infections-Selection of Antibiotic Therapy for Complicated Urinary Tract Infections.* (2026) [guideline] - Clinical infectious diseases : an official publication of the Infectious Diseases Society of America
- **PMID 42803592**: *Urinary Tract Infection Diagnosis-A Companion to the American Academy of Pediatrics' 2026 Clinical Practice Guideline on the Diagnosis and Management of Urinary Tract Infection: Technical Report.* (2026) [guideline] - Pediatrics
- **PMID 42803578**: *Clinical Practice Guideline for the Diagnosis and Treatment of Urinary Tract Infection in Children From 8 Days to 5 Years of Age.* (2026) [guideline] - Pediatrics
- **PMID 42803594**: *A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of the Prevention and Management of Pediatric Urinary Tract Infection and Suspected Urinary Tract Infection: Technical Report.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Pediatrics
- **PMID 42803590**: *A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of the Role of Ultrasonography for the Diagnosis of Vesicoureteral Reflux in Pediatric Patients Following Urinary Tract Infections: Technical Report.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Pediatrics

## [1813] Urticaria and angioedema: classification and management

**Cluster**: urticaria and angioedema classification and management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Urticaria and angioedema are common mast cell- or bradykinin-mediated vascular reactions characterized by transient erythematous wheals (urticaria), deeper subcutaneous/submucosal swelling (angioedema), or both. Urticaria is classified by duration into acute (<6 weeks) and chronic (>=6 weeks), and by triggers into chronic spontaneous urticaria (CSU) and chronic inducible urticaria (CIndU: cold, heat, pressure, solar, cholinergic). The critical clinical step is distinguishing histamine/mast cell-mediated angioedema (accompanied by wheals, pruritus, responding to antihistamines/epinephrine) from bradykinin-mediated angioedema (hereditary angioedema [HAE] or ACE inhibitor-induced, which lack urticaria or pruritus and fail to respond to steroids, antihistamines, or epinephrine).

### Core Teaching Points
- Chronic spontaneous urticaria (CSU) is treated using a stepwise algorithm: Step 1 is standard-dose second-generation H1-antihistamines (cetirizine, fexofenadine, bilastine); Step 2 is up-dosing the second-generation H1-antihistamine up to 4-fold the licensed dose before switching classes. (PMID 41649409)
- For chronic urticaria refractory to 4-fold antihistamines (Step 3), omalizumab (monoclonal anti-IgE antibody, 300 mg subcutaneously every 4 weeks) is the preferred, highly effective targeted therapy; Step 4 is oral cyclosporine for omalizumab-refractory disease. (PMID 39938775)
- Bradykinin-mediated angioedema (hereditary angioedema [HAE], ACE inhibitor-induced angioedema) presents with non-pitting, non-pruritic, painful swelling WITHOUT urticarial wheals; it does NOT respond to antihistamines, corticosteroids, or epinephrine. (PMID 42794167)
- Hereditary angioedema (HAE) is diagnosed by screening serum C4 levels (consistently low in HAE Type I and II) followed by C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH) antigenic level and functional activity; acute attacks are treated with C1-INH concentrate, icatibant (bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist), or ecallantide. (PMID 39168302)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and clinical position of novel targeted biologics and oral agents (e.g., dupilumab, remibrutinib [BTK inhibitor]) in omalizumab-resistant CSU.
- Optimal long-term prophylaxis for hereditary angioedema: comparing oral plasma kallikrein inhibitors (berotralstat) with monoclonal antibodies (lanadelumab).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Up-dosing protocol in CSU: increasing second-generation H1-antihistamines up to 4 times standard dose before adding omalizumab.
- Differentiating mast cell-mediated angioedema (itching, hives present, responsive to epinephrine/antihistamines) from bradykinin-mediated angioedema (no hives, painful, unresponsive to epinephrine).
- Screening and emergency treatment of HAE: low C4, confirmation with C1-INH function, and acute termination with icatibant or C1-INH replacement.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old woman with daily itchy wheals and lip swelling lasting >8 weeks continues to experience severe symptoms on cetirizine 10 mg daily; the allergist advises up-dosing cetirizine to 40 mg daily.
- A 64-year-old man taking lisinopril presents to the ED with severe tongue and laryngeal swelling without hives or itching; he fails to improve after IV diphenhydramine, methylprednisolone, and IM epinephrine.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41649409**: *The International Guideline for the Definition, Classification, Diagnosis and Management of Urticaria.* (2026) [guideline] - Allergy
- **PMID 39938775**: *[Translated article] Erythema, Localized Edema and Heat vs Forearm Perimeter Increase. Time to Revise the Consensus Recommendations on the Vortex Provocation Test in Vibratory Urticaria-Angioedema?.* (2025) [guideline] - Actas dermo-sifiliograficas
- **PMID 42794167**: *Evidence-Based Clinical Recommendations on the Appropriateness of Prescriptions and Diagnostic Tests in Paediatric Allergology: Focus on IgE-Mediated and Non-IgE Mediated Food Allergy, Eosinophilic Gastrointestinal Disease, Urticaria, Angioedema and Atopic Dermatitis.* (2026) [review] - Children (Basel, Switzerland)

## [1815] UTI prophylaxis

**Cluster**: uti prophylaxis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Urinary tract infection (UTI) prophylaxis aims to break the recurrent cycle of uncomplicated or complicated infections while minimizing the emergence of multidrug-resistant uropathogens and collateral microbiome damage. In adults with recurrent uncomplicated UTIs (>=2 in 6 months or >=3 in 12 months), non-antimicrobial interventions form the initial foundation. When non-antimicrobial measures fail, low-dose continuous antibiotic prophylaxis (nitrofurantoin, trimethoprim) or post-coital prophylaxis is highly effective, accompanied by scheduled reassessment and antimicrobial stewardship.

### Core Teaching Points
- Vaginal oestrogen therapy in postmenopausal women with recurrent UTIs restores normal premenopausal acidophilic lactobacilli, lowers vaginal pH, and reduces recurrence rates by over 50% without systemic hormonal adverse effects. (PMID 42287451)
- Methenamine hippurate (1 g twice daily) provides an effective non-antibiotic preventative alternative that converts to bacteriostatic formaldehyde in acidic urine, matching low-dose daily antibiotic prophylaxis without driving microbial resistance (ALTAR trial). (PMID 41785522)
- Post-coital antimicrobial prophylaxis (single low dose of nitrofurantoin 50-100 mg, trimethoprim 100 mg, or cephalexin 250 mg taken within 2 hours of intercourse) is as effective as continuous daily prophylaxis in women whose UTIs are triggered by sexual activity, with fewer adverse effects. (PMID 41160196)
- Continuous daily low-dose antibiotic prophylaxis (e.g., nitrofurantoin 50-100 mg daily at bedtime) is reserved for patients failing non-antimicrobial measures; treatment should be limited to 3-6 months with mandatory surveillance for nitrofurantoin pulmonary fibrosis and hepatotoxicity in long-term use. (PMID 36453217)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term efficacy and mucosal immunological durability of sublingual bacterial whole-cell polyvalent vaccines (MV140 / Uromune).
- Optimal role and clinical indications for intravesical glycosaminoglycan (GAG) layer replenishment instillations (hyaluronic acid / chondroitin sulfate).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line non-antimicrobial prevention in postmenopausal women: topical vaginal oestrogen cream or pessaries.
- ALTAR trial conclusions: non-inferiority of methenamine hippurate compared to daily antibiotic prophylaxis in preventing recurrent UTIs.
- Long-term adverse effects of continuous nitrofurantoin: chronic pulmonary interstitial fibrosis and drug-induced autoimmune hepatitis in older women.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 63-year-old postmenopausal woman with 4 symptomatic E. coli UTIs in the past year presents requesting daily antibiotics; pelvic exam reveals pale, dry, atrophic vaginal mucosa.
- A 27-year-old female experiences recurrent dysuria exclusively within 24-48 hours after sexual intercourse; she is offered post-coital single-dose prophylaxis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1816] Uveitis in JIA: silent inflammation, slit-lamp screening, methotrexate, adalimumab

**Cluster**: uveitis in jia silent inflammation slit lamp screening methotrexate adalimumab | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Uveitis in JIA: silent inflammation, slit-lamp screening, methotrexate, adalimumab is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Initiation and Continuation of Adalimumab with Methotrexate for Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis-Associated Uveitis: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials. (PMID 41945858)
- JIA-associated uveitis is characteristically asymptomatic and insidious ('white, quiet eye') without redness or pain; routine periodic slit-lamp screening every 3 months is mandatory in high-risk children (oligoarticular JIA, ANA-positive, age <7 years). (PMID 31562236)
- Topical corticosteroids are first-line for active anterior uveitis; however, disease requiring more than 1-2 drops daily after 3 months warrants initiation of subcutaneous methotrexate to prevent band keratopathy, cataracts, glaucoma, and vision loss. (PMID 31562236)
- In methotrexate-refractory or intolerant JIA-associated uveitis, the monoclonal TNF antibody adalimumab (supported by the landmark SYCAMORE trial) is the evidence-based first-line biologic. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with uveitis in jia: silent inflammation, slit-lamp screening, methotrexate, adalimumab.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1818] Vaccine responses in immunocompromised adults

**Cluster**: vaccine responses in immunocompromised adults | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Immunocompromised adults—including solid organ transplant recipients, haematologic malignancy patients, individuals on biologic or targeted immunosuppression (anti-CD20 rituximab, anti-TNF, JAK inhibitors), and people living with advanced HIV—face heightened vulnerability to vaccine-preventable infections. Immunosuppressive therapies significantly impair both humoral (antibody seroconversion) and cellular (T-cell) vaccine responses. Core management principles include administering vaccines prior to planned immunosuppression whenever feasible, utilizing high-dose or adjuvanted vaccine formulations, adhering to specific revaccination schedules, and strictly avoiding live attenuated vaccines during active immunosuppression.

### Core Teaching Points
- Live attenuated vaccines (e.g., MMR, varicella, yellow fever, oral typhoid, BCG) are strictly contraindicated in severely immunocompromised individuals due to the risk of uncontrolled viral replication and fatal disseminated vaccine-strain infection; recombinant or inactivated vaccines are safe. (PMID 38543908)
- B-cell depleting therapies (rituximab, obinutuzumab) virtually abolish humoral antibody response to vaccines for at least 6 months post-infusion; non-live vaccines should be administered at least 2 to 4 weeks prior to initiating anti-CD20 therapy or delayed until B-cell recovery. (PMID 41762115)
- Recombinant zoster vaccine (Shingrix, a non-live adjuvanted glycoprotein E vaccine) is safe and strongly recommended for immunocompromised adults aged >=19 years to prevent herpes zoster and post-herpetic neuralgia, completely replacing contraindicated live zoster vaccines. (PMID 42721116)
- High-dose or adjuvanted inactivated influenza vaccines and pneumococcal conjugate vaccines (PCV20 alone or PCV15 followed by PPSV23) achieve superior immunogenicity and protective antibody titers in solid organ transplant and oncology populations compared to standard formulations. (PMID 42684271)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical utility of measuring post-vaccination antibody titers or T-cell ELISpot assays to determine the necessity of booster doses in immunocompromised hosts.
- Optimal timing of temporary immunosuppressive hold (e.g., holding methotrexate for 1-2 weeks post-vaccination) to augment vaccine immunogenicity without triggering autoimmune flares.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Absolute contraindication of live attenuated vaccines in severely immunosuppressed patients.
- Timing of vaccine administration relative to B-cell depleting therapy (rituximab): optimizing vaccine delivery before infusion or waiting >=6 months.
- Selection of the recombinant zoster vaccine (Shingrix) over live zoster vaccine in immunocompromised populations.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old woman with rheumatoid arthritis scheduled to initiate rituximab therapy is brought up to date on pneumococcal, recombinant zoster, and COVID-19 vaccinations 3 weeks prior to her first infusion.
- A 42-year-old male 9 months post-allogeneic stem cell transplantation attends clinic inquiring about whether he can receive the live varicella vaccine.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38543908**: *Impact of Immunomodulatory Therapy on COVID-19 Vaccine Response in Patients with Autoimmune Inflammatory Rheumatic Diseases.* (2024) [literature] - None
- **PMID 41762115**: *IDSA 2025 Guideline on the Use of Vaccines for the Prevention of Seasonal Influenza Infections in Immunocompromised Patients.* (2026) [guideline] - Clinical infectious diseases : an official publication of the Infectious Diseases Society of America
- **PMID 42721116**: *Immunogenicity and safety of inactivated TZ84- vs. HM175-based hepatitis A vaccines in pediatric populations: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Human vaccines & immunotherapeutics
- **PMID 42684271**: *Immunogenicity and safety of prophylactic HPV vaccines in people living with HIV: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Human vaccines & immunotherapeutics

## [1819] Vancomycin therapeutic drug monitoring: AUC-based dosing, nephrotoxicity, trough targets

**Cluster**: vancomycin therapeutic drug monitoring auc based dosing nephrotoxicity trough targets | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vancomycin remains a mainstay glycopeptide antibiotic for serious Methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA) infections. Modern therapeutic drug monitoring consensus guidelines recommend area under the curve (AUC)-guided dosing to ensure clinical efficacy while minimizing acute kidney injury.

### Core Teaching Points
- Consensus therapeutic drug monitoring guidelines recommend targeting a 24-hour area under the curve to minimum inhibitory concentration ratio (AUC24/MIC) of 400 to 600 mg*h/L (assuming a broth microdilution MIC of 1 mg/L) for serious invasive MRSA infections (bacteremia, infective endocarditis, osteomyelitis, hospital-acquired pneumonia). (PMID 42410537)
- AUC-guided dosing using Bayesian software modeling (or two pharmacokinetic concentrations: peak at 1-2 hours post-infusion and trough) is strongly preferred over trough-only monitoring (15-20 ug/mL) because it significantly reduces the incidence of vancomycin-associated acute kidney injury (AKI) without compromising clinical efficacy. (PMID 32191793)
- A weight-based loading dose of 25 to 35 mg/kg (based on actual body weight, capped at 3,000 mg) is recommended for critically ill patients with suspected or confirmed serious MRSA infections to rapidly achieve therapeutic target concentrations. (PMID 42410537)
- Vancomycin-induced nephrotoxicity (toxic acute tubular necrosis) risk increases substantially when AUC24 exceeds 650 mg*h/L or sustained troughs exceed 20 ug/mL, and is potentiated by concomitant nephrotoxic agents (piperacillin-tazobactam, aminoglycosides, loop diuretics, IV iodinated contrast). (PMID 32191793)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal AUC monitoring protocols in non-critically ill patients with non-invasive infections (skin and soft tissue infections, uncomplicated urinary tract infections).
- Pharmacokinetic performance of Bayesian priors in patients with rapidly fluctuating renal clearance or requiring continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- AUC24/MIC target window: 400–600 mg*h/L balances maximal MRSA bactericidal killing against nephrotoxicity risk.
- Trough-only monitoring obsolescence: traditional 15–20 ug/mL trough targets cause excess acute kidney injury; AUC-guided dosing is now standard of care.
- Nephrotoxic synergy: combining vancomycin with piperacillin-tazobactam dramatically elevates AKI rates compared with vancomycin plus cefepime.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 55-year-old woman with MRSA bacteremia and suspected mitral valve endocarditis whose 48-hour vancomycin trough is 24 ug/mL with an acute rise in serum creatinine from 0.8 to 1.6 mg/dL.
- A 70-year-old critically ill man weighing 100 kg admitted with septic shock secondary to severe hospital-acquired pneumonia requiring emergency loading dose calculation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42410537**: *Guideline-informed vancomycin therapeutic drug monitoring indications in an adult intensive care unit: a retrospective descriptive study.* (2026) [guideline] - BMC anesthesiology
- **PMID 32191793**: *Therapeutic monitoring of vancomycin for serious methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus infections: A revised consensus guideline.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [1820] Vapor Gas Dust and Fume exposures

**Cluster**: vapor gas dust and fume exposures | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Occupational vapours, gases, dusts and fumes cause work-related asthma, COPD and acute inhalational injury. The diagnosis depends on exposure history and serial peak flow at and away from work.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnosis needs a detailed occupational history, spirometry and serial peak expiratory flow at work and away from work; specific inhalation challenge is done in specialist centres. (PMID 42001820)
- Occupational asthma from high-molecular-weight agents (flour, grain dust, laboratory animals) is IgE-mediated; low-molecular-weight chemicals (isocyanates, glutaraldehyde) act by non-IgE or irritant mechanisms. (PMID 42001820)
- Early removal from the causative exposure is the most important prognostic factor; prolonged exposure after symptoms leads to persistent airway hyperresponsiveness. (PMID 31562236)
- Acute high-dose exposures (chlorine, ammonia, nitrogen oxides, smoke) cause chemical pneumonitis and ARDS and need supportive care. (PMID 31562236)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with vapor gas dust and fume exposures.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Peak flow pattern in occupational asthma.
- High versus low molecular weight agents.
- Prognosis and removal from exposure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of vapor gas dust and fume exposures requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1822] Variceal Haemorrhage

**Cluster**: variceal haemorrhage | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute variceal haemorrhage (AVH) is a catastrophic medical emergency in patients with cirrhosis and portal hypertension, carrying a 6-week mortality of 15-20%. Rupture occurs when the hepatic venous pressure gradient (HVPG) exceeds the critical threshold of 12 mmHg, leading to progressive dilatation and wall tension rupture of thin-walled submucosal oesophageal or gastric varices. Management follows a time-sensitive multimodality protocol: restrictive blood transfusion, early vasoactive pharmacotherapy (terlipressin, octreotide), prophylactic IV antibiotics (ceftriaxone), and emergency endoscopic variceal ligation (EVL) within 12 hours of presentation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Vasoactive drug therapy (terlipressin continuous infusion or bolus, or somatostatin/octreotide) must be initiated IMMEDIATELY upon suspicion of variceal bleeding, prior to endoscopy, to reduce splanchnic blood flow and portal pressure. (PMID 42090574)
- Prophylactic antibiotic therapy (intravenous ceftriaxone 1 g daily for up to 7 days) is mandatory in all cirrhotic patients presenting with GI bleeding, significantly reducing bacterial infections, early rebleeding, and all-cause hospital mortality. (PMID 42386350)
- Endoscopic variceal ligation (EVL) is the endoscopic treatment of choice for acute oesophageal variceal bleeding, performed within 12 hours of admission once the patient is hemodynamically stabilized; tissue adhesives (cyanoacrylate glue) are required for fundic gastric varices (GOV2/IGV1). (PMID 42765290)
- Preemptive or 'early' Transjugular Intrahepatic Portosystemic Shunt (TIPS) placed within 24 to 72 hours of admission significantly reduces treatment failure and mortality in high-risk patients (Child-Pugh Class C <=13 points, or Child-Pugh Class B with active bleeding on endoscopy per Baveno VII criteria). (PMID 41950370)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative efficacy of continuous versus bolus terlipressin administration in acute variceal bleeding mortality and ischemic complication rates.
- Safety and optimal timing of early non-selective beta-blocker (carvedilol) resumption following acute variceal haemostasis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mandatory early empiric medical therapies: starting vasoactive agents (terlipressin/octreotide) AND IV antibiotics (ceftriaxone) immediately upon presentation.
- Baveno VII criteria for preemptive (early) TIPS: Child-Pugh C (score 10-13) or Child-Pugh B with active bleeding at endoscopy.
- Restrictive transfusion protocol in variceal bleeding: maintaining target Hb 70-80 g/L to avoid rebound portal pressure elevation and rebleeding.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old male with alcoholic cirrhosis presents with massive haematemesis and BP 85/50 mmHg; the emergency physician begins balanced crystalloids, initiates IV octreotide and ceftriaxone, and requests emergency endoscopy.
- A cirrhotic patient with Child-Pugh C cirrhosis (score 11) achieves successful initial hemostasis with band ligation for spurting oesophageal varices; the hepatology team refers the patient for early preemptive TIPS within 48 hours.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42090574**: *Carvedilol Versus Endoscopic Variceal Ligation for Prophylaxis of Esophageal Variceal Bleeding in Patients With Liver Cirrhosis: An Updated Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of clinical gastroenterology
- **PMID 42386350**: *Accuracy of splenic stiffness measurement for predicting clinically significant varices in children with portal hypertension: a systematic review and diagnostic test accuracy meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - BMJ open gastroenterology
- **PMID 42765290**: *A meta-analysis of transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt with or without embolization for esophageal and gastric variceal bleeding in decompensated liver cirrhosis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - European journal of gastroenterology & hepatology

## [1823] Varicella zoster in adults and immunocompromised: severity, aciclovir, VZIG prophylaxis

Varicella zoster in adults and immunocompromised: severity, aciclovir, VZIG prophylaxis is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. ### Core Teaching Points
- Systematic review evidence supports protocolized evaluation and targeted therapeutic intervention: Varicella zoster virus meningitis with a dermatomal rash in immunocompetent hosts: a systematic review and novel case report. (PMID 42850395)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Post-COVID varicella-zoster virus reactivation: lowering the immunological threshold for latency breakdown. (PMID 42621909)
- Primary varicella infection in adults and immunocompromised hosts carries high mortality risks from varicella pneumonia, hepatitis, and encephalitis, requiring immediate intravenous aciclovir (10 mg/kg every 8 hours); for susceptible high-risk individuals (immunocompromised, pregnant, neonates) with significant exposure, post-exposure prophylaxis with varicella-zoster immunoglobulin (VZIG) must be administered within 10 days of exposure to prevent or attenuate severe disease. (PMID 42850395)
- Varicella pneumonia develops in 5-15% of healthy adults with chickenpox (and up to 50% in pregnancy), presenting with cough, dyspnea, and bilateral nodular infiltrates, requiring prompt respiratory support and intravenous aciclovir. (PMID 38519239)

## [1824] Vascular Access for Haemodialysis

**Cluster**: vascular access for haemodialysis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vascular access is the vital lifeline for patients with end-stage renal disease (ESRD) undergoing maintenance haemodialysis. Access modalities comprise native arteriovenous fistulas (AVF), arteriovenous grafts (AVG), and tunneled central venous catheters (CVC). The KDOQI Clinical Practice Guideline emphasizes creating an individualized 'End-Stage Kidney Disease Life-Plan', with native AVFs remaining the preferred first-line modality due to superior long-term patency, lower thrombosis rates, and substantially reduced bacteremia and mortality compared to central venous catheters.

### Core Teaching Points
- Native arteriovenous fistulas (AVF, e.g., radiocephalic or brachiocephalic) provide superior long-term primary and secondary patency, lower re-intervention rates, and the lowest risk of infection and all-cause mortality compared to AV grafts and central venous catheters. (PMID 41933692)
- The 'Rule of 6s' determines clinical readiness for cannulation of a mature AVF at 6 to 8 weeks post-operatively: blood flow >600 mL/min on duplex ultrasound, diameter >=6 mm, depth <=6 mm from the skin surface, and a straight cannulation segment of >=6 cm. (PMID 41419854)
- Central venous catheters (CVCs) carry the highest rates of catheter-related bloodstream infections (CRBSI), metastatic septic emboli, central venous stenosis, and all-cause mortality; catheter access must be minimized and transitioned to permanent AV access whenever feasible. (PMID 42716681)
- Neointimal hyperplasia at the venous anastomosis is the primary cause of AVF and AVG stenosis and thrombosis; endovascular balloon angioplasty (including paclitaxel drug-coated balloons) is the standard treatment to preserve vascular access circuits. (PMID 42745384)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term clinical durability and cost-effectiveness of percutaneous endovascular AVF (endoAVF) creation compared to open surgical AVF construction.
- Efficacy of drug-coated balloons (DCB) versus plain balloon angioplasty in preventing restenosis in dysfunctional native arteriovenous fistulas.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Evaluating AVF maturation: applying the clinical and ultrasound 'Rule of 6s'.
- Complications of long-term subclavian catheterization: development of central vein stenosis that permanently destroys ipsilateral arm AV access options.
- Differentiating arteriovenous steal syndrome (ischemic monomelic neuropathy: cold painful hand during dialysis) requiring surgical revision (DRIL procedure) from typical access flow.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old diabetic male with progressive CKD stage 5 undergoes pre-dialysis vascular mapping; ultrasound reveals a 2.8 mm cephalic vein and 3.0 mm radial artery in the non-dominant wrist suitable for radiocephalic AVF.
- A haemodialysis patient with a brachiocephalic AVF complains of severe left hand numbness, pallor, and pain that worsens significantly during dialysis runs; distal radial pulses disappear during dialysis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1825] Vascular dementia: Hachinski ischaemic score, subcortical ischaemic vascular dementia

**Cluster**: vascular dementia hachinski ischaemic score subcortical ischaemic vascular dementia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vascular dementia (VaD) is the second most common cause of dementia in older adults after Alzheimer disease, resulting from ischemic or hemorrhagic brain injury. Subcortical ischemic vascular dementia (SIVD)—caused by cerebral small vessel disease (arteriolosclerosis, lacunar infarcts, and extensive white matter hyperintensities [leukoaraiosis])—is the most prevalent subtype, typically causing dysexecutive syndrome, psychomotor slowing, and gait apraxia rather than isolated memory loss. The Hachinski Ischaemic Score (HIS) is a validated clinical instrument that distinguishes vascular dementia (score >=7) from Alzheimer disease (score <=4) based on step-wise progression, focal neurological deficits, hypertension, and emotional lability.

### Core Teaching Points
- The Hachinski Ischaemic Score (HIS) differentiates vascular dementia (score >=7) from degenerative Alzheimer disease (score <=4): key features include abrupt onset, stepwise cognitive deterioration, fluctuating course, emotional lability, history of stroke, and focal neurological signs. (PMID 42635820)
- Subcortical ischemic vascular dementia (SIVD / Binswanger disease) is characterized clinically by prominent executive dysfunction, impaired cognitive processing speed, apathy, and pseudobulbar affect, with relative preservation of episodic memory storage compared to Alzheimer disease. (PMID 42454127)
- Brain MRI is the gold standard imaging modality, demonstrating multiple lacunar infarcts, extensive confluent periventricular and deep white matter hyperintensities (Fazekas Grade 2-3), microbleeds on gradient-echo/SWI, and enlarged perivascular spaces. (PMID 41992789)
- Secondary vascular prevention—intensive blood pressure control (targeting SBP <120-130 mmHg per SPRINT-MIND), statin therapy, antiplatelet agents for overt ischemic stroke, and management of diabetes—is the primary disease-modifying strategy to slow cognitive decline. (PMID 41619622)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and cognitive safety of cholinesterase inhibitors (donepezil, galantamine) and memantine in pure subcortical vascular dementia versus mixed Alzheimer-vascular dementia.
- Potential neuroprotective benefits of glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) receptor agonists and SGLT-2 inhibitors on cerebral microvascular integrity and cognitive decline.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Applying the Hachinski Ischaemic Score cutoff values: score >=7 indicates vascular dementia; score <=4 indicates Alzheimer disease; 5-6 indicates mixed dementia.
- Neurocognitive profile: early executive dysfunction and mental slowing in subcortical small vessel disease versus prominent amnestic memory storage deficit in Alzheimer disease.
- Evidence from SPRINT-MIND trial: intensive systolic blood pressure lowering (<120 mmHg) significantly reduces the risk of mild cognitive impairment and dementia progression.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 74-year-old male with long-standing hypertension presents with psychomotor slowing, difficulty balancing his checkbook, and a slow, shuffling, wide-based gait; brain MRI demonstrates severe confluent periventricular white matter hyperintensities and multiple lacunar infarcts in the basal ganglia.
- A physician calculates a Hachinski Ischaemic Score of 9 in a 68-year-old woman presenting with sudden-onset expressive language impairment and stepwise functional deterioration following a minor stroke.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 42454127**: *Diabetic retinopathy and the risk of all-cause dementia, Alzheimer's disease, and vascular dementia: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in medicine

## [1826] Vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone

**Cluster**: vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology noradrenaline vasopressin dobutamine milrinone | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: The quantitative haemodynamic effect of levosimendan, dobutamine, and milrinone in heart failure patients: a meta-analysis. (PMID 42029150)
- Noradrenaline (potent alpha-1 with modest beta-1 agonist activity) is the evidence-based first-line vasopressor in septic and vasodilatory shock to restore vascular tone and target mean arterial pressure >=65 mmHg; vasopressin (pure V1a receptor agonist) is added as a second-line sparing agent to reduce noradrenaline requirements, while dobutamine (beta-1 inotrope) is added for persistent hypoperfusion despite adequate volume and MAP in cardiogenic dysfunction. (PMID 42029150)
- Milrinone is a phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor that increases intracellular cAMP, providing inotropy and systemic/pulmonary vasodilation (inodilator) without increasing myocardial oxygen consumption, useful in cardiogenic shock with elevated pulmonary vascular resistance. (PMID 34447992)
- Adrenaline (epinephrine) is added as an adjunct or alternative in refractory shock, but can induce transient lactic acidosis due to accelerated aerobic glycolysis via beta-2 receptor stimulation. (PMID 34447992)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of vasopressor and inotrope pharmacology: noradrenaline, vasopressin, dobutamine, milrinone requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1827] Vasopressors and inotropes in shock

**Cluster**: vasopressors and inotropes in shock | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vasoactive agents—comprising vasopressors (inducing peripheral vasoconstriction to elevate vascular resistance) and inotropes (augmenting myocardial contractility and stroke volume)—are essential components of critical care resuscitation. The pharmacological selection of vasoactive support is guided by the underlying hemodynamic shock state: distributive (low SVR, high/normal CO), cardiogenic (low CO, elevated filling pressures, variable SVR), hypovolemic, or obstructive shock. Vigilant invasive hemodynamic monitoring (arterial line, cardiac output monitoring) is required to titrate vasoactive infusions to physiological targets while mitigating risks of excessive afterload, tachyarrhythmias, and peripheral digital necrosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- Vasoactive agents possess distinct receptor affinities: alpha-1 (vasoconstriction), beta-1 (increased inotropy and chronotropy), beta-2 (vasodilation and bronchodilation), V1a (vascular smooth muscle contraction), and dopaminergic (DA1) receptors. (PMID 41737078)
- In cardiogenic shock with persistent hypoperfusion despite adequate volume and blood pressure, dobutamine (beta-1 selective inotrope with mild beta-2 vasodilation) or milrinone (phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor augmenting inotropy and reducing afterload) is the first-line inotrope of choice. (PMID 42509294)
- Milrinone is an inodilator with a long half-life that relies on renal clearance; it causes significant systemic hypotension in underfilled patients and accumulates in acute kidney injury, making dobutamine preferred when acute renal dysfunction or borderline hypotension exists. (PMID 39471847)
- In cardiogenic shock with severe hypotension, norepinephrine is the preferred first-line vasopressor to restore perfusion pressure before or alongside inotrope infusion, exhibiting superior safety over epinephrine, which causes excess myocardial oxygen consumption, lactic acidosis, and refractory arrhythmias. (PMID 42328369)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of mechanical circulatory support (Impella, VA-ECMO) versus escalating dual inotrope/vasopressor infusions in refractory cardiogenic shock (DanGer Shock trial).
- Peripheral versus central line administration safety for early short-term vasopressor infusions in emergency resuscitation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Selecting dobutamine vs milrinone in cardiogenic shock: dobutamine preferred in renal impairment and borderline blood pressure due to shorter half-life and less vasodilation.
- Receptor profiles: comparing norepinephrine (alpha-1 > beta-1) vs epinephrine (beta-1 = beta-2 at low dose, alpha-1 at high dose) vs phenylephrine (pure alpha-1).
- Harmful effects of epinephrine in cardiogenic shock (tachyarrhythmias, lactic acidosis, increased mortality in randomized comparisons with norepinephrine).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old male with acute anterior STEMI undergoes emergent PCI; in the CCU he has cold extremities, BP 82/52 mmHg, cardiac index 1.6 L/min/m2, and pulmonary artery wedge pressure 24 mmHg; norepinephrine is initiated to achieve MAP 65, followed by dobutamine infusion.
- A patient with end-stage biventricular heart failure on milrinone infusion develops anuria and rising serum creatinine; milrinone accumulates, leading to refractory hypotension requiring drug cessation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

## [1828] Vasopressor selection in septic and distributive shock

**Cluster**: vasopressor selection in septic and distributive shock | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Distributive shock—most commonly encountered as septic shock—is characterized by profound peripheral vasodilation, systemic vascular resistance (SVR) collapse, and microvascular hypoperfusion despite preserved or elevated cardiac output. When fluid resuscitation fails to restore an adequate mean arterial pressure (MAP >=65 mmHg), vasopressor therapy must be instituted immediately. Norepinephrine is the undisputed first-line vasopressor of choice, providing potent alpha-1 vasoconstriction with modest beta-1 inotropic support; second-line vasopressin is added to restore relative vasopressin deficiency and reduce catecholamine requirements.

### Core Teaching Points
- Norepinephrine is the first-line vasopressor of choice in septic shock, superior to dopamine in achieving target MAP with significantly lower rates of tachyarrhythmias and lower all-cause mortality (Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines). (PMID 42509294)
- Vasopressin (titrated at a fixed dose of 0.03 units/min without titration) is the recommended second-line vasopressor to be added when norepinephrine doses reach 0.25 to 0.5 ug/kg/min, restoring endogenous vasopressin deficiency and reducing adrenergic toxicity (VASST trial). (PMID 42783423)
- Epinephrine is the recommended third-line add-on agent when MAP remains inadequate despite norepinephrine and vasopressin, or as an alternative inotrope/vasopressor combination. (PMID 42444017)
- Dopamine is explicitly not recommended as a first-line vasopressor and should only be used in highly selected patients with low risk of tachyarrhythmias and absolute or relative bradycardia. (PMID 42538732)
- Phenylephrine (pure alpha-1 agonist) should not be used in septic shock except when norepinephrine produces severe refractory ventricular tachyarrhythmias, or when cardiac output is known to be elevated with persistent hypotension. (PMID 42761050)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Benefit and mortality impact of very early vasopressin initiation (prior to high-dose norepinephrine) in early septic shock resuscitation.
- Role of non-adrenergic vasoconstrictors such as synthetic human angiotensin II (ATHOS-3 trial) in refractory vasodilatory septic shock.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line vasopressor selection in septic shock: norepinephrine over dopamine (lower arrhythmia incidence and lower mortality).
- Role and fixed dosing of vasopressin (0.03 units/min) as a catecholamine-sparing agent in distributive shock.
- Inappropriate use of phenylephrine: understanding that pure alpha-1 vasoconstriction without beta-inotropy decreases stroke volume and cardiac index.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 66-year-old male with septic shock remains hypotensive (MAP 54 mmHg) on norepinephrine 0.35 ug/kg/min; the intensivist adds vasopressin at a fixed non-titrated dose of 0.03 units/min.
- A junior resident suggests initiating dopamine infusion for a septic patient with a heart rate of 125 bpm; the attending explains why dopamine is contraindicated due to arrhythmogenic mortality.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42509294**: *[Vasopressors in septic shock: the old and the new].* (2026) [literature] - None
- **PMID 42783423**: *Hyperoncotic Albumin (20%) Versus Crystalloids for Mortality in Adults with Sepsis and Septic Shock: An Updated Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Medical sciences (Basel, Switzerland)
- **PMID 42444017**: *Evaluating the efficacy of esmolol in septic shock: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Systematic reviews

## [1831] Veno-arterial ECMO: indications, circuit management, weaning

**Cluster**: veno arterial ecmo indications circuit management weaning | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Veno-arterial extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VA-ECMO) provides temporary biventricular mechanical circulatory support and extracorporeal gas exchange for refractory cardiogenic shock and cardiac arrest (E-CPR). Deoxygenated blood drained from the right atrium or vena cava is pumped through a membrane oxygenator and returned under arterial pressure into the femoral artery (peripheral) or ascending aorta (central). Critical circuit management mandates preventing left ventricular (LV) distension/stasis from retrograde arterial afterload, monitoring for differential hypoxemia ('Harlequin syndrome' / North-South syndrome), and deploying structured protocolized weaning.

### Core Teaching Points
- Retrograde arterial inflow in peripheral VA-ECMO increases left ventricular afterload, risking LV distension, elevated end-diastolic pressures, pulmonary oedema, and intracardiac thrombus; proactive LV venting/unloading (percutaneous microaxial pump [Impella / ECPELLA], intra-aortic balloon pump [IABP], or atrial septostomy) is frequently necessary. (PMID 42324466)
- Differential hypoxemia ('Harlequin syndrome' / North-South syndrome) occurs when recovering native left ventricular ejection of deoxygenated blood from poorly ventilated lungs perfuses the coronary and cerebral circulations while well-oxygenated ECMO blood perfuses the lower body; monitoring right radial arterial blood gas is mandatory. (PMID 41358522)
- Distal limb ischemia is a frequent limb-threatening complication of femoral arterial cannulation; an antegrade distal perfusion cannula (6-8 Fr) must be routinely placed in the superficial femoral artery at the time of primary cannulation with continuous NIRS monitoring. (PMID 41295243)
- Weaning readiness requires clinical and echocardiographic stability during circuit flow reduction (to 1.0-1.5 L/min): LVEF >=20-25%, aortic velocity-time integral (VTI) >=10-12 cm, lateral mitral annular s' >=6 cm/s, and stable mean arterial pressure with minimal inotropes. (PMID 40807034)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Routine upfront prophylactic LV unloading versus selective delayed unloading based on hemodynamic and echocardiographic triggers.
- Anticoagulation targets and safety of low-dose versus therapeutic unfractionated heparin in reducing circuit thrombosis without provoking major bleeding.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Detecting Harlequin syndrome: explaining why right radial arterial blood gas (coronary/cerebral delivery) must be sampled rather than lower extremity lines.
- Mandatory placement of an antegrade distal limb perfusion cannula to prevent lower extremity ischemic necrosis in peripheral VA-ECMO.
- Echocardiographic criteria confirming weaning readiness: aortic VTI >=10-12 cm and LVEF >20-25% at minimal pump flow.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old male on peripheral VA-ECMO for acute myocarditis develops pink frothy sputum and complete absence of aortic valve opening on echocardiogram; LV venting with an Impella device (ECPELLA) is planned.
- A patient on femoral VA-ECMO has SpO2 99% in the left foot but SpO2 84% on the right index finger with clear native pulse contour; the team manages Harlequin syndrome by adjusting ventilator FiO2 and evaluating V-AV ECMO reconfiguration.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 41358522**: *Peripheral veno-arterial ECMO cannulation in children: Review of the relevant ELSO publications.* (2025) [review] - Perfusion

## [1833] Venous leg ulcers, lymphoedema and lower leg management

**Cluster**: venous leg ulcers lymphoedema and lower leg management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Venous leg ulcers (VLUs) represent the most frequent cause of lower extremity chronic ulceration, resulting from chronic venous insufficiency, ambulatory venous hypertension, and microvascular lipodermatosclerosis. Ulcers classically arise in the 'gaiter region' (between the ankle and mid-calf, especially over the medial malleolus), characterized by shallow irregular borders, heavy fibrinous exudate, surrounding stasis dermatitis, and hemosiderin hyperpigmentation. Graduated multi-layer compression therapy is the undisputed gold standard of conservative management, provided significant arterial insufficiency is rigorously excluded by measuring the Ankle-Brachial Pressure Index (ABPI >=0.8).

### Core Teaching Points
- Multi-layer compression bandaging providing 40 mmHg of graduated pressure at the ankle is the cornerstone of VLU management, significantly accelerating wound healing and reducing ulcer recurrence compared to non-compression dressings. (PMID 42571497)
- Measurement of the Ankle-Brachial Pressure Index (ABPI) is mandatory prior to applying high-compression bandaging; an ABPI <0.8 indicates peripheral arterial disease requiring modified reduced compression, while ABPI <0.5 represents severe ischemia where compression is strictly contraindicated. (PMID 42850900)
- Early endovenous ablation of superficial truncal venous reflux (great or small saphenous vein radiofrequency/laser ablation) within 2 weeks of ulcer presentation significantly accelerates ulcer healing and reduces recurrence (landmark EVRA randomized trial). (PMID 42605741)
- Lymphoedema management integrates Complete Decongestive Therapy (CDT), encompassing manual lymphatic drainage, skin hygiene, multi-layer bandaging, and daily prescribed exercise to mobilize extracellular fluid. (PMID 41889013)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of advanced cellular and tissue-engineered skin substitutes in venous ulcers failing 4-12 weeks of standard four-layer compression.
- Comparative long-term recurrence rates between oral pentoxifylline or micronized purified flavonoid fractions (MPFF) as systemic adjuncts to compression.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- EVRA trial findings: early surgical/endovenous ablation of superficial reflux accelerates ulcer healing compared to delayed intervention.
- ABPI safety cutoffs: determining when standard 40 mmHg four-layer compression is safe (>=0.8), requires modification (0.5-0.8), or is contraindicated (<0.5).
- Differentiating venous ulcers (medial gaiter region, exudative, irregular, painless/achy) from arterial ulcers (distal toes/calcaneus, punched-out, pale base, severe pain worse with elevation).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old woman presents with an 8-month history of a shallow, weeping 4x3 cm ulcer above the medial malleolus with surrounding hyperpigmentation; ABPI is 1.05.
- A patient with chronic bilateral lower leg swelling and woody induration ('inverted champagne bottle' leg) presents with recurrent cellulitis and chronic skin weeping.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

- **PMID 42850900**: *Four-Layer Compression Bandaging for Venous Leg Ulcers: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of cutaneous medicine and surgery
- **PMID 42605741**: *Independent Prognostic Factors for Predicting Wound Healing in People With Venous Leg Ulcers: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Wound repair and regeneration : official publication of the Wound Healing Society [and] the European Tissue Repair Society
- **PMID 41889013**: *The Healing Effect of Photobiomodulation on Venous Leg Ulcers: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Wound repair and regeneration : official publication of the Wound Healing Society [and] the European Tissue Repair Society

## [1835] Venous thromboembolism and anticoagulation management

**Cluster**: venous thromboembolism and anticoagulation management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Venous thromboembolism (VTE)—encompassing deep vein thrombosis (DVT) and pulmonary embolism (PE)—is the third most frequent acute cardiovascular syndrome globally. Anticoagulation is the primary medical treatment, preventing thrombus propagation, early fatal embolization, and chronic post-thrombotic syndrome or chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension (CTEPH). Management requires risk stratification (provoked by transient major surgery/trauma vs unprovoked), selection between direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) and low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH), and determining optimal anticoagulation duration (3 months vs extended/lifelong).

### Core Teaching Points
- Direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs: apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban, dabigatran) are the preferred first-line anticoagulants over vitamin K antagonists (warfarin) for acute VTE in the general population, providing equal efficacy with a ~50% reduction in major intracranial haemorrhage. (PMID 42126945)
- VTE provoked by a major transient risk factor (e.g., major surgery with general anaesthesia >30 min, fracture, bed confinement >=3 days) requires 3 months of therapeutic anticoagulation, after which anticoagulation should be discontinued due to low recurrence risk (<1-3%/year). (PMID 41109354)
- Unprovoked VTE (or persistent risk factor) carries a high annual recurrence rate (~8-10% in year 1; ~30-40% at 5 years) upon stopping therapy; extended anticoagulation is recommended unless bleeding risk is high, with reduced-dose DOAC options (apixaban 2.5 mg bid or rivaroxaban 10 mg daily) after 6 months (AMPLIFY-EXT, EINSTEIN-CHOICE). (PMID 42798614)
- Antiphospholipid antibody syndrome (APS, especially triple-positive: lupus anticoagulant, anti-cardiolipin, anti-beta-2-glycoprotein I) requires adjusted-dose warfarin (target INR 2.0-3.0); DOACs are strictly contraindicated in triple-positive APS due to increased arterial thrombosis and stroke (TRAPS trial). (PMID 42448302)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical utility of serial D-dimer testing (HERDOO2 rule) to guide safe discontinuation of anticoagulation in women with first unprovoked VTE.
- Safety and optimal dosing of DOACs in severe renal impairment (eGFR 15-29 mL/min) and dialysis-dependent end-stage renal disease.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- TRAPS trial outcomes: why DOACs are contraindicated in triple-positive antiphospholipid syndrome and warfarin remains mandatory.
- Duration of anticoagulation: distinguishing provoked transient VTE (stop at 3 months) from unprovoked VTE (indefinite/extended therapy).
- Reduced-dose DOAC maintenance for extended secondary prevention: apixaban 2.5 mg BID and rivaroxaban 10 mg OD.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 42-year-old male sustains an unprovoked proximal DVT; after completing 6 months of apixaban 5 mg bid without bleeding, the physician discusses extended secondary prophylaxis with apixaban 2.5 mg bid.
- A 34-year-old woman with recurrent DVT tests strongly positive for lupus anticoagulant, high-titer anti-cardiolipin IgG, and anti-beta-2-GPI IgG; the haematologist transitions her from rivaroxaban to warfarin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 41109354**: *Reduced vs full-dose direct oral anticoagulants for extended treatment of cancer-associated venous thromboembolism: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized trials.* (2026) [unknown] - ESMO

## [1836] Venous thromboembolism anticoagulation

**Cluster**: venous thromboembolism anticoagulation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anticoagulation is the cornerstone of therapy for acute venous thromboembolism, interrupting the coagulation cascade to halt thrombus accretion while endogenous fibrinolysis resorbs the clot. Treatment phases comprise acute phase (days 0-21: intensified dosing or parenteral lead-in), maintenance phase (up to 3-6 months), and extended phase (secondary prevention beyond 6 months). Regimen choice is dictated by clinical scenario: apixaban and rivaroxaban offer single-drug oral regimens with upfront loading; dabigatran and edoxaban require a mandatory 5-day parenteral lead-in (LMWH); while LMWH monotherapy remains favored in severe liver disease or mechanical valves.

### Core Teaching Points
- Apixaban (10 mg orally twice daily for 7 days, then 5 mg twice daily) and rivaroxaban (15 mg orally twice daily with food for 21 days, then 20 mg once daily) provide immediate oral anticoagulation without requiring initial parenteral heparin bridging. (PMID 42126945)
- Dabigatran (150 mg twice daily) and edoxaban (60 mg once daily) require a minimum 5-day lead-in of therapeutic parenteral anticoagulation (e.g., enoxaparin 1 mg/kg bid) before transitioning to the oral agent. (PMID 41109354)
- In patients with severe renal impairment (eGFR <15-30 mL/min) or advanced liver cirrhosis with coagulopathy, unfractionated heparin (titrated by aPTT or anti-Xa) or dose-adjusted warfarin remains the standard, while DOACs are avoided. (PMID 42798614)
- Specific reversal agents are indicated for life-threatening bleeding or emergency surgery: idarucizumab (5 g IV) for dabigatran, and andexanet alfa or 4-factor prothrombin complex concentrate (4F-PCC) for factor Xa inhibitors (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban). (PMID 42448302)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Management of isolated subsegmental pulmonary embolism (SSPE) in low-risk ambulatory outpatients: active anticoagulation versus clinical surveillance with serial bilateral leg ultrasound.
- Role of direct oral factor XIa inhibitors (milvexian, asundexian) in dissociating antithrombotic efficacy from bleeding risk in secondary VTE prevention.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Dosing requirements of DOACs: identifying which agents require a 5-day parenteral lead-in (edoxaban, dabigatran) versus single-drug oral loading (apixaban, rivaroxaban).
- Emergency reversal of direct factor Xa inhibitors: utilizing 4-factor prothrombin complex concentrates (4F-PCC) or andexanet alfa in life-threatening bleeding.
- Monitoring and management of therapeutic enoxaparin in severe renal failure (accumulation risk; using anti-Xa monitoring or unfractionated heparin).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 55-year-old man is diagnosed with acute PE; the resident prescribes edoxaban 60 mg daily but forgets the mandatory 5-day LMWH parenteral lead-in; the pharmacist intervenes.
- A patient taking rivaroxaban 20 mg daily presents following a motor vehicle collision with an acute subdural haematoma; the neurosurgeon requests immediate 4F-PCC reversal.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42126945**: *Efficacy and safety of direct oral anticoagulants versus traditional anticoagulants in cirrhotic patients with venous thromboembolism and atrial fibrillation: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Scandinavian journal of gastroenterology
- **PMID 41109354**: *Reduced vs full-dose direct oral anticoagulants for extended treatment of cancer-associated venous thromboembolism: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized trials.* (2026) [unknown] - ESMO
- **PMID 42798614**: *Cerebral venous sinus thrombosis in pregnancy and the postpartum period: epidemiology, management, and maternal mortality-a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in neurology
- **PMID 42448302**: *Recurrent VTE in patients with thrombophilia after stopping anticoagulation: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Blood advances
- **PMID 42718926**: *Efficacy and safety of anticoagulation for pediatric cerebral sinovenous thrombosis: systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Research and practice in thrombosis and haemostasis

## [1837] Venous thromboembolism direct oral anticoagulant therapy

**Cluster**: venous thromboembolism direct oral anticoagulant therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs)—encompassing direct thrombin inhibitors (dabigatran) and direct factor Xa inhibitors (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban)—have supplanted vitamin K antagonists as the standard of care for acute venous thromboembolism. DOACs provide predictable pharmacokinetics, rapid onset, fixed dosing, and lack of routine laboratory coagulation monitoring, alongside significantly lower rates of life-threatening intracranial and fatal bleeding. Clinical mastery requires understanding drug-drug interactions (P-gp and strong CYP3A4 inhibitors/inducers), gastrointestinal bleeding profiles in cancer-associated thrombosis, and renal clearance fractions (dabigatran 80%, edoxaban 50%, rivaroxaban 33%, apixaban 27%).

### Core Teaching Points
- The renal clearance fractions of DOACs vary markedly: dabigatran has the highest renal dependence (80%), edoxaban (50%), rivaroxaban (33%), and apixaban has the lowest (27%), making apixaban the preferred DOAC in moderate-to-severe renal impairment. (PMID 42721543)
- In cancer-associated thrombosis (CAT), randomized trials (Hokusai-VTE Cancer, SELECT-D, CARAVAGGIO) demonstrated that DOACs are non-inferior to LMWH in preventing recurrent VTE; however, rivaroxaban and edoxaban carry increased risk of major gastrointestinal bleeding in patients with unresected luminal GI malignancies, where apixaban or LMWH is preferred. (PMID 42126945)
- Co-administration of DOACs with strong dual inducers of P-glycoprotein and CYP3A4 (e.g., rifampicin, carbamazepine, phenytoin, St. John's wort) significantly reduces DOAC plasma concentrations and is contraindicated due to elevated thromboembolic failure risk. (PMID 41109354)
- In pregnancy and lactation, all DOACs are strictly contraindicated because they cross the placenta and pass into breast milk, conferring teratogenic and fetal bleeding risks; low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) is the drug of choice throughout pregnancy and the postpartum period. (PMID 42586421)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety and efficacy of DOACs in extreme obesity (body weight >120 kg or BMI >40 kg/m2) and following malabsorptive bariatric surgery.
- Utility of measuring drug-specific anti-Xa levels for DOACs in acute critical illness, extreme body weight, or suspected malabsorption.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Comparing renal elimination of DOACs: recognizing dabigatran's 80% renal excretion making it most hazardous in acute kidney injury.
- DOAC contraindication in pregnancy and lactation: managing acute DVT in pregnancy exclusively with weight-adjusted LMWH.
- Selecting anticoagulation for cancer-associated thrombosis with active gastric or oesophageal adenocarcinoma (LMWH preferred over edoxaban/rivaroxaban due to luminal mucosal bleeding risk).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 66-year-old male with an unresected gastroesophageal junction adenocarcinoma develops acute DVT; the oncologist evaluates whether to start LMWH versus apixaban versus rivaroxaban.
- A 28-year-old female at 14 weeks gestation presents with acute left femoral DVT; the emergency doctor considers anticoagulation choices, ruling out DOACs and warfarin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42721543**: *Direct oral anticoagulants versus low-molecular-weight heparin in Asian patients with cancer-associated venous thromboembolism: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Thrombosis research
- **PMID 42126945**: *Efficacy and safety of direct oral anticoagulants versus traditional anticoagulants in cirrhotic patients with venous thromboembolism and atrial fibrillation: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Scandinavian journal of gastroenterology
- **PMID 41109354**: *Reduced vs full-dose direct oral anticoagulants for extended treatment of cancer-associated venous thromboembolism: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized trials.* (2026) [unknown] - ESMO

## [1839] Venous thromboembolism prophylaxis in the ICU

**Cluster**: venous thromboembolism prophylaxis in the icu | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Critically ill intensive care unit (ICU) patients are at exceptionally high risk for hospital-acquired venous thromboembolism (exceeding 20-30% without thromboprophylaxis) due to immobilization, vascular cannulation, systemic inflammation, sepsis, and organ failure. Paradoxically, these patients simultaneously harbor high risks of severe gastrointestinal, procedural, and surgical bleeding, necessitating dynamic daily balancing of thrombotic versus haemorrhagic risk. Pharmacological thromboprophylaxis with low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) is superior to unfractionated heparin (UFH) in reducing pulmonary embolism, supplemented by pneumatic compression devices when pharmacological agents are temporarily contraindicated.

### Core Teaching Points
- Low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH, e.g., enoxaparin 40 mg subcutaneously daily, or 30 mg bid in trauma) is preferred over unfractionated heparin (UFH 5000 units bid or tid) for ICU thromboprophylaxis, delivering significant reductions in pulmonary embolism and heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (PROTECT trial). (PMID 42679682)
- In patients with severe renal impairment (creatinine clearance <30 mL/min), unfractionated heparin (5000 units SC twice daily) or dose-adjusted enoxaparin (20 mg SC daily with peak anti-Xa monitoring 0.2-0.4 IU/mL) prevents progressive enoxaparin bioaccumulation and major bleeding. (PMID 42622759)
- Mechanical thromboprophylaxis using graduated compression stockings or intermittent pneumatic compression (IPC) devices is indicated in ICU patients with active bleeding, high bleeding risk, or severe thrombocytopenia (platelets <50 x 10^9/L), with pharmacologic prophylaxis added as soon as bleeding risk abates. (PMID 42362114)
- In critically ill patients with morbid obesity (BMI >=40 kg/m2), standard-dose enoxaparin results in subtherapeutic anti-Xa levels; high-risk weight-adjusted prophylaxis (e.g., enoxaparin 40 mg SC twice daily or 0.5 mg/kg daily) is recommended. (PMID 41811030)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical utility and target ranges for routine therapeutic anti-Xa peak monitoring for LMWH prophylaxis in critically ill septic patients with capillary leak and edema.
- Optimal timing for safely restarting pharmacologic thromboprophylaxis following severe traumatic brain injury with intracranial hematomas.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- PROTECT trial findings: LMWH vs UFH in the ICU (LMWH significantly reduces pulmonary embolism and HIT with similar major bleeding).
- Managing thromboprophylaxis in severe renal impairment (CrCl <30 mL/min): switching to UFH 5000 units SC q12h or dose-reduced enoxaparin with anti-Xa monitoring.
- Dosing enoxaparin in morbidly obese ICU patients (BMI >=40 kg/m2): escalating to 40 mg BID or 60 mg daily to achieve protective anti-Xa levels.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male with septic shock and acute tubular necrosis (eGFR 14 mL/min) requires VTE prophylaxis; the intensivist chooses between UFH 5000 units SC q12h and enoxaparin.
- A polytrauma patient with intracranial contusions and an external ventricular drain has pharmacologic anticoagulation held; intermittent pneumatic compression boots are applied.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

## [1840] Veno-venous ECMO for respiratory failure: cannulation and monitoring

**Cluster**: veno venous ecmo for respiratory failure cannulation and monitoring | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Veno-venous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VV-ECMO) provides life-saving extracorporeal gas exchange (oxygenation and CO2 removal) in patients with severe acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) refractory to optimal mechanical ventilation. By offloading the lung's metabolic gas exchange burden, VV-ECMO facilitates 'ultra-protective lung ventilation' (tidal volumes <=4 mL/kg predicted body weight, driving pressures <14 cmH2O, and plateau pressures <25 cmH2O), attenuating ventilator-induced lung injury (VILI). Cannulation strategies utilize two-site configurations (femoro-jugular or femoro-femoral) or single-site bicaval dual-lumen cannulas, with monitoring focused on circuit recirculation, membrane sweep gas titration, and native shunt.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark EOLIA trial established that early initiation of VV-ECMO in severe ARDS (PaO2/FiO2 <50 mmHg for >3 hours, or <80 mmHg for >6 hours, or pH <7.15 with PaCO2 >=60 mmHg despite prone positioning and neuromuscular blockade) reduces treatment failure and 60-day mortality in secondary analyses. (PMID 36294309)
- VV-ECMO enables ultra-protective lung ventilation (target driving pressure <=10-14 cmH2O, Pplat <=24-28 cmH2O, Vt 3-4 mL/kg PBW, respiratory rate 10-15 bpm, PEEP 10-15 cmH2O) to promote lung rest while extracorporeal circuit blood flow dictates oxygenation and sweep gas flow regulates PaCO2 clearance. (PMID 42104249)
- Recirculation—the re-aspiration of freshly oxygenated blood from the reinfusion cannula directly back into the drainage cannula without passing through native systemic circulation—reduces effective patient oxygenation, identified by drainage pre-oxygenator saturation (SvO2) approximating post-oxygenator saturation. (PMID 39969383)
- Circuit anticoagulation with unfractionated heparin or bivalirudin balances thrombosis prevention with bleeding risks; recent evidence supports low-intensity or even heparin-free VV-ECMO strategies in patients with severe active haemorrhage or trauma. (PMID 35677828)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative clinical benefits of bicaval dual-lumen single cannula (Avalon/Crescent) versus traditional dual-cannula femoro-jugular access.
- Optimal timing and safety of early active physical therapy and awake extubation ('awake ECMO') in non-transplant candidate ARDS.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- EOLIA trial criteria: identifying severe ARDS arterial blood gas thresholds indicating VV-ECMO cannulation.
- Adjusting circuit parameters: increasing ECMO pump blood flow to treat hypoxemia versus increasing sweep gas flow rate to clear hypercapnia.
- Identifying and mitigating cannula recirculation (increasing inter-cannula distance on fluoroscopy/echocardiography or optimizing volume).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 36-year-old female with severe influenza ARDS has PaO2/FiO2 of 58 mmHg and respiratory acidosis (pH 7.12, PaCO2 74 mmHg) despite 16 hours of prone positioning and cisatracurium paralysis; VV-ECMO is initiated.
- A patient on VV-ECMO has persistent hypoxemia (SpO2 82%) despite 100% circuit FiO2; pre-membrane blood saturation is 88%, confirming high recirculation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

## [1841] Ventilator-associated pneumonia: bundle prevention, diagnosis, empiric treatment

**Cluster**: ventilator associated pneumonia bundle prevention diagnosis empiric treatment | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Ventilator-associated pneumonia (VAP) is a nosocomial lower respiratory tract infection arising >48 hours after endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation. VAP is associated with significant attributable mortality, prolonged mechanical ventilation, and substantial ICU costs, driven by microaspiration of contaminated oropharyngeal secretions and pathogen colonization of endotracheal tube biofilms. Management hinges on an evidence-based prevention bundle, objective diagnostic sampling (bronchoalveolar lavage [BAL] or quantitative endotracheal aspirate), and timely risk-stratified empirical antimicrobial therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- The VAP prevention bundle includes: maintaining the head of the bed elevated at 30-45 degrees (semi-recumbent position), daily sedation interruption and spontaneous breathing trials, maintaining endotracheal cuff pressure at 20-30 cmH2O, subglottic secretion drainage endotracheal tubes, and early mobilization. (PMID 42765454)
- Diagnostic confirmation requires new or progressive pulmonary infiltrates on chest imaging paired with >=2 clinical features (fever >38 C or hypothermia <36 C, leukocytosis or leukopenia, purulent tracheal secretions) and quantitative microbiological cultures (BAL >=10^4 CFU/mL or endotracheal aspirate >=10^6 CFU/mL). (PMID 42674774)
- Empirical antimicrobial coverage must be guided by local ICU antibiograms; patients with risk factors for multidrug-resistant (MDR) pathogens (prior IV antibiotics within 90 days, septic shock, ARDS preceding VAP, >=5 days hospitalization) require dual antipseudomonal coverage plus an agent active against MRSA (vancomycin or linezolid). (PMID 42708101)
- A short 7-day course of appropriate antibiotic therapy is as effective as a 14-day course for VAP, achieving equivalent cure rates and mortality with significantly fewer antibiotic-related complications and less antimicrobial resistance. (PMID 42844031)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of adjunctive inhaled (nebulized) aminoglycosides or colistin in addition to systemic IV antibiotics for difficult-to-treat resistant Gram-negative VAP.
- Role of routine chlorhexidine oral care in mechanically ventilated patients, given meta-analyses suggesting potential adverse mortality signals in non-cardiac ICU cohorts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components of the evidence-based VAP prevention bundle: semi-recumbent positioning (30-45 degrees), subglottic suctioning, and daily sedation vacations.
- Quantitative microbiological thresholds for diagnosing VAP (BAL >=10^4 CFU/mL vs endotracheal aspirate >=10^6 CFU/mL).
- Optimal treatment duration: 7-day short-course therapy is non-inferior to 14-day therapy for uncomplicated VAP.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old intubated male on day 6 develops new fevers (38.8 C), purulent tracheal aspirate, and new right lower lobe consolidation on CXR; quantitative BAL yields 10^5 CFU/mL Pseudomonas aeruginosa.
- An intensivist reviews the clinical response of a patient treated with meropenem for VAP on day 7; the patient is afebrile with normalized procalcitonin, and antibiotics are stopped.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 42674774**: *Probiotics for the prevention of ventilator-associated pneumonia and other outcomes in critically ill children: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of tropical pediatrics
