## [1842] Ventilator-induced lung injury: volutrauma, barotrauma, atelectrauma

**Cluster**: ventilator induced lung injury volutrauma barotrauma atelectrauma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Ventilator-induced lung injury (VILI) describes iatrogenic parenchymal lung injury and systemic inflammatory cascade provoked by mechanical ventilation in vulnerable lungs. The classical pathophysiological mechanisms comprise volutrauma (alveolar overdistension from excessive tidal volumes), barotrauma (gross air leaks—pneumothorax, pneumomediastinum—from high transpulmonary pressures), atelectrauma (shear stress from cyclic opening and collapse of unstable alveoli), and biotrauma (translocation of inflammatory cytokines into the systemic circulation). Lung-protective ventilation—pioneered by the landmark ARDS Network ARMA trial—remains the foundation of modern critical care.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ARMA trial established that lung-protective ventilation using low tidal volumes (6 mL/kg of predicted body weight [PBW], NOT actual body weight) and plateau pressure limit <=30 cmH2O reduces mortality in ARDS from 40% to 31% compared to traditional 12 mL/kg volumes. (PMID 41270941)
- Driving pressure (Delta P = Plateau Pressure - PEEP, reflecting the ratio of tidal volume to respiratory system compliance: Vt / Crs) is the strongest physiological variable correlated with survival in ARDS; targeting driving pressure <=14-15 cmH2O significantly reduces VILI. (PMID 42735803)
- Mechanical power (the energy delivered to the respiratory system per minute, determined by tidal volume, driving pressure, flow rate, and respiratory rate) integrates all physical variables of VILI; mechanical power >12-17 J/min is associated with ventilator-induced damage. (PMID 41648056)
- Prone positioning for >=16 hours daily in moderate-to-severe ARDS (PaO2/FiO2 <150 mmHg) improves survival (PROSEVA trial) by homogenizing pleural pressure gradients, reducing alveolar shear stress, and unloading the right ventricle. (PMID 41868223)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and clinical utility of titration of positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) guided by esophageal balloon transpulmonary pressure monitoring (EPVent-2 trial).
- Individualized mechanical power limits across different patient age groups and baseline lung compliance.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ARMA trial landmark principles: calculating tidal volumes based on PREDICTED body weight (PBW, using height and sex) rather than actual measured body weight.
- Calculating driving pressure (Plateau pressure minus PEEP) and understanding its target threshold (<=14 cmH2O).
- PROSEVA trial criteria for prone positioning in severe ARDS: PaO2/FiO2 <150 mmHg, PEEP >=10 cmH2O, FiO2 >=0.6, maintained for >=16 consecutive hours.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 180 cm male weighing 120 kg (predicted body weight 75 kg) is admitted with ARDS; the resident calculates initial tidal volume at 6 mL/kg PBW (450 mL), preventing catastrophic volutrauma.
- A ventilated ARDS patient has a plateau pressure of 28 cmH2O and PEEP of 16 cmH2O (driving pressure = 12 cmH2O); the team evaluates whether lung-protective thresholds are met.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41270941**: *Mechanical power in mechanical ventilation and its association with ventilator-induced lung injury: A systematic review.* (2025) [systematic review] - Respiratory medicine
- **PMID 42735803**: *Mechanisms related to increased susceptibility of the elderly lung to ventilator-induced lung injury: A scoping review.* (2026) [review] - Experimental gerontology
- **PMID 41648056**: *Ventilator-Induced Lung Injury: Mechanotransduction and Potential Therapeutic Targets.* (2026) [review] - MedComm

## [1843] Ventilator weaning and spontaneous breathing trials

**Cluster**: ventilator weaning and spontaneous breathing trials | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Weaning from invasive mechanical ventilation represents the structured process of transitioning patients to unassisted spontaneous breathing, accounting for up to 40-50% of total ventilator duration. Both premature extubation (leading to emergent re-intubation, aspiration, and increased mortality) and delayed extubation (causing ventilator-associated pneumonia, airway trauma, and muscle atrophy) carry substantial clinical hazards. Weaning requires daily protocolized screening for readiness, coupled with a spontaneous breathing trial (SBT) using pressure support ventilation or T-piece, and objective assessment of airway patency and protection before extubation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Daily readiness testing for spontaneous breathing trials (SBT) requires: resolution/improvement of primary respiratory failure, adequate oxygenation (PaO2/FiO2 >=150-200 with PEEP <=5-8 cmH2O and FiO2 <=0.4-0.5), hemodynamic stability without high-dose vasopressors, and spontaneous respiratory drive with adequate conscious level. (PMID 42561371)
- A Spontaneous Breathing Trial (SBT) performed using low-level pressure support (PSV 5-8 cmH2O with PEEP 0-5 cmH2O) for 30 minutes results in higher extubation success and lower post-extubation reintubation rates compared to T-piece trials in modern randomized clinical trials. (PMID 42379753)
- The Rapid Shallow Breathing Index (RSBI = Respiratory Rate [bpm] / Tidal Volume [L]) measured during unassisted breathing predicts weaning success; an RSBI <105 breaths/min/L strongly predicts successful extubation, whereas RSBI >105 predicts weaning failure. (PMID 42576250)
- Prior to extubation, patients must demonstrate adequate cough strength, minimal secretions, and a positive endotracheal tube 'cuff leak test' (verifying peritubal air leak when the cuff is deflated to exclude life-threatening post-extubation laryngeal stridor/edema). (PMID 42186936)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Predictive accuracy of point-of-care diaphragmatic ultrasound (diaphragmatic excursion and thickening fraction) over traditional clinical weaning indices.
- Prophylactic high-flow nasal cannula (HFNC) versus non-invasive ventilation (NIV) immediately post-extubation in patients at high risk of extubation failure.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Calculating the Rapid Shallow Breathing Index (RSBI = RR / Vt in liters) and applying the <105 threshold for extubation readiness.
- Comparing SBT techniques: pressure support (5-8 cmH2O) yielding higher extubation success than T-piece trials.
- Evaluating a negative cuff leak test: administering systemic IV corticosteroids (e.g., methylprednisolone 20 mg q4h for 4 doses) prior to planned extubation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old male ventilated for 5 days for pneumonia meets readiness criteria; during a 30-minute PSV SBT, his RR is 18 bpm and Vt is 450 mL (RSBI = 18 / 0.45 = 40), predicting extubation success.
- A 45-year-old female intubated for 7 days has zero audible cuff leak upon endotracheal tube cuff deflation; the team plans IV steroid administration and repeat assessment.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42561371**: *Effect of a short period of invasive mechanical ventilation following a successful spontaneous breathing trial in adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Critical care science
- **PMID 42379753**: *Ventilator-derived &lt;i&gt;Versus&lt;/i&gt; Standard Rapid Shallow Breathing Index for Weaning Assessment: A Systematic Review.* (2026) [systematic review] - In vivo (Athens, Greece)
- **PMID 42576250**: *Predictive value of diaphragmatic ultrasound for weaning outcomes in mechanically ventilated patients with acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Critical care (London, England)
- **PMID 42186936**: *Protocolized Weaning From Mechanical Ventilation in the PICU: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Pediatric pulmonology

## [1844] Ventricular tachycardia catheter ablation

**Cluster**: ventricular tachycardia catheter ablation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Ventricular tachycardia (VT) in structural heart disease—most commonly ischemic cardiomyopathy post-myocardial infarction or non-ischemic dilated cardiomyopathy—is predominantly driven by scar-related macro-reentry. Implantable cardioverter-defibrillators (ICDs) prevent sudden cardiac death through antitachycardia pacing (ATP) or shock delivery, but recurrent ICD shocks cause severe psychological distress, myocardial injury, and increased mortality. Catheter ablation—utilizing 3D electroanatomic mapping to identify scar borders, low-voltage areas, and critical diastolic isthmus channels—significantly reduces recurrent VT episodes, ICD shocks, and hospitalizations.

### Core Teaching Points
- In patients with ischemic cardiomyopathy and an ICD who experience recurrent VT despite antiarrhythmic drugs (or antiarrhythmic drug intolerance), catheter ablation significantly reduces recurrent VT shocks, storm, and cardiovascular hospitalizations (VANISH trial). (PMID 42116319)
- Substrate-based catheter ablation targets electrophysiological properties of the scar during stable sinus rhythm (late potentials, local abnormal ventricular activities [LAVA], and pace-mapping of conducting channels), eliminating the need for prolonged hemodynamic instability from induced VT mapping. (PMID 38397868)
- Non-ischemic cardiomyopathy VT substrates frequently reside in the mid-myocardial or epicardial layers, frequently requiring combined endocardial and percutaneous subxiphoid epicardial mapping and ablation approaches. (PMID 38087147)
- Preemptive mechanical circulatory support (e.g., Impella percutaneous LV assist device) during catheter ablation allows mapping of hemodynamically unstable VT circuits and reduces procedural decompensation and 30-day mortality in high-risk patients. (PMID 42771085)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- First-line early VT ablation at the time of initial ICD implantation versus waiting for first shock recurrence (PARTITA and PAUSE-SCD trials).
- Role of stereotactic body radiation therapy (SBRT / cardiac radioablation) in treating refractory VT circuits inaccessible to catheter ablation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- VANISH trial landmark outcomes: superiority of VT catheter ablation over escalated antiarrhythmic drug therapy (amiodarone/mexiletine) in ischemic cardiomyopathy with recurrent ICD shocks.
- Substrate mapping techniques: identifying late potentials and LAVA in dense scar tissue without inducing sustained unstable VT.
- Indications for epicardial subxiphoid access in non-ischemic dilated cardiomyopathy and arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy (ARVC).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 66-year-old male with prior anterior MI and an ICD experiences three appropriate shocks in 24 hours for sustained monomorphic VT despite amiodarone therapy; he is transferred for emergent catheter ablation.
- An electrophysiologist maps a post-infarct VT circuit; 3D voltage mapping reveals an extensive basal inferior scar with isolated diastolic potentials that match the clinical VT morphology during pace mapping.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42116319**: *Early catheter ablation as first-line therapy for ventricular tachycardia due to structural heart disease: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Medicine
- **PMID 38397868**: *Ventricular Tachycardia Catheter Ablation: Retrospective Analysis and Prospective Outlooks-A Comprehensive Review.* (2024) [review] - Biomedicines
- **PMID 38087147**: *Technological advances in ventricular tachycardia catheter ablation: the relentless quest for novel solutions to old problems.* (2024) [review] - Journal of interventional cardiac electrophysiology : an international journal of arrhythmias and pacing
- **PMID 42771085**: *Preemptive mechanical circulatory support in ventricular tachycardia ablation: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of interventional cardiac electrophysiology : an international journal of arrhythmias and pacing
- **PMID 42150018**: *Mechanical Circulatory Support During Ventricular Tachycardia Ablation: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Procedural and Clinical Outcomes.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of cardiovascular electrophysiology

## [1845] Vericiguat in worsening heart failure

**Cluster**: vericiguat in worsening heart failure | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
In heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF), endothelial dysfunction, chronic inflammation, and oxidative stress impair endogenous nitric oxide (NO) bioavailability, downregulating soluble guanylate cyclase (sGC) and cyclic GMP synthesis. This deficiency accelerates myocardial stiffness, cardiomyocyte hypertrophy, interstitial fibrosis, and vascular remodeling. Vericiguat is an oral, direct stimulator of soluble guanylate cyclase that directly enhances cGMP production independently of endogenous NO and sensitizes sGC to available NO, reducing adverse remodeling.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark VICTORIA trial established that in high-risk patients with worsening HFrEF (LVEF <45%, recent HF hospitalization or requirement for IV diuretics, and elevated natriuretic peptides), vericiguat added to baseline GDMT significantly reduced the composite of cardiovascular death or HF hospitalization (35.5% vs 38.5%, p = 0.02). (PMID 42110159)
- Vericiguat acts via a novel mechanism independent of the neurohormonal system, binding directly to soluble guanylate cyclase (sGC) to stimulate cGMP synthesis without causing clinically significant renal impairment or hyperkalaemia. (PMID 42281670)
- Initiation begins at an oral dose of 2.5 mg once daily with food, titrated every 2 weeks to 5 mg once daily and to the target maintenance dose of 10 mg once daily, monitored for symptomatic hypotension and anaemia. (PMID 42590021)
- Vericiguat is specifically indicated for 'worsening heart failure'—patients recently stabilized following a decompensation event—functioning as a valuable add-on agent when standard GDMT (quadruple therapy) has been established. (PMID 41778370)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical efficacy of vericiguat in heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF) in the ongoing VITALITY-HFpEF and CAPACITY trials.
- Direct comparison of vericiguat add-on therapy versus early optimization of foundational quadruple GDMT in real-world registries.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- VICTORIA trial patient population: patients with worsening HFrEF recently hospitalized or requiring outpatient IV diuretics with elevated BNP/NT-proBNP.
- Mechanism of action of vericiguat: direct stimulation of soluble guanylate cyclase (sGC) augmenting cGMP, distinct from PDE-5 inhibitors.
- Lack of electrolyte and renal hemodynamic contraindications: vericiguat does not provoke hyperkalaemia or decline in eGFR, making it uniquely safe in borderline renal disease.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male with ischemic cardiomyopathy (LVEF 28%) is hospitalized for decompensated heart failure; he is already on target doses of sacubitril-valsartan, carvedilol, dapagliflozin, and spironolactone; vericiguat 2.5 mg daily is initiated at discharge.
- A patient with HFrEF taking vericiguat undergoes dose titration from 5 mg to 10 mg daily; routine labs show stable serum potassium (4.4 mmol/L) and creatinine (105 umol/L).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 42281670**: *Efficacy and Safety of Vericiguat in Heart Failure: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Cureus

## [1846] Vestibular Schwannoma: surveillance vs radiation vs surgery, neurofibromatosis 2

**Cluster**: vestibular schwannoma surveillance vs radiation vs surgery neurofibromatosis 2 | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vestibular schwannoma (acoustic neuroma) is a benign, slow-growing neoplasm arising from the Schwann cell sheath of the superior or inferior vestibular branch of the eighth cranial nerve (CN VIII) within the internal auditory canal (IAC) and cerebellopontine angle (CPA). Sporadic tumours are typically unilateral and present in the fifth to sixth decades, whereas bilateral vestibular schwannomas are pathognomonic for Neurofibromatosis Type 2 (NF2-related schwannomatosis, caused by NF2 tumour suppressor gene mutations on chromosome 22q12). Management decisions—surveillance ('wait-and-scan'), stereotactic radiosurgery (SRS), or microsurgical resection—depend on tumour size (Koos classification), hearing status, brainstem compression, and patient age.

### Core Teaching Points
- The classic triad of symptoms includes progressive unilateral sensorineural hearing loss, asymmetrical tinnitus, and unsteadiness; high-resolution contrast-enhanced brain MRI (T1 with gadolinium through the posterior fossa and thin-slice CISS/FIESTA) is the gold standard diagnostic imaging. (PMID 42637963)
- Initial observation with serial MRI ('wait-and-scan' protocol every 6-12 months) is the preferred primary strategy for small, intracanalicular tumours (<1.5 cm) or asymptomatic lesions in older adults, as up to 50% demonstrate no measurable growth over several years. (PMID 41952108)
- Stereotactic radiosurgery (SRS, e.g., Gamma Knife, single fraction <=12-13 Gy to margin) provides excellent long-term local tumour control (>90-95%) with low facial nerve injury risk (<1%) for small-to-medium tumours (<2.5-3.0 cm) without significant brainstem compression. (PMID 42234018)
- Microsurgical resection (translabyrinthine, retrosigmoid, or middle cranial fossa approach) is indicated for large tumours (Koos Grade IV, >3 cm) causing brainstem displacement, progressive obstructive hydrocephalus, or intractable trigeminal neuropathy. (PMID 42430393)
- In Neurofibromatosis Type 2 (NF2), bilateral vestibular schwannomas cause inevitable bilateral profound deafness; bevacizumab (anti-VEGF monoclonal antibody) is an evidence-based medical therapy that halts tumour progression and preserves or improves hearing in up to 40-50% of NF2 patients. (PMID 42771071)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term risk of secondary radiation-induced malignant transformation (malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumour) following stereotactic radiosurgery in young patients and NF2 patients.
- Optimal timing of auditory brainstem implant (ABI) versus cochlear implant (CI) in NF2 patients undergoing vestibular schwannoma removal.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pathognomonic criterion for Neurofibromatosis Type 2 (NF2): bilateral vestibular schwannomas on neuroimaging.
- Comparing surgical approaches: translabyrinthine approach guarantees complete hearing sacrifice but minimizes brain retraction; retrosigmoid allows potential hearing preservation.
- Role of bevacizumab in NF2: medical management to reduce tumour volume and preserve hearing without surgery.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old male presents with gradual hearing loss in his left ear and difficulty understanding telephone speech; audiogram shows high-frequency asymmetric sensorineural loss; MRI demonstrates an 8 mm left intracanalicular mass.
- A 21-year-old woman with known NF2 presents with bilateral vestibular schwannomas (1.8 cm right, 2.2 cm left); she has rapidly deteriorating speech discrimination in her remaining functional ear; bevacizumab therapy is initiated.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42637963**: *Machine learning for functional outcome prediction after vestibular schwannoma surgery: a systematic review and diagnostic test accuracy meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of neuro-oncology
- **PMID 41952108**: *Current Evidence on Medical Therapy for Sporadic Vestibular Schwannoma: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Otology & neurotology : official publication of the American Otological Society, American Neurotology Society [and] European Academy of Otology and Neurotology
- **PMID 42234018**: *Correction to: Accuracy of vestibular schwannoma segmentation using deep learning models - a systematic review &amp; meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Neuroradiology
- **PMID 42430393**: *Transcriptomic meta-analysis identifies dysregulated pathways and potential therapeutic targets in Vestibular Schwannoma.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - PloS one

## [1847] VEXAS Syndrome

**Cluster**: vexas syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
VEXAS syndrome (Vacuoles, E1 enzyme, X-linked, Autoinflammatory, Somatic) is a severe, life-threatening adult-onset autoinflammatory and haematologic disorder described in 2020. It is caused by acquired somatic missense mutations in the UBA1 gene on the X chromosome (most commonly affecting codon 41: p.Met41Thr, p.Met41Val, p.Met41Leu) within haematopoietic stem and progenitor cells, predominantly affecting men aged >50 years. VEXAS manifests as a devastating overlap of systemic inflammation (relapsing polychondritis, chondritis of ears/nose, neutrophilic dermatoses, pulmonary infiltrates, fever, venous thromboembolism) and haematologic abnormalities (macrocytic anaemia, myelodysplastic syndrome [MDS], and characteristic bone marrow proerythroblast cytoplasmic vacuoles).

### Core Teaching Points
- VEXAS syndrome is caused by somatic mutations in the UBA1 gene (encoding the ubiquitin-activating enzyme E1), affecting haematopoietic stem cells and causing defective ubiquitination and systemic autoinflammation. (PMID 40287866)
- Clinical presentation classically mimics multiple rheumatologic diseases: relapsing polychondritis (ear/nose chondritis sparing earlobes), Sweet syndrome, polyarteritis nodosa, pulmonary infiltrates, recurrent unprovoked venous thromboembolism, and fever, refractory to standard DMARDs. (PMID 40157447)
- Haematologic hallmarks include progressive macrocytic anaemia (elevated MCV), thrombocytopenia, myelodysplastic syndrome, and pathognomonic cytoplasmic vacuoles in erythroid and myeloid precursor cells on bone marrow aspirate. (PMID 39060513)
- Definitive diagnosis requires targeted genetic sequencing (Sanger sequencing or NGS) demonstrating somatic UBA1 variants in peripheral blood or bone marrow; high-dose corticosteroids achieve initial symptom control, while hypomethylating agents (azacitidine), JAK inhibitors (ruxolitinib), and allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) offer targeted therapy. (PMID 41531276)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal patient selection, conditioning regimens, and transplant-related mortality of allogeneic haematopoietic cell transplantation in elderly VEXAS patients.
- Comparative long-term clinical response between azacitidine and JAK inhibitors (ruxolitinib, tofacitinib) in delaying progression to bone marrow failure.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Genetic basis and inheritance: VEXAS is an acquired somatic mutation in UBA1 on the X chromosome, NOT an inherited germline defect.
- Pathognomonic bone marrow biopsy finding: cytoplasmic vacuolization of myeloid and erythroid precursor cells.
- Therapeutic escalation in steroid-refractory VEXAS: utilizing azacitidine (hypomethylating agent) or JAK inhibitors (ruxolitinib), and curative potential of allogeneic stem cell transplantation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male presents with recurrent fevers, bilateral ear chondritis, pulmonary infiltrates, recurrent DVTs, and macrocytic anaemia (Hb 82 g/L, MCV 108 fL); he is steroid-dependent, flaring whenever prednisone is tapered below 25 mg daily.
- A bone marrow biopsy in an elderly male with suspected myelodysplasia reveals abundant prominent intracytoplasmic vacuoles in pronormoblasts and early myeloid cells; peripheral blood NGS confirms a UBA1 p.Met41Thr mutation.

## [1848] Viral encephalitis diagnostic and empiric acyclovir strategy

**Cluster**: viral encephalitis diagnostic and empiric acyclovir strategy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Viral encephalitis is a medical and neurological emergency characterized by acute inflammation of the brain parenchyma, manifesting as altered mental status, fever, seizures, and focal neurological deficits. Herpes simplex virus type 1 (HSV-1) is the most frequent and devastating cause of sporadic viral encephalitis in adults, carrying an untreated mortality of >70% with devastating permanent neurocognitive sequelae. Immediate empirical intravenous acyclovir must be instituted upon the earliest clinical suspicion without waiting for lumbar puncture results or neuroimaging, as treatment delay directly correlates with fatal outcomes and catastrophic permanent brain necrosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- Empirical intravenous acyclovir (10 mg/kg IV every 8 hours, adjusted for renal function) must be administered IMMEDIATELY to any patient presenting with acute encephalopathy and fever, prior to or concurrently with diagnostic lumbar puncture and brain MRI. (PMID 42781247)
- CSF analysis classically reveals lymphocytic pleocytosis (50-500 WBC/uL), elevated protein (0.6-1.5 g/L), normal glucose (CSF-to-serum ratio >0.5), and frequently elevated red blood cells reflecting necrotizing haemorrhagic encephalitis. (PMID 42170976)
- CSF HSV-1 polymerase chain reaction (PCR) is the gold standard diagnostic test (>95% sensitivity and specificity); however, testing performed within the first 24-48 hours can yield false-negative results; if clinical suspicion remains high, acyclovir must be continued and CSF PCR repeated at 3 to 7 days. (PMID 42601017)
- Brain MRI is the neuroimaging modality of choice, characteristically demonstrating hyperintensity on T2-weighted and FLAIR sequences with restricted diffusion restricted to the temporal lobes, insular cortex, and inferior frontal lobes (frequently asymmetric). (PMID 42048968)
- Intravenous acyclovir therapy for confirmed HSV encephalitis must be continued for a full 14 to 21 days; acyclovir can precipitate crystal nephropathy and acute tubular obstruction, requiring adequate pre-hydration and renal function monitoring. (PMID 41884829)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of adjunctive systemic corticosteroids (e.g., dexamethasone) alongside acyclovir in reducing cerebral edema and long-term neurocognitive impairment.
- Diagnostic yield and turnaround impact of metagenomic next-generation sequencing (mNGS) in suspected infectious encephalitis with negative standard viral PCR panels.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Emergency management priority: starting empiric IV acyclovir immediately upon presentation before neuroimaging or LP.
- Characteristic brain MRI distribution of HSV-1 encephalitis: bilateral asymmetric temporal and frontobasal cortical T2/FLAIR hyperintensity.
- Handling early negative CSF HSV PCR: recognizing false-negative results in the first 24-48 hours and continuing acyclovir with repeat LP in 3-7 days.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 46-year-old male is brought to the ED with acute confusion, fever of 38.9 C, aphasia, and a focal motor seizure; non-contrast head CT is unremarkable; the physician immediately begins IV acyclovir 10 mg/kg.
- A patient with confirmed HSV-1 encephalitis receives IV acyclovir; on day 4, serum creatinine rises from 85 to 210 umol/L; urine microscopy shows needle-shaped birefrigent acyclovir crystals.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 42170976**: *Immunity-oriented drug strategies for viral encephalitis: Acyclovir, antiepileptics and corticosteroids in neurological intensive care.* (2026) [review] - Pakistan journal of pharmaceutical sciences

## [1849] Viral encephalitis: HSV encephalitis, aciclovir, CSF PCR, MRI findings, empirical approach

**Cluster**: viral encephalitis hsv encephalitis aciclovir csf pcr mri findings empirical approach | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Herpes simplex encephalitis is a medical emergency. Intravenous aciclovir is started before confirmation; MRI and CSF HSV PCR confirm the diagnosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- Start IV aciclovir 10 mg/kg every 8 hours immediately on suspicion, without waiting for imaging or lumbar puncture results; adjust for renal function and hydrate. (PMID 42001849)
- MRI shows asymmetric temporal and inferior frontal T2/FLAIR hyperintensity with restricted diffusion and is more sensitive than CT. (PMID 22404980)
- CSF shows a lymphocytic pleocytosis with raised protein and normal or low glucose; HSV PCR is about 96% sensitive, but can be falsely negative in the first 72 hours, so repeat if suspicion persists. (PMID 22404980)
- Treat for at least 14 days (longer, up to 21 days, in some guidance or if immunocompromised) and confirm negative PCR before stopping in at-risk patients; consider autoimmune encephalitis if there is no response. (PMID 22404980)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with viral encephalitis: hsv encephalitis, aciclovir, csf pcr, mri findings, empirical approach.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Empirical aciclovir dose and timing.
- MRI distribution.
- CSF findings and PCR limits.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of viral encephalitis: hsv encephalitis, aciclovir, csf pcr, mri findings, empirical approach requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1850] Viral haemorrhagic fevers and high-consequence infectious diseases in the ICU

**Cluster**: viral haemorrhagic fevers and high consequence infectious diseases in the icu | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Viral haemorrhagic fevers (VHFs)—encompassing Filoviruses (Ebola, Marburg), Arenaviruses (Lassa fever), Bunyavirales (Crimean-Congo haemorrhagic fever), and Flaviviruses (Yellow fever, severe Dengue)—are high-consequence infectious diseases (HCIDs) capable of causing catastrophic multi-organ failure and nosocomial dissemination. Pathophysiology involves viral tropism for endothelial cells, macrophages, and dendritic cells, triggering massive pro-inflammatory cytokine release, microvascular leakage, consumptive coagulopathy, and profound hypovolemic/distributive shock. ICU management demands strict biosafety containment, aggressive volume resuscitation, organ support, and rapid deployment of targeted therapeutics (monoclonal antibodies for Ebola, intravenous ribavirin for Lassa fever).

### Core Teaching Points
- Infection prevention and control is paramount: strict biosafety Level 4 precautions or specialized High-Level Isolation Units (HLIU) with full personal protective equipment (PPE: powered air-purifying respirators [PAPRs] or fluid-resistant coveralls with double gloving, N95/FFP3 respirators, and chemical-resistant boots) with trained observers for donning/doffing. (PMID 36758568)
- Severe Ebola virus disease (EVD) typically manifests with massive gastrointestinal volume loss (severe watery diarrhoea and vomiting up to 5-10 L/day), causing profound hypovolemia, electrolyte wasting (hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia), metabolic acidosis, and prerenal AKI requiring aggressive point-of-care-guided fluid replacement. (PMID 42455865)
- Monoclonal antibody therapies—specifically Inmazeb (atoltivimab, maftivimab, and odesivimab-ebgn cocktail) and Ebanga (ansuvimab-zykw)—significantly reduce 28-day mortality in Ebola virus disease (Zaire ebolavirus) when administered early (PALM randomized trial). (PMID 42433267)
- Intravenous ribavirin is the specific antiviral therapy of choice for Lassa fever and Crimean-Congo haemorrhagic fever, demonstrating significant mortality reduction when initiated within the first 6 days of illness onset. (PMID 39452780)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of novel broad-spectrum antivirals (remdesivir, favipiravir) across diverse filovirus species (e.g., Sudan virus, Marburg virus) where specific monoclonal antibodies are ineffective.
- Safety and optimal delivery of continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) and mechanical ventilation in high-level biocontainment environments.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Specific antiviral treatments by VHF family: monoclonal antibodies (Inmazeb, Ebanga) for Ebola vs intravenous ribavirin for Lassa fever.
- Primary mechanism of death in severe Ebola virus disease: profound hypovolemic shock and electrolyte collapse from massive gastrointestinal fluid losses.
- Personal protective equipment protocol: mandatory use of trained buddy observers during doffing to eliminate self-contamination.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A healthcare aid worker returns from the Democratic Republic of Congo with high fevers, diffuse myalgias, and explosive watery diarrhea; RT-PCR confirms Zaire ebolavirus; isolation and monoclonal antibody therapy are mobilized.
- A traveller from rural Sierra Leone presents with fever, pharyngitis, facial edema, and mucosal bleeding; Lassa virus RT-PCR is positive, and intravenous ribavirin is started.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36758568**: *Standard of care for viral haemorrhagic fevers (VHFs): a systematic review of clinical management guidelines for high-priority VHFs.* (2023) [guideline] - The Lancet. Infectious diseases
- **PMID 42455865**: *Bleeding in haemorrhagic fever with renal syndrome: A systematic review characterising the loss of haemostasis in hantavirus infections.* (2026) [systematic review] - PLoS neglected tropical diseases
- **PMID 42433267**: *Coagulopathy in viral haemorrhagic fevers and beyond: molecular mechanisms and targeted interventions.* (2026) [review] - Frontiers in molecular biosciences

## [1851] Viral hepatitis A and E: faeco-oral transmission, self-limiting, vaccination, fulminant hepatitis

**Cluster**: viral hepatitis a and e faeco oral transmission self limiting vaccination fulminant hepatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Viral hepatitis A and E: faeco-oral transmission, self-limiting, vaccination, fulminant hepatitis is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Genetic and autoimmune predispositions to fulminant viral hepatitis in children. (PMID 42630887)
- Hepatitis A (HAV) and Hepatitis E (HEV genotypes 1 and 2) are transmitted primarily via the fecal-oral route through contaminated food and water, causing acute self-limiting hepatitis diagnosed by serum IgM antibodies. (PMID 30140224)
- Hepatitis E infection during pregnancy (especially in the third trimester with genotypes 1 and 2) carries a devastating risk of fulminant hepatic failure and maternal mortality approaching 20-25%. (PMID 30140224)
- In solid organ transplant recipients and immunosuppressed individuals, HEV (genotype 3) can cause chronic hepatitis and rapid progression to cirrhosis, requiring reduction in immunosuppression and treatment with oral ribavirin. (PMID 30140224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with viral hepatitis a and e: faeco-oral transmission, self-limiting, vaccination, fulminant hepatitis.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

## [1854] Viral-Induced Arthritides

**Cluster**: viral induced arthritides | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Viral-induced arthritides are acute, self-limiting or chronic inflammatory joint syndromes triggered by systemic viral infections. Underlying mechanisms include direct synovial viral invasion, immune complex deposition with complement consumption (Type III hypersensitivity), and molecular mimicry triggering autoimmunity. Classic culprits include Parvovirus B19 (symmetric small joint polyarthritis mimicking early rheumatoid arthritis in adults), Hepatitis B and C, Rubella, and arthropod-borne alphaviruses (Chikungunya, Ross River, Mayaro); distinguishing viral arthritis from emerging autoimmune disease avoids unnecessary immunosuppressive therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Parvovirus B19 causes erythema infectiosum ('fifth disease', slapped-cheek rash) in children, but in adults (especially mothers and schoolteachers) presents as acute symmetric polyarthritis of the small joints of the hands, wrists, and knees, closely mimicking rheumatoid arthritis with positive anti-B19 IgM antibodies. (PMID 42297949)
- Hepatitis B virus infection triggers a serum sickness-like prodrome prior to the onset of jaundice, characterized by urticarial rash, angioedema, symmetric polyarthritis, and hypocomplementaemia due to circulating HBsAg-anti-HBs immune complex deposition. (PMID 42248032)
- Hepatitis C virus is strongly associated with mixed cryoglobulinaemia (Type II/III), presenting with the classic Meltzer triad of purpura, arthralgias, and asthenia/fatigue, accompanied by positive rheumatoid factor and low C4 levels. (PMID 42357670)
- Chikungunya virus (transmitted by Aedes mosquitoes) causes an acute syndrome of high fever, petechial rash, and severe, debilitating, incapacitating polyarthralgias/polyarthritis that can persist chronically in over 30-50% of patients for months to years. (PMID 40224321)
- Viral arthritides are generally managed symptomatically with non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) and paracetamol; systemic corticosteroids and immunosuppressives should be strictly avoided in the acute phase as they can impair viral clearance. (PMID 38320811)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Pathophysiological mechanisms governing the transition from acute Chikungunya viremia to chronic, destructive post-viral arthropathy.
- Role of temporary low-dose methotrexate or biologics in chronic, refractory Chikungunya arthritis unresponsive to NSAIDs.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating Parvovirus B19 polyarthritis from rheumatoid arthritis: sudden onset, exposure to children, spontaneous resolution within 2-6 weeks, and presence of anti-B19 IgM.
- Recognizing Hepatitis B serum sickness-like prodrome: acute arthritis, urticaria, low complement (C3/C4) preceding hepatic transaminase elevation.
- Mixed cryoglobulinaemia associated with Hepatitis C: low C4, positive RF, purpura, and treatment directed at antiviral cure (direct-acting antivirals [DAAs]).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 32-year-old primary school teacher presents with sudden bilateral wrist and MCP swelling and morning stiffness 10 days after several students in her class had facial rashes; Parvovirus B19 IgM is positive.
- A 45-year-old male with chronic Hepatitis C presents with palpable purpura on both lower legs, arthralgias, and numbness in his feet; laboratory work reveals elevated rheumatoid factor and undetectable serum C4.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

- **PMID 42248032**: *Viral-induced autoimmune disorders: Mechanistic insights and emerging concepts.* (2026) [review] - Human immunology

## [1855] Viral respiratory infections: RSV, parainfluenza, adenovirus: clinical features, at-risk populations

**Cluster**: viral respiratory infections rsv parainfluenza adenovirus clinical features at risk populations | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Viral respiratory pathogens beyond influenza and SARS-CoV-2—prominently respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), human parainfluenza viruses (HPIV), and adenoviruses—cause immense disease burden across extremes of age and in immunocompromised hosts. RSV is the primary cause of bronchiolitis and pneumonia in infants and acute cardiopulmonary hospitalization in older adults. Parainfluenza viruses (especially HPIV-1 and HPIV-2) are the classic culprits behind acute laryngotracheobronchitis (croup), while adenovirus produces severe pharyngitis, follicular conjunctivitis, pneumonia, and life-threatening disseminated disease in stem cell transplant recipients.

### Core Teaching Points
- Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) manifests in infants as acute bronchiolitis (tachypnoea, wheezing, subcostal retractions, feeding difficulty); in older adults (>=60-65 years) and patients with COPD or congestive heart failure, RSV causes severe lower respiratory tract disease and mortality comparable to non-pandemic influenza. (PMID 41690197)
- Passive immunoprophylaxis with nirsevimab (a long-acting monoclonal antibody targeting the prefusion F protein) administered as a single intramuscular injection protects all infants entering their first RSV season; maternal prefusion F bivalent vaccine (Abrysvo) given at 32-36 weeks gestation confers passive transplacental protection. (PMID 41360478)
- Human parainfluenza virus (HPIV-1) is the leading cause of viral croup (laryngotracheobronchitis) in children aged 6 months to 3 years, characterized by a classic 'barking' seal-like cough, inspiratory stridor, and the 'steeple sign' (subglottic tracheal narrowing) on neck radiograph; treatment is oral dexamethasone (0.15-0.60 mg/kg) and nebulized epinephrine for severe distress. (PMID 42243031)
- Adenovirus infections in allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplant recipients can cause disseminated disease, necrotizing pneumonia, and acute liver failure; quantitative plasma adenovirus PCR is monitored, with intravenous cidofovir (or brincidofovir) used for preemptive or targeted therapy. (PMID 42580773)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical efficacy and renal safety profile of novel investigational oral RSV fusion inhibitors in immunocompromised bone marrow transplant patients.
- Efficacy of adoptive virus-specific T-cell (VST) immunotherapy in refractory disseminated adenovirus infections post-transplantation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- RSV preventative strategies: nirsevimab long-acting monoclonal antibody for infants vs maternal vaccination during weeks 32-36.
- Management of acute croup: single dose oral dexamethasone (0.15-0.6 mg/kg) with nebulized racemic epinephrine for resting stridor.
- Recognizing life-threatening adenovirus disease in transplant recipients: quantitative plasma PCR monitoring and cidofovir therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 4-month-old infant presents in December with rhinorrhoea, persistent wheezing, intercostal retractions, and oxygen saturation 89% on room air; viral multiplex PCR is positive for RSV.
- A 2-year-old boy presents at 2 AM with a sudden barking cough, hoarseness, and inspiratory stridor that worsens with crying; neck X-ray demonstrates subglottic narrowing ('steeple sign').

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41690197**: *Climatic factors drive global viral respiratory infections with regional heterogeneity: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Environment international
- **PMID 41360478**: *Adherence to handwashing behaviour and its impact on the incidence and death of viral respiratory infectious diseases: a systematic review, meta-analysis and modelling study.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - BMJ global health
- **PMID 42243031**: *Viral respiratory infections in children and metabolomics: a review of literature.* (2026) [review] - Paediatric respiratory reviews

## [1856] viral wheeze

**Cluster**: viral wheeze | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
viral wheeze is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Association of fermented dairy consumption with asthma and wheeze risk in children: a systematic review and meta-analysis of observational studies. (PMID 42553163)
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Viral Bronchiolitis in Children. (PMID 26735994)
- In preschool children, viral-induced wheeze is primarily triggered by common respiratory viruses (rhinovirus, RSV) without atopic sensitization; acute treatment centers on inhaled short-acting beta-2 agonists (salbutamol via metered-dose inhaler with spacer) titrated to clinical response, whereas routine oral corticosteroids are not recommended in mild-to-moderate preschool viral wheeze due to clear evidence of lack of clinical efficacy. (PMID 42553163)
- Parent-initiated intermittent high-dose inhaled corticosteroids (fluticasone 750-1000 ug/day) at the earliest onset of viral upper respiratory tract symptoms can reduce attack severity in severe preschool episodic wheezers who fail as-needed bronchodilator therapy. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with viral wheeze.

## [1857] Vitamin B12 and Folate Deficiency

**Cluster**: vitamin b12 and folate deficiency | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vitamin B12 (cobalamin) and folate (vitamin B9) are essential water-soluble vitamins critical for DNA synthesis, erythropoiesis, and neurological myelin maintenance. Both deficiencies impair pyrimidine synthesis, resulting in nuclear-cytoplasmic dyssynchrony that manifests morphologically as megaloblastic anaemia (macrocytosis, MCV >100-110 fL, and hypersegmented neutrophils on peripheral smear). The critical clinical mandate is never administering folic acid alone for megaloblastic anaemia without first excluding vitamin B12 deficiency; folate corrects the haematologic anaemia but accelerates catastrophic, irreversible neurological degeneration (subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord).

### Core Teaching Points
- Folic acid supplementation must NEVER be given as monotherapy in undiagnosed megaloblastic anaemia without ruling out vitamin B12 deficiency; folate bypasses the thymidylate synthase block to correct haematologic cytopenias while allowing irreversible neurological destruction to progress unchecked. (PMID 41750218)
- Vitamin B12 deficiency causes subacute combined degeneration (SCD) of the spinal cord, characterized by demyelination of the posterior columns (loss of vibration and proprioception, sensory ataxia) and lateral corticospinal tracts (spastic paraparesis, hyperreflexia, extensor plantar responses). (PMID 39991695)
- Biochemical differentiation between B12 and folate deficiency uses metabolic intermediates: methylmalonic acid (MMA) is elevated in B12 deficiency but NORMAL in folate deficiency; homocysteine is elevated in BOTH B12 and folate deficiencies. (PMID 40754128)
- Pernicious anaemia—an autoimmune gastritis causing intrinsic factor deficiency from destruction of gastric parietal cells—is the leading cause of B12 deficiency in adults, confirmed by positive anti-intrinsic factor antibodies (highly specific) or anti-parietal cell antibodies. (PMID 38774742)
- Parenteral vitamin B12 replacement (intramuscular hydroxycobalamin 1000 ug on alternate days until neurological symptoms stabilize, followed by maintenance every 2-3 months) is the standard for pernicious anaemia and patients with neurological involvement. (PMID 37772082)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical equivalence of high-dose oral vitamin B12 (1000-2000 ug daily via passive intestinal diffusion) versus intramuscular injections in patients with severe pernicious anaemia and neurological deficits.
- Diagnostic accuracy of serum total B12 versus holotranscobalamin (active B12) in borderline deficiency states.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic biochemical distinction: elevated methylmalonic acid (MMA) confirms B12 deficiency (normal in folate deficiency).
- Consequences of administering folic acid monotherapy to a B12-deficient patient (corrects macrocytic anaemia but precipitates/accelerates subacute combined degeneration of the cord).
- Specific antibody testing in pernicious anaemia: anti-intrinsic factor antibody (high specificity ~99%) vs anti-parietal cell antibody (high sensitivity ~90% but lower specificity).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old woman presents with progressive tingling in her fingers and toes, unsteady gait, and positive Romberg sign; CBC reveals Hb 85 g/L, MCV 114 fL, and blood smear shows hypersegmented neutrophils; serum methylmalonic acid is markedly elevated.
- A strict vegan of 6 years who takes no supplements presents with fatigue, memory lapses, and lower extremity hyperreflexia with bilateral Babinski signs.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41750218**: *Association Between Homocysteine, Vitamin B12, Folate and Migraine: An Updated Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Brain sciences
- **PMID 39991695**: *Vitamin B12, folate, and homocysteine levels in children and adolescents with obesity: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in public health
- **PMID 40754128**: *Exploring the Link of Serum Vitamin B12, Folate, and Homocysteine Concentrations in Individuals with Multiple Sclerosis: An Umbrella Meta-analysis of Case-control Studies.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Neuroscience
- **PMID 38774742**: *Association of vitamin B12, folate, and homocysteine with COVID-19 severity and mortality: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2024) [meta-analysis] - SAGE open medicine
- **PMID 37772082**: *Vitamin B12, folate, and homocysteine in metabolic syndrome: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2023) [meta-analysis] - Frontiers in endocrinology

## [1859] Vitamin D deficiency: 25-OHD thresholds, supplementation doses, osteomalacia

**Cluster**: vitamin d deficiency 25 ohd thresholds supplementation doses osteomalacia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vitamin D is essential for calcium homeostasis, bone mineralization, and neuromuscular function. Assessment utilizes serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D [25(OH)D], with treatment strategies stratified by deficiency severity to heal osteomalacia, prevent rickets, and preserve bone mineral density.

### Core Teaching Points
- Serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D [25(OH)D] is the definitive diagnostic marker for evaluating vitamin D status; deficiency is defined as <25-30 nmol/L (<10-12 ng/mL), insufficiency as 30-50 nmol/L (12-20 ng/mL), and sufficiency as >50 nmol/L (>20 ng/mL), with target levels of 75 nmol/L (30 ng/mL) recommended in osteoporosis. (PMID 42746921)
- Treatment of symptomatic deficiency or osteomalacia requires high-dose repletion: oral cholecalciferol (vitamin D3) 50,000 IU once weekly (or 300,000 IU total cumulative loading dose divided over 6 to 10 weeks), followed by lifelong daily maintenance dosing of 800 to 2,000 IU daily. (PMID 21646368)
- Severe prolonged vitamin D deficiency leads to secondary hyperparathyroidism, hypophosphatemia, elevated alkaline phosphatase, and defective osteoid mineralization, manifesting as osteomalacia in adults (diffuse bone pain, proximal muscle weakness, Waddling gait, pseudo-fractures/Looser zones) and rickets in children. (PMID 42746921)
- Cholecalciferol (vitamin D3) is preferred over ergocalciferol (vitamin D2) because it maintains serum 25(OH)D concentrations longer and has greater bioefficacy; active 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D (calcitriol) is indicated only in severe renal failure or 1-alpha-hydroxylase deficiency, not in nutritional deficiency. (PMID 21646368)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal target 25(OH)D levels for extra-skeletal health benefits (immune modulation, cardiovascular protection, cancer prevention).
- Comparative safety and hypercalcemia risk of intermittent high-dose bolus therapy (e.g., annual megadoses) versus daily low-dose maintenance supplementation in fall prevention.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic biomarker selection: measure 25-hydroxyvitamin D (calcidiol), not 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D (calcitriol), which is often normal or elevated in nutritional deficiency due to secondary hyperparathyroidism.
- Radiographic hallmark of osteomalacia: Looser zones (pseudofractures)—transverse cortical radiolucent bands perpendicular to the bone cortex, classically in the femoral neck, pubic rami, or scapula.
- Laboratory pattern in osteomalacia: low or low-normal calcium, low phosphate, elevated parathyroid hormone (PTH), and elevated alkaline phosphatase (ALP).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old housebound woman presenting with chronic widespread bone pain, difficulty standing from a chair, a serum 25(OH)D of 16 nmol/L, and elevated alkaline phosphatase.
- A 32-year-old woman with celiac disease presenting with bilateral groin pain whose pelvic radiographs demonstrate bilateral femoral neck Looser zones.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 21646368**: *Evaluation, Treatment, and Prevention of Vitamin D Deficiency: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2011) [guideline] -

## [1860] vitamin K antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative BRIDGE trial

**Cluster**: vitamin k antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative bridge trial | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
vitamin K antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative BRIDGE trial is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Direct oral anticoagulants versus vitamin K antagonists for postoperative atrial fibrillation after cardiac surgery: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials. (PMID 41634587)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Switching from Vitamin K Antagonists to Direct Oral Anticoagulants in Frail Elderly Patients with Atrial Fibrillation: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. (PMID 42783389)
- In the landmark BRIDGE randomized trial of patients with non-valvular atrial fibrillation receiving warfarin who required temporary interruption for elective procedures, bridging anticoagulation with therapeutic low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) failed to reduce arterial thromboembolism (0.4% vs 0.3%) and significantly tripled the risk of major bleeding (3.2% vs 1.3%) compared with no bridging, establishing that routine heparin bridging is unnecessary and harmful for standard-risk AF. (PMID 41634587)
- In patients with mechanical heart valves, particularly in the mitral position or with older-generation valves, therapeutic LMWH or unfractionated heparin bridging remains recommended perioperatively despite the findings of the BRIDGE trial in non-valvular AF. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with vitamin k antagonist bridging atrial fibrillation perioperative bridge trial.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles

## [1861] Vitamin K Deficiency and Liver Disease Coagulopathy

**Cluster**: vitamin k deficiency and liver disease coagulopathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Coagulopathy in chronic liver disease was historically viewed as an isolated bleeding diathesis, defined by elevated conventional clotting times (prothrombin time [PT] and international normalized ratio [INR]). Modern haematology recognizes cirrhosis as a state of 'rebalanced haemostasis': concomitant declines in liver-synthesized procoagulant factors (II, VII, IX, X, V) are offset by parallel declines in endogenous anticoagulants (protein C, protein S, antithrombin III) and elevated endothelial von Willebrand factor. Vitamin K deficiency (from malnutrition, prolonged antibiotics, or cholestasis with bile salt deficiency impairing fat-soluble vitamin absorption) must be distinguished from parenchymal hepatic synthetic failure.

### Core Teaching Points
- Liver disease coagulopathy represents a fragile 'rebalanced haemostasis' where patients have reduced procoagulants but also reduced anticoagulants (protein C, protein S, antithrombin); routine INR reflects ONLY procoagulant factor deficiencies and does NOT accurately predict clinical bleeding risk or protection against thrombosis. (PMID 42700010)
- Vitamin K is an essential cofactor for gamma-glutamyl carboxylase, which carboxymates procoagulant factors II, VII, IX, X, and anticoagulant proteins C and S; vitamin K deficiency responds to intravenous vitamin K (10 mg IV), whereas end-stage parenchymal failure does not correct. (PMID 42392054)
- Factor V activity testing differentiates vitamin K deficiency from hepatic synthetic failure: Factor V is synthesized by the liver independently of vitamin K; low Factor V indicates genuine parenchymal synthetic failure, whereas normal Factor V with prolonged PT/INR points to pure vitamin K deficiency. (PMID 39940057)
- Prophylactic correction of elevated INR using fresh frozen plasma (FFP) prior to low-risk invasive procedures (paracentesis, thoracentesis, endoscopic band ligation) is explicitly not recommended by gastroenterology guidelines, as it increases portal hypertension and circulatory overload without reducing bleeding. (PMID 40644452)
- Viscoelastometric testing (thromboelastography [TEG] or rotational thromboelastometry [ROTEM]) provides a comprehensive assessment of whole-blood clot firmness and fibrinolysis, serving as the superior method to guide blood product transfusions in actively bleeding cirrhotic patients. (PMID 40303879)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety and efficacy of 4-factor prothrombin complex concentrates (4F-PCC) versus fresh frozen plasma in acute severe bleeding in cirrhosis.
- Reliability of viscoelastometric parameters (TEG/ROTEM) in guiding pre-operative optimization prior to major hepatic resection or liver transplantation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating vitamin K deficiency from liver failure using Factor V vs Factor VII (Factor V synthesis is vitamin K-independent).
- Why INR cannot be used to predict procedural bleeding risk in cirrhotic patients (rebalanced haemostasis with concurrent protein C/S reduction).
- Inappropriateness of routine pre-procedure fresh frozen plasma (FFP) transfusions in stable cirrhosis undergoing paracentesis (increases volume and portal pressure without hemostatic benefit).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old male with biliary obstruction from choledocholithiasis presents with an INR of 2.4; after receiving 10 mg of intravenous phytomenadione (vitamin K1), his INR normalizes to 1.1 within 24 hours.
- A patient with decompensated cirrhosis (Child-Pugh C) has an INR of 2.2 and platelet count 55 x 10^9/L; the surgical resident orders 4 units of FFP before an elective paracentesis, which the attending cancels.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42700010**: *Association between vitamin D deficiency and chronic kidney disease progression: A GRADE-based dose-response systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Medicine
- **PMID 42392054**: *Vitamin D Deficiency and Severe Dengue Fever: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - The American journal of tropical medicine and hygiene
- **PMID 39940057**: *Vitamin deficiency, a neglected risk factor for post-anesthesia complications: a systematic review.* (2025) [systematic review] - European journal of medical research

## [1862] Vocal Cord Dysfunction

**Cluster**: vocal cord dysfunction | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vocal cord dysfunction (VCD)—now formally termed inducible laryngeal obstruction (ILO) or paradoxical vocal fold motion (PVFM)—is characterized by inappropriate, episodic adduction of the vocal cords during respiration, primarily during inspiration. VCD causes acute upper airway obstruction, manifesting as episodic dyspnoea, choking sensations, throat tightness, and inspiratory stridor, frequently triggered by exercise, emotional distress, cold air, or irritants (laryngopharyngeal reflux). Because VCD classically mimics asthma, patients are frequently misdiagnosed for years, receiving escalating high-dose inhaled corticosteroids, systemic steroids, and repeated emergency department visits without clinical improvement.

### Core Teaching Points
- Vocal cord dysfunction / inducible laryngeal obstruction (VCD/ILO) is defined by paradoxical adduction of the true vocal folds during inspiration, producing acute inspiratory dyspnoea, throat tightness, and stridor; symptoms are unresponsive to inhaled short-acting beta-agonists (albuterol). (PMID 37343843)
- The diagnostic gold standard is direct visualization via flexible nasolaryngoscopy during an acute symptomatic episode (or immediately following a provocative trigger, such as continuous exercise testing), documenting >=50% vocal fold adduction during inspiration with posterior chink formation. (PMID 34699093)
- Spirometry during an acute episode demonstrates a classic variable extrathoracic upper airway obstruction pattern: marked flattening or truncation of the inspiratory flow-volume loop with a normal expiratory limb. (PMID 39426423)
- First-line management centers on specialized speech-language pathology (respiratory retraining therapy, quick-release rescue breathing techniques: pursed-lip breathing, sniff-puffs, diaphragmatic breathing) and treating concurrent triggers (gastroesophageal/laryngopharyngeal reflux and post-nasal drip). (PMID 37221142)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic sensitivity and concordance of continuous laryngoscopy during exercise (CLE) testing versus post-exercise laryngoscopy.
- Role of laryngeal botulinum toxin (Botox) injections into the thyroarytenoid muscles in severe, intractable VCD refractory to behavioral speech retraining.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Flow-volume loop characteristics of VCD: truncated/flattened inspiratory loop with preserved expiratory loop (variable extrathoracic obstruction).
- Gold standard diagnostic test: direct flexible nasolaryngoscopy demonstrating paradoxical vocal fold adduction during inspiration.
- First-line therapy for VCD: speech therapy and respiratory retraining breathing maneuvers rather than bronchodilators or steroids.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 17-year-old female competitive cross-country runner experiences acute severe breathlessness and high-pitched inspiratory wheezing that develops precisely 5 minutes into maximal sprinting and resolves 10 minutes post-rest; albuterol inhalers provide no relief.
- A 42-year-old woman treated for 'refractory severe asthma' on triple-therapy inhalers and oral prednisone undergoes spirometry; the flow-volume loop shows complete flattening of the inspiratory loop with normal FEV1/FVC ratio.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 34699093**: *Treatment effectiveness for Vocal Cord Dysfunction in adults and adolescents: A systematic review.* (2022) [systematic review] - Clinical and experimental allergy : journal of the British Society for Allergy and Clinical Immunology
- **PMID 39426423**: *Gastroesophageal reflux disease, laryngopharyngeal reflux, and vocal cord dysfunction/inducible laryngeal obstruction-overlapping conditions that affect asthma.* (2024) [review] - The Journal of allergy and clinical immunology

## [1863] Von Hippel-Lindau syndrome: phaeochromocytoma, renal cell carcinoma, pancreatic NETs

**Cluster**: von hippel lindau syndrome phaeochromocytoma renal cell carcinoma pancreatic nets | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Von Hippel-Lindau (VHL) syndrome is an autosomal dominant hereditary cancer predisposition syndrome caused by germline inactivating mutations in the VHL tumour suppressor gene on chromosome 3p25.3. Loss of functional pVHL impairs ubiquitin-mediated degradation of hypoxia-inducible factor alpha (HIF-1alpha and HIF-2alpha), causing constitutive HIF accumulation and overtranscription of angiogenic and oncogenic target genes (VEGF, PDGF, erythropoietin). VHL manifests with multi-organ hypervascular neoplasms: central nervous system and retinal hemangioblastomas, clear cell renal cell carcinoma (ccRCC), pheochromocytomas/paragangliomas, and pancreatic neuroendocrine tumours (pNETs).

### Core Teaching Points
- Von Hippel-Lindau syndrome is inherited in an autosomal dominant fashion with high penetrance, caused by mutations in the VHL gene on chromosome 3p25, predisposing to hypervascular tumours driven by constitutive HIF-2alpha accumulation. (PMID 42609681)
- Surveillance for clear cell renal cell carcinoma (ccRCC) utilizes contrast-enhanced abdominal MRI/CT; to preserve long-term renal function, surgical intervention (nephron-sparing partial nephrectomy or thermal ablation) is deferred until the largest solid renal mass reaches 3 cm in diameter ('the 3 cm rule'). (PMID 42626756)
- Pheochromocytomas in VHL produce predominantly normetanephrine (noradrenaline hypersecretion) rather than metanephrine/adrenaline; cortical-sparing partial adrenalectomy is preferred over total adrenalectomy to avoid lifelong adrenal insufficiency in bilateral disease. (PMID 42707860)
- Belzutifan—a first-in-class small-molecule oral HIF-2alpha inhibitor—is FDA/EMA approved for adult patients with VHL syndrome who require therapy for associated ccRCC, CNS hemangioblastomas, or pancreatic neuroendocrine tumours not requiring immediate surgery, achieving high objective response rates (>50-60%). (PMID 42604778)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration of belzutifan therapy and safety of drug holidays in patients achieving durable objective radiographic tumor regression.
- Malignant potential and surgical intervention thresholds for VHL-associated pancreatic neuroendocrine tumours based on exon 3 mutations or size criteria (>2-3 cm).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The '3 cm rule' for renal cell carcinoma in VHL: active surveillance for tumors <3 cm to preserve renal parenchyma and defer partial nephrectomy until >=3 cm.
- Mechanism of action and indications for belzutifan: direct oral inhibitor of hypoxia-inducible factor-2alpha (HIF-2alpha) in VHL-associated neoplasms.
- Screening guidelines: annual blood pressure and fractionated plasma/urinary metanephrines starting at age 5; biennial central nervous system and abdominal MRI starting in adolescence.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old male with known VHL syndrome has multiple bilateral renal cysts and a 2.4 cm solid enhancing cortex lesion in the right kidney on surveillance MRI; his eGFR is normal; the urologist recommends continued surveillance until the mass reaches 3 cm.
- A 34-year-old female with VHL and growing multiple spinal hemangioblastomas and a 2.5 cm pancreatic neuroendocrine tumor is initiated on oral belzutifan 120 mg daily.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42609681**: *Diabetic ketoacidosis associated with pancreatic involvement in a patient with von Hippel-Lindau syndrome: a case report.* (2026) [review] - Frontiers in endocrinology
- **PMID 42626756**: *Belzutifan in Von Hippel-Lindau-associated Hemangioblastomas: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Journal of neurological surgery. Part B, Skull base
- **PMID 42707860**: *Regenerative potential of von Hippel-Lindau tumor suppressor protein.* (2026) [review] - World journal of biological chemistry
- **PMID 42604778**: *[Treatment of von Hippel-Lindau Disease and Hemangioblastoma].* (2026) [review] - No shinkei geka. Neurological surgery
- **PMID 42313274**: *Von Hippel-Lindau disease: pathophysiological and clinical advances.* (2026) [review] - Familial cancer

## [1864] Von Willebrand Disease

**Cluster**: von willebrand disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Von Willebrand Disease (VWD) is the most prevalent inherited human bleeding disorder, caused by quantitative deficiency or qualitative structural dysfunction of von Willebrand factor (VWF). VWF serves dual critical hemostatic roles: mediating platelet adhesion to subendothelial collagen at sites of vascular injury (via platelet glycoprotein Ib receptors) and serving as the carrier chaperone protein protecting circulating Factor VIII from rapid degradation. VWD is categorized into quantitative deficiencies (Type 1: partial deficiency, >75-80% of cases; Type 3: total absence) and qualitative structural defects (Type 2 variants: 2A, 2B, 2M, 2N), with treatment tailored to subtype and bleeding severity.

### Core Teaching Points
- VWD diagnostic evaluation requires a diagnostic panel: VWF antigen (VWF:Ag, quantitative level), VWF platelet-dependent activity (VWF:RCo or VWF:GPIbM / VWF:GPIbR, functional activity), Factor VIII coagulant activity (FVIII:C), and VWF multimers. (PMID 42248413)
- Type 1 VWD is characterized by proportional reduction of both VWF:Ag and VWF activity (VWF activity-to-antigen ratio >0.7); Type 2 variants exhibit qualitative defects with disproportionate functional loss (VWF activity-to-antigen ratio <0.7). (PMID 41786033)
- Desmopressin (DDAVP, 0.3 ug/kg IV or subcutaneous, or 300 ug concentrated nasal spray) stimulates rapid endogenous release of stored VWF and Factor VIII from endothelial Weibel-Palade bodies; it is the first-line treatment for minor bleeding or procedural prophylaxis in Type 1 VWD. (PMID 41902888)
- Desmopressin is strictly contraindicated in Type 2B VWD because the mutant VWF has hyper-affinity for platelet GPIb receptors, causing platelet hyper-aggregation and precipitating acute severe thrombocytopenia; desmopressin is also ineffective in Type 3 VWD (no endogenous stores). (PMID 42598104)
- Plasma-derived or recombinant VWF concentrates containing Factor VIII are the definitive treatment for Type 3 VWD, severe Type 2 variants, and Type 1 patients unresponsive to or with contraindications to DDAVP. (PMID 42241704)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic definition and clinical bleeding significance of 'low VWF' levels (30-50 IU/dL) versus definitive VWD (<30 IU/dL).
- Long-term efficacy and safety of recombinant VWF (vonicog alfa) compared to plasma-derived concentrates in major surgery and delivery.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Contraindication of desmopressin (DDAVP) in Type 2B VWD: induces paradoxical platelet clumping and severe thrombocytopenia.
- Distinguishing Type 1 (concordant ratio >0.7) from Type 2 variants (discordant activity/antigen ratio <0.7).
- Type 2N VWD mimicry: qualitative defect in Factor VIII binding domain that mimics mild-to-moderate Haemophilia A in males and females.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 22-year-old woman with severe menorrhagia and recurrent epistaxis has VWF:Ag 28%, VWF:RCo 24% (ratio 0.85), and FVIII 45%; a desmopressin trial demonstrates an excellent 3-fold rise in both VWF and FVIII.
- A 14-year-old boy diagnosed with atypical VWD receives DDAVP prior to dental extraction; 1 hour post-dose, his platelet count drops precipitously from 180 to 22 x 10^9/L, confirming Type 2B VWD.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42248413**: *Management of women with type 2B von Willebrand disease during pregnancy and postpartum: guidance from ISTH SSC subcommittees on von Willebrand factor and women's health issues in thrombosis and hemostasis.* (2026) [guideline] - Journal of thrombosis and haemostasis : JTH
- **PMID 41786033**: *Recommendation to adopt the type 1C VWD nomenclature into the classification of von Willebrand disease: communication from the ISTH Scientific and Standardisation Subcommittee on von Willebrand Factor.* (2026) [guideline] - Journal of thrombosis and haemostasis : JTH

## [1865] V/Q Mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange

**Cluster**: v q mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
V/Q Mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contemporary evidence synthesis highlights core pathophysiology, clinical staging, and multidisciplinary management: Pulmonary Embolism and Gas Exchange. (PMID 31390642)
- Ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) mismatch is the most common cause of hypoxemia in clinical medicine (e.g. COPD, asthma, pulmonary embolism, interstitial lung disease) and is characterized by an elevated alveolar-arterial (A-a) oxygen gradient that corrects with low-to-moderate supplemental oxygen (FiO2 0.3-0.5). (PMID 28459336)
- True right-to-left anatomical or physiological shunt (V/Q ratio of zero, seen in severe ARDS, dense lobar consolidation, and intracardiac shunts) presents with refractory hypoxemia that does not fully correct even with 100% inspired oxygen. (PMID 28459336)
- Dead-space ventilation (V/Q ratio approaching infinity, seen in pulmonary embolism) impairs carbon dioxide elimination, resulting in hypercapnia and increased minute ventilation requirements. (PMID 28459336)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with v/q mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for v/q mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of v/q mismatch and pulmonary gas exchange requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.
