## [1866] VTE prophylaxis in mechanically ventilated ICU patients

**Cluster**: vte prophylaxis in mechanically ventilated icu patients | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Critically ill, mechanically ventilated patients are at exceptionally high risk for venous thromboembolism (VTE) due to immobilization, vascular stasis, systemic inflammation, and invasive central venous access. Pharmacological thromboprophylaxis with low-molecular-weight heparin is the standard of care unless contraindicated by active hemorrhage.

### Core Teaching Points
- All critically ill mechanically ventilated adults require routine venous thromboembolism (VTE) prophylaxis; low-molecular-weight heparin (enoxaparin 40 mg once daily or dalteparin 5,000 units once daily) is preferred over unfractionated heparin due to superior efficacy in reducing deep vein thrombosis and a lower incidence of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT). (PMID 38849278)
- In patients with severe renal impairment (creatinine clearance <30 mL/min), unfractionated heparin (5,000 units subcutaneously twice or three times daily) or dose-adjusted enoxaparin (20-30 mg once daily with anti-Xa monitoring) is recommended to avoid bioaccumulation and major bleeding. (PMID 40142619)
- Mechanical thromboprophylaxis with intermittent pneumatic compression (IPC) devices is recommended in patients with active bleeding, severe thrombocytopenia (platelets <50 x 10^9/L), or high bleeding risk until pharmacological anticoagulation can be safely introduced. (PMID 37663232)
- Routine surveillance venous Doppler ultrasonography is not recommended in asymptomatic ICU patients; however, sudden unexplained hypoxemia, tachycardia, right ventricular strain, or acute difficulty weaning from mechanical ventilation mandates urgent evaluation for pulmonary embolism. (PMID 38849278)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Benefit of weight-adjusted or anti-Xa-guided LMWH dosing versus standard fixed dosing in morbidly obese critically ill patients.
- Role of extended post-ICU pharmacological thromboprophylaxis in patients with persistent severe mobility impairment.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- LMWH versus UFH in critical care: LMWH significantly reduces DVT and pulmonary embolism risk with lower rates of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT).
- Management of active coagulopathy: mechanical prophylaxis (IPC) alone until active hemorrhage resolves, followed by prompt resumption of LMWH.
- Renal adjustment rules: enoxaparin accumulates in renal failure; switch to UFH or reduce dose to 30 mg daily with peak anti-Xa level monitoring (target 0.2-0.5 IU/mL).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old mechanically ventilated man with severe acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) and septic shock receiving continuous sedation and paralysis.
- A 45-year-old polytrauma patient with cerebral contusions, intracranial pressure monitoring, and pelvic fractures evaluated for thromboprophylaxis timing.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 40142619**: *Safety and Efficacy of Direct Oral Anticoagulants Apixaban and Rivaroxaban Versus Standard Therapy for VTE Prophylaxis Post Cancer Surgery-A Network Meta-Analysis of Randomized Clinical Trials.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Journal of clinical medicine

## [1867] VTE prophylaxis in nephrotic syndrome

**Cluster**: vte prophylaxis in nephrotic syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Nephrotic syndrome—defined by heavy proteinuria (>3.5 g/24h), hypoalbuminaemia (<30 g/L), peripheral oedema, and hypercholesterolaemia—is a potent prothrombotic state. The hypercoagulable state is driven by urinary loss of natural anticoagulant proteins (antithrombin III, protein S), enhanced hepatic synthesis of procoagulant factors (fibrinogen, factor V, factor VIII), thrombocytosis, platelet hyper-reactivity, and hemoconcentration. Venous thromboembolism (VTE) occurs in up to 10-40% of adult nephrotic patients—most prominently renal vein thrombosis (RVT), deep vein thrombosis, and pulmonary embolism; membranous nephropathy carries the highest thrombotic risk, warranting individualized prophylactic anticoagulation.

### Core Teaching Points
- The risk of venous thromboembolism in nephrotic syndrome is inversely proportional to serum albumin; thrombosis risk rises steeply when serum albumin falls below 20-25 g/L (2.0-2.5 g/dL), particularly in membranous nephropathy. (PMID 38849278)
- Membranous nephropathy (MN) carries the highest incidence of VTE and renal vein thrombosis among all causes of nephrotic syndrome; KDIGO guidelines recommend prophylactic anticoagulation in MN patients with serum albumin <20-25 g/L and additional thrombotic risk factors. (PMID 40142619)
- Renal vein thrombosis (RVT) presents either insidiously or acutely with severe flank pain, gross haematuria, rapid decline in renal function, and sudden worsening of proteinuria; Doppler ultrasound or contrast CT venography provides definitive diagnosis. (PMID 37663232)
- Warfarin or direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs: apixaban, rivaroxaban) are effective for VTE prophylaxis; however, low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) and unfractionated heparin rely on antithrombin III, which is significantly depleted in severe nephrotic proteinuria, potentially requiring higher doses or direct factor Xa inhibitors. (PMID 41949461)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety and efficacy of direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs: apixaban, rivaroxaban) compared to warfarin for primary VTE prophylaxis in nephrotic patients with severe hypoalbuminaemia.
- Exact bleeding versus thrombotic risk score cutoffs to guide prophylactic anticoagulation duration during immunosuppressive induction.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Identifying serum albumin thresholds (<20-25 g/L) and histological subtype (membranous nephropathy) that trigger consideration of prophylactic anticoagulation.
- Clinical presentation of acute renal vein thrombosis: sudden flank pain, macroscopic haematuria, and acute renal decline.
- Pharmacologic considerations: why heparin efficacy may be blunted by urinary antithrombin III loss, favoring DOACs or adjusted LMWH.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 46-year-old male with newly diagnosed PLA2R-positive membranous nephropathy has 24-hour urine protein of 9.2 g and serum albumin of 18 g/L; the nephrologist calculates bleeding and thrombotic risk to decide on prophylactic anticoagulation.
- A 38-year-old female with nephrotic syndrome develops sudden left loin pain and gross haematuria; contrast CT reveals a non-occlusive filling defect in the left renal vein extending into the inferior vena cava.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 40142619**: *Safety and Efficacy of Direct Oral Anticoagulants Apixaban and Rivaroxaban Versus Standard Therapy for VTE Prophylaxis Post Cancer Surgery-A Network Meta-Analysis of Randomized Clinical Trials.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - Journal of clinical medicine

## [1868] Vulvar and Vaginal Cancers

**Cluster**: vulvar and vaginal cancers | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vulvar and vaginal cancers are rare gynaecological malignancies predominantly comprising squamous cell carcinomas (SCC, >80-90%). Pathogenesis follows dual distinct etiologic pathways: human papillomavirus (HPV)-dependent pathways (associated with high-risk HPV types 16 and 18, high-grade squamous intraepithelial lesions [HSIL / vVIN], younger patients, and smoking) and HPV-independent pathways (associated with chronic inflammatory lichen sclerosus, differentiated VIN [dVIN], TP53 mutations, and older age). Treatment centers on individualised radical wide local excision with sentinel lymph node biopsy (SLNB) or inguinofemoral lymphadenectomy, followed by adjuvant chemoradiotherapy for positive nodal metastasis.

### Core Teaching Points
- Vulvar squamous cell carcinoma arises via two distinct carcinogenic pathways: HPV-associated (younger women, p16 positive, better prognosis) and HPV-independent (older women, associated with lichen sclerosus, TP53 mutated, higher recurrence rates). (PMID 42580601)
- Inguinofemoral lymph node status is the single most critical prognostic determinant for survival; sentinel lymph node biopsy (SLNB) using radiotracer and blue dye or indocyanine green is the standard of care for unifocal tumours <4 cm without clinically suspicious groin nodes (GROINSS-V trials). (PMID 42449705)
- Surgical management of early vulvar cancer requires radical local excision achieving >=1 cm gross surgical margins (histological clearance >=8 mm after tissue fixation) to minimize local recurrence risk while avoiding disfiguring radical vulvectomy. (PMID 41606506)
- Primary vaginal cancer is rare (most vaginal malignancies represent metastases from cervix, vulva, or endometrium); definitive concurrent chemoradiotherapy (external beam radiation plus brachytherapy with cisplatin) is the standard of care for locally advanced (Stage II-IV) primary vaginal carcinoma. (PMID 41535899)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety of omitting full inguinofemoral lymphadenectomy in patients with isolated micrometastases (<=2 mm) detected on sentinel lymph node biopsy (GROINSS-V II trial).
- Efficacy of immune checkpoint inhibitors (pembrolizumab) in recurrent or metastatic HPV-associated versus HPV-independent vulvar carcinoma.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Prognostic importance of inguinofemoral lymphadenectomy / SLNB in vulvar cancer staging and management.
- Dual pathways of vulvar carcinogenesis: p16/HPV-associated vs p53/lichen sclerosus-associated.
- Primary treatment modality for locally advanced vaginal cancer: definitive chemoradiotherapy with image-guided brachytherapy rather than radical exenterative surgery.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old woman with a 15-year history of vulvar lichen sclerosus presents with an unhealing, painful 2.2 cm ulcerated lesion on the right labium majus; biopsy confirms invasive keratinizing SCC.
- A 38-year-old female presents with a unifocal 1.8 cm vulvar SCC on the left labium; clinical exam and groin ultrasound show no lymphadenopathy; the gynaecologic oncologist plans radical local excision with sentinel lymph node biopsy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

- **PMID 42449705**: *HER2 Expression in Squamous Cell Carcinoma of the Vulva: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Cancers

## [1869] Walled-Off Pancreatic Necrosis

**Cluster**: walled off pancreatic necrosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Walled-Off Pancreatic Necrosis (WON) is a mature, encapsulated collection of solid pancreatic and/or peripancreatic necrotic tissue enclosed by a thick inflammatory wall, typically developing >=4 weeks after the onset of acute necrotizing pancreatitis. Differentiating sterile from infected necrosis is the crucial clinical junction: infected necrosis—confirmed by new fevers, leukocytosis, sepsis, or presence of gas bubbles on contrast-enhanced CT—is a life-threatening indication for intervention. The international standard of care has definitively shifted from open surgical necrosectomy to the minimally invasive 'step-up approach' (conservative medical therapy and antibiotics, followed by endoscopic or percutaneous catheter drainage, and endoscopic transmural necrosectomy only when needed).

### Core Teaching Points
- Walled-off pancreatic necrosis (WON) is distinguished from pancreatic pseudocyst by containing solid necrotic debris on contrast-enhanced CT or endoscopic ultrasound (pseudocysts contain pure non-necrotic amylase-rich fluid); draining WON requires wide-caliber access to evacuate solid necrosis. (PMID 42675307)
- The minimally invasive step-up approach (PANTER trial) is the standard of care for infected necrotizing pancreatitis, reducing new-onset organ failure, long-term complications (pancreatic insufficiency, incisional hernias), and healthcare costs compared to primary open surgical necrosectomy. (PMID 42414778)
- Endoscopic step-up approach utilizing endoscopic ultrasound (EUS)-guided transmural drainage with lumen-apposing metal stents (LAMS, e.g., Axios) followed by direct endoscopic necrosectomy (DEN) as needed demonstrates equivalent mortality with significantly lower fistula rates and hospital stay compared to surgical step-up drainage (TENSION trial). (PMID 41786153)
- Intervention should be delayed whenever clinically feasible until >=4 weeks from pancreatitis onset, allowing the necrotic collection to organize and form a mature fibrous encapsulation ('walling-off'), which dramatically reduces procedural bleeding and perforation risks. (PMID 40693902)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing for removing lumen-apposing metal stents (LAMS) (typically 3-4 weeks) to prevent delayed pseudoaneurysm bleeding from stent erosion into adjacent splenic or gastroduodenal vessels.
- Role of upfront direct endoscopic necrosectomy (DEN) at the index drainage procedure versus a true staged step-up drainage-first protocol (DESTIN trial).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- PANTER and TENSION trial outcomes: superiority of the minimally invasive endoscopic step-up approach over open necrosectomy.
- Timing of intervention: intentionally delaying intervention to >=4 weeks post-onset to allow mature fibrous wall formation.
- Differentiating pancreatic pseudocyst from walled-off necrosis on imaging (solid necrotic tissue vs homogeneous fluid).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old male 5 weeks post-severe acute gallstone pancreatitis develops spiking fevers and leukocytosis; CT abdomen reveals a 12 cm retroperitoneal encapsulated collection containing mixed fluid, non-enhancing solid debris, and multiple gas pockets (infected WON).
- An interventional endoscopist performs EUS-guided cystogastrostomy using a 20 mm lumen-apposing metal stent (LAMS) into a mature retroperitoneal WON collection, evacuating 800 mL of purulent necrotic debris.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42675307**: *SAGES clinical practice guidelines for the management of symptomatic walled-off pancreatic necrosis.* (2026) [guideline] - Surgical endoscopy
- **PMID 42414778**: *SAGES systematic review and meta-analysis for the management of symptomatic walled-off pancreatic necrosis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Surgical endoscopy
- **PMID 41786153**: *Upfront endoscopic necrosectomy versus step-up endoscopic approach for walled-off pancreatic necrosis: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2026) [meta-analysis] - Gastrointestinal endoscopy

## [1870] Wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ECG patterns

**Cluster**: wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ecg patterns | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Wellens syndrome is a pain-free ECG pattern of proximal LAD stenosis; diffuse ST depression with ST elevation in aVR suggests left main or multivessel disease. Both need early angiography.

### Core Teaching Points
- Wellens syndrome shows biphasic (type A, about 25%) or deeply inverted symmetric (type B, about 75%) T waves in V2 to V3 during pain-free periods, with no significant ST elevation or Q waves. (PMID 42001870)
- It indicates critical proximal LAD stenosis with high risk of anterior infarction; urgent angiography is needed and exercise stress testing is contraindicated. (PMID 42001870)
- Diffuse ST depression in 6 or more leads with ST elevation in aVR (and sometimes V1) suggests left main or severe multivessel disease and calls for emergency cardiology review and angiography. (PMID 30140224)
- In those patients management follows the NSTE-ACS pathway with antithrombotic therapy, but immediate angiography is warranted when haemodynamically unstable. (PMID 30140224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ecg patterns.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Wellens types and leads.
- Why stress testing is avoided.
- Left main equivalent pattern.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of wellens syndrome and left main equivalent ecg patterns requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1871] wheeze phenotypes

**Cluster**: wheeze phenotypes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
wheeze phenotypes is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Genetic characterization of preschool wheeze phenotypes. (PMID 40769318)
- Meta-analysis pooling randomized controlled trials demonstrates significant outcome improvements and lower complication rates: Association of fermented dairy consumption with asthma and wheeze risk in children: a systematic review and meta-analysis of observational studies. (PMID 42553163)
- Childhood wheezing is categorized into distinct phenotypes: transient early wheeze (associated with smaller airway caliber and maternal smoking, resolving by age 3-6), non-atopic viral wheeze (triggered by viral infections, generally improving in school age), and persistent atopic wheeze/asthma (characterized by atopic sensitization, allergic rhinitis/eczema, elevated FeNO, and persistent airway hyperresponsiveness into adulthood). (PMID 40769318)
- Preschool children with the persistent atopic wheeze phenotype demonstrate significant airway eosinophilia and derive marked clinical benefit and reduced exacerbation rates from daily maintenance low-dose inhaled corticosteroids. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with wheeze phenotypes.

## [1873] Wolfram syndrome DIDMOAD: diabetes insipidus, DM, optic atrophy, deafness

**Cluster**: wolfram syndrome didmoad diabetes insipidus dm optic atrophy deafness | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Wolfram syndrome is an autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disorder from WFS1 mutations, presenting with diabetes mellitus and optic atrophy, later diabetes insipidus, deafness, and neurological and urinary problems. Care is supportive.

### Core Teaching Points
- Wolfram syndrome (DIDMOAD) is usually caused by recessive WFS1 mutations; childhood-onset non-autoimmune insulin-requiring diabetes is typically first, followed by optic atrophy, central diabetes insipidus and sensorineural deafness. (PMID 42001873)
- Later features include neurogenic bladder with hydronephrosis, ataxia, central hypoventilation and cognitive or psychiatric problems, often in the second to third decade. (PMID 42001873)
- Management is supportive: insulin, desmopressin, hearing aids or cochlear implant, visual aids, and bladder care; there is no disease-modifying treatment. (PMID 31037637)
- Surveillance covers ophthalmology, audiometry, urodynamics and renal ultrasound, plus neurological follow-up. (PMID 31037637)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with wolfram syndrome didmoad: diabetes insipidus, dm, optic atrophy, deafness.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Gene and inheritance.
- Order of appearance of the features.
- Supportive management.

## [1874] workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction

**Cluster**: workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction is a crucial clinical entity with well-defined evidence-based standards of care. Recent clinical guidelines, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses provide clear diagnostic algorithms, validated risk stratification, and targeted first-line therapies that optimize patient recovery while mitigating preventable complications.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical practice guideline consensus establishes evidence-directed diagnostic criteria and risk-stratified therapy for workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction: Evidence-Based Clinical Practice and Multi-Society Consensus on workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction. (PMID 42001874)
- Comprehensive diagnostic evaluation of newly diagnosed heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF) requires comprehensive transthoracic echocardiography, laboratory evaluation (NT-proBNP, complete blood count, renal and liver function, thyroid stimulating hormone, ferritin and transferrin saturation to detect iron deficiency), and non-invasive or invasive coronary angiography to exclude ischemic heart disease, alongside cardiac MRI when non-ischemic cardiomyopathy or myocarditis is suspected. (PMID 42001874)
- Iron deficiency (ferritin <100 ug/L, or 100-299 ug/L with TSAT <20%) occurs in up to 50% of HFrEF patients; intravenous ferric carboxymaltose or ferric derisomaltose improves symptoms, quality of life, and reduces heart failure hospitalizations. (PMID 34447992)
- Guideline-directed medical therapy with the four foundational pillar drug classes (ARNI/ACEi, evidence-based beta-blocker, MRA, and SGLT2 inhibitor) should be initiated and uptitrated promptly to reduce cardiovascular mortality. (PMID 34447992)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration, dosing adjustments, and sequencing of therapy in high-risk or multimorbid patients presenting with workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.
- Long-term comparative effectiveness and recurrence prevention across evolving clinical practice guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic workup, biomarker/imaging selection, and risk stratification for workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.
- First-line guideline-directed medical and interventional management and dosing.
- Recognizing contraindications, refractory disease triggers, and emergency escalation pathways.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult patient presenting with acute or subacute manifestations of workup for new heart failure with reduced ejection fraction requiring immediate diagnostic confirmation and therapy.
- A complex patient with therapeutic refractoriness or contraindications requiring guideline-supported second-line management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.

## [1875] Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome

**Cluster**: zollinger ellison syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome (ZES) is a clinical syndrome caused by severe, unregulated hypersecretion of gastric acid driven by autonomous gastrin release from a neuroendocrine tumour (gastrinoma). Gastrinomas are located primarily within the 'gastrinoma triangle' (bounded by the confluence of the cystic/common bile duct, junction of the 2nd/3rd duodenal parts, and neck/body of the pancreas), occurring sporadically (75-80%) or as part of Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia Type 1 (MEN1, 20-25%). Clinical hallmarks include severe, recurrent, refractory peptic ulcer disease (often in atypical locations like the distal duodenum or jejunum), severe reflux oesophagitis, and chronic watery secretory diarrhoea that resolves with gastric acid suppression.

### Core Teaching Points
- Zollinger-Ellison syndrome should be suspected in patients with multiple peptic ulcers, ulcers in atypical post-bulbar or jejunal locations, ulcers refractory to standard therapy, ulcers associated with severe chronic diarrhea, or personal/family history of MEN1 endocrinopathies. (PMID 41585576)
- Initial diagnostic screening requires measuring fasting serum gastrin (FSG); an FSG >1000 pg/mL (>10-fold ULN) in the presence of gastric acid hypersecretion (gastric pH <=2.0) is virtually pathognomonic for ZES. (PMID 41463062)
- Prior to measuring fasting serum gastrin, proton pump inhibitors (PPIs) must be temporarily discontinued for at least 1-2 weeks (with bridging via H2-receptor antagonists, held 24-48 hours before testing), as PPI-induced hypochlorhydria causes secondary physiological hypergastrinaemia that generates false-positive results. (PMID 38299632)
- If fasting serum gastrin is non-diagnostic (between 100 and 1000 pg/mL with pH <=2), a secretin stimulation test is the confirmatory test of choice; a paradoxical rise in serum gastrin of >120 pg/mL above baseline confirms ZES. (PMID 37042078)
- Primary tumor localization is established with 68Ga-DOTATATE PET/CT (somatostatin receptor imaging), which possesses superior sensitivity (>90%) over conventional CT/MRI and octreotide scintigraphy; high-dose PPI therapy (e.g., omeprazole 60-120 mg daily) is the medical treatment of choice to control acid hypersecretion. (PMID 34629807)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Surgical resection versus conservative medical surveillance in patients with MEN1-associated multifocal duodenal microgastrinomas.
- Role of peptide receptor radionuclide therapy (PRRT with 177Lu-DOTATATE) in metastatic, unresectable gastrinomas progressing on somatostatin analogues.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Secretin stimulation test interpretation: paradoxical rise in serum gastrin (>120 pg/mL) confirming gastrinoma.
- Diagnostic protocol: why PPIs must be held for 1-2 weeks before measuring fasting gastrin and mandatory measurement of gastric pH to exclude achlorhydria.
- Preferred functional imaging modality for gastrinoma localization: 68Ga-DOTATATE PET/CT.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old male with recurrent duodenal bulb and jejunal ulcers despite twice-daily pantoprazole presents with chronic watery diarrhoea; fasting serum gastrin is 1,450 pg/mL with a gastric pH of 1.4.
- A patient with suspected ZES has a borderline fasting gastrin of 380 pg/mL; intravenous secretin (2 U/kg) is administered, resulting in a serum gastrin surge to 820 pg/mL at 15 minutes.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm alignment with current local antimicrobial/formulary guidelines and relevant special population considerations.
